JOHN GOULD . I lost a good many things—these knives are mine.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner lodged with you? A. Yes—I gave her notice—she could not pay her rent, and I put in a distress—I examined her box before the broker—I charged her with stealing my daughter's bonnet—the inspector dismissed that charge—I charged her with stealing three towels, and the Magistrate dismissed that.
NOT GUILTY .

I am a grocer, and live in Green-street, Leicester-squar About a quarter before four o'clock on the 8th of April, the prioner came into my shop and took this bag of coffee from the box it laid on—he ran out with it—I ran, and overtook him—I never lost sight of him—I saw him throw the coffee into an area—this is my coffee.
GEORGE ANDERSON ( police-constable C 85.) I took the prisoner, and got the coffee out of an area where the prisoner had thrown it.
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming out of Green-court, and heard the cry of "Stop thief"—a young man had just passed me—I was following him, and three gentlemen stopped me—it was nearly a minute and a half before this man came up—I know no more about it.

[trialAccount t18400406-1249]
1249. JAMES HAZELL and JOHN GASPAR were indicted for stealing, on the 11th of March, 1 jacket, value 25 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 20 s.; 1 waistcoat, value 10 s.; 1 hat, value 2 s. 6 d.; 1 handkerchief, value 1 s.; 1 pair of boots, value 7 s.; 3 shillings, and 1 sixpence; the property of Thomas Bell.
THOMAS BELL . I am a sailor, belonging to the Queen Victoria, of Aberdeen, On the 11th of March I came to London—about ten or eleven o'clock I met some person whom I do not know, they called him Jack —he introduced me to a lodging-house where he was himself—the two prisoners were there—they told me to go to bed, and I should be safe in the morning—I did so, and about an hour after Gaspar came and looked over me in the bed—Hazell was behind him, and took my boots—I did not halloo out to them, as I thought it was no harm—I had put my things under my head, that they might be safe in the morning, as Gaspar told me—I had tied the door, as Gaspar told me, but they had cut the string—I got up in the morning, and all my things were gone, and the prisoners too—I do not know who took any thing but the boots.
Gaspar. Q. When I met you did I stop you and take you to a place? A. Yes—we went to one public-house, and then to another—I had 5 s. or 6 s.—I had 3 s. 6 d. when I went to bed—I paid you 1 s. for my supper, and gave you a share of two pots of beer—all that was left in the morning was my keys—a servant of the house took me to bed—you forbid me to give my things to the mistress, but told me to put them under my head, and they would be safe—you came up without a light—I saw you open the door—it was light enough to see you—there was a sky-light above where I lay—it was moon-light—I saw you take the boots—I did not speak to you, because you pretended to be a friend.
MART ANN SIEX . I lodge at this house. I remember Gaspar came home with the prosecutor—Hazell was in the house at the time—I heard them talk together most of the evening, they said it would be a pity for any one to meddle with his clothes, and they would take care no one should—they drank together—the prosecutor wanted some supper, and the prisoners fetched a loaf and some sausages—the prosecutor gave them the money—about two o'clock Hazell came into my room, and took off his shoes, and left them by my bed-side—I do not know whether he put on the boots—he went to Sal's bed-side, and said to her, "Get up, we are going to nail the sailor"—she said she would have nothing to do with it—about six o'clock in the morning the prosecutor came into my room, and asked me where the man was that brought him there—I said he slept in the next room, and the prisoners were gone, they had not come to bed at all that night.
Gaspar. Q. How long was I in the house? A. Five or six weeks—the prosecutor was only there one night.
EMMA BROWN . I lodge at this house. I was sleeping in the adjoining bed to Gaspar—he went to his wife, and told her he was going to rob the sailor—he went out again, and came in again, and said the sailor should
only have 2 s.—he undressed himself, and went out again, he came in again, and said he should dress himself in the sailor's clothes, and should be in the coffee-shop till the morning.
Gaspar. Q. Do you mean to say you saw me come into the room? A. Yes—I went to bed about half-an-hour before the sailor—you said to your wife, "I am going to rob the sailor," and she began to cry, and said, "His but and shoes are under the bed"—I cannot tell what you did with your clothes—your wife went to bed after me—I do not know whether it was morning—I saw you go out of the room without your clothes.
Gaspar's Defence. I had lodged there seven or eight weeks; Hazell had only been there one night—I met the prosecutor, who asked where he could get a lodging. I took him to where I lodged; we had our supper, and I said to him, "If you will take my advice, put your clothes under your head, as we always do;" he was taken to bed by the servant-girl, who has run away since; there was no light in the room, and he could not see me; he never said one word when he saw this man take his boots; Brown is not to be believed—she says she heard me say I was going to rob the sailor, and why did she not tell the man by the side of her to go and tell the sailor? She said she would get me transported.
Hazell's Defence. I know nothing about it; I wished to go to bed before this prisoner and the sailor came home, but they said they had no candlestick; my shoes were under the bed before the sailor came home.
HAZELL— GUILTY . Aged 19.
GASPAR— GUILTY . Aged 29.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18400406-1250]
1250. MARY FARRELL was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of March, 1 1/2 yard of printed cotton, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of George Clarke; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
GEORGE CLARKE . I keep a linen-draper's shop in Whitecross-street, St. Luke's. On the Friday after the 16th of March Hayward called to know if I had lost any thing—I had seen the prisoner in my shop on the 16th—I did not miss 'any thing till Hayward came—I saw this printed cotton safe in my shop on Monday morning, the 16th—I had not sold it—I have missed it—it is mine, and has my private mark on it—it was a remnant, and could not be sold without asking me, as the price was not marked.
HARRIET HATWARD . I am a searcher at the station-house—I was sent for to search the prisoner on Monday evening, the 16th of March—she said she had not a farthing's worth about her, but in her basket I found this print.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought it for 1 s. 4 d. in his shop; I have a witness to prove it.

inside my shop, and saw the prisoner at the door, looking at some furniture on a board inside the shop—I had suspicion, and looked round a pile of carpet, saw her take a piece, put it under her cloak, and walk off with it—I stopped her at the corner of Union-place, opened her cloak, and saw it in her hand—I brought her back, and she dropped it in the doorway.

1252. JOHN WILLIAMS was indicted for breaking and entering the shop of Henry Isaac Mayes, at St. Matthew, Bethnal-green, and stealing therein 20 pieces of woollen cloth, value 2 s., his goods.
HENRY ISAAC MAYES . I am a tailor, and live in Portugal-place, Cambridge-road, St. Matthew, Bethnal-green. I was walking past my shop about nine o'clock, on the 6th of April—I saw the door broken, one man inside the shop, and the other out—the prisoner was one—I said, "Halloo!"—they said, "It is all right"—I said, "I don't think it is all right"—I sung out, "Police," and "Stop thief"—the two men made a rush to get out—there is a sort of wicket-gate, and the other man's hat fell off, and in it was a number of these pieces of woollen cloth he got from me—I ran after him to Northampton-street, and lost sight of him for five or six minutes—I then went back to the shop, knowing I had left it just as the men ran out—then I found the hat outside the gateway, and also a bundle, containing the remainder of the pieces, wrapped up in a silk handkerchief—the prisoner was one of the two men I saw there—I have no doubt, I am sure of him—I cannot say whether he was outside or inside the door—I had left the door safely shut up.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you not at first express some doubt about his being the man? A. When he was brought to me, he turned and said, "Then you swear I am the person"—I said, "Stop till we get to the shop, because I should not like to say I know you are the man till I am positive"—it was about nine o'clock at night—the pieces of cloth are worth 2 s.—nothing was found on him.
HENRY LEWIS TYTLAR . I am a tailor, and live in Whitechapel-road. I was in Cambridge-road on that evening—I saw the prisoner running up Northampton-street to Essex-street, and then he got over a garden-wall—he said, "Let me be, I will not run"—I had made no charge against him then—he did not say it to any one in particular, but there were several people standing round—he then walked a few yards, and began to run—the had left policeman who came up pursued and took him.
Cross-examined. Q. There were a number of people collected? A. Yes—this garden is about ten feet long—he had just come over the garden-wall when he said, "Let me alone"—I was coming along the road, and heard the cry of "Stop thief"—there might be twenty people.
DANIEL HEGARTY ( police-constable K 201.) I heard the cry of "Stop thief," and came up with this man, in possession of two men—he was pointed out as the person who attempted to commit the robbery—I took him to the prosecutor, who gave him in charge.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner denied it? A. Yes.
GUILTY .* Aged 30.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18400406-1253]
1253. JOHN CARROLL and JAMES ROACH were indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of April, 2 pump-boxes, value 10 s., the goods of Thomas Heath and another.
JOHN ROSS . I am chief mate of the ship Malcolm, belonging to Thomas Heath. These pump-boxes, belonging to the ship, were in the carpenter's store-room, and on the 2nd of April I had them moved, about the middle of the day—they were loose—on the morning of the 3rd I was called to the station-house, and missed them out of the ship—I believe them to belong to Thomas Heath and another, as far as the ship is concerned.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Who is the other partner? A. Captain James Froome; and I believe there are other partners besides him, but I do not know who they are.
EDWARD POYNTON . I am night watchman. I stopped the prisoners with the property—I do not know who owns the ship.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18400406-1254]
1254. HENRY HORNER, alias Cooper, was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of March, 12 pain of socks, value 11 s., the goods of William Boome.
GEORGE LAWS . I am a shopman to William Broome, a hosier, in Oxford-street. About eight o'clock in the evening, of the 30th of March I saw these socks safe just inside the door—I saw them unusually working up and down—I ran to the door, and saw the prisoner with another lad run off—I followed, and saw the prisoner throw these socks up Marylebone-lane—I followed, and took him—they are called half-hose in the trade—some people call them socks.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Where were you? A. Behind the counter serving a customer—I was three yards in the shop—I had to come five or six yards round to the door—I gave an alarm to a young man; and went round myself—a person outside might see me in the shop—it was about eight o'clock at night—I saw the prisoner and another lad running from the door—I saw the string was cut—I found they were gone before I ran out—I never said I ran out, and found the parcel gone—I could not miss them till I got to the door—the prisoner was about three yards from the door when I went out—I turned to see the parcel was cut, and they ran off directly—before I came out I saw the parcel was missing—the persons I saw were to the left—I pursued the prisoner, and he threw them from his hand up Marylebone-lane, which is three or four yards from the shop—I saw them while I was standing at the door—when I got out they were walking—I followed the prisoner to Welbeck-street, about a hundred yards—I did not lose sight of him—I was not more than two or three yards behind him—I slipped for a minute, or I should have caught him long before.
GUILTY .* Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.

1256. JOHN CROFT and MARY CROFT were indicted for stealing, on the 27th of March, 1 copper-lid, value 2 s., the goods, of James Nicholson.
JAMES NICHOLSON . I keep the sutling-house at St. James's-palace. On the 27th of March the prisoners came to my house together, about four o'clock in the afternoon—the man asked for half-a-pint of beer—they went into the tap-room, and sat together—I had seen this copper-lid safe that day between eleven and twelve o'clock—I had occasion to send the boy out while they were in the tap-room, and while he was away the man came to the bar, and called for half-a-pint of beer—while I was serving, the woman passed me, and I saw the copper-lid projecting from under her cloak—the man stepped inside the tap-room, and then he followed her.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were there any people in the tap-room? A. Not that tap-room—there was in another—I have not found the copper-lid.
COURT. Q. Why did you not stop her? A. It is a usual thing for mechanics to come and eat their dinners at my house, and I thought it might be her property—all I know is I saw something like a copper-lid under her cloak.
HENRY GALLIMORE . On the 27th of March I left the copper-lid safe a little after four o'clock—I came back in about five minutes, and then the party and the lid were gone.
Cross-examined. Q. Were there oyster people and other people in the tap-room? A. Yes—there were no soldiers in the other tap-room.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18400406-1258]
1258. WILLIAM DEMMETT was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of March, 10 loaves of bread, value 6 s. 6 d., the goods of Richard Hooke, his master.
RICHARD HOOKE . I am a baker, and live in Shoe-lane—the prisoner was my servant—on the 13th of March I sent him, among other places, to Mrs. Norie, in Bear-alley, with fifteen loaves—he brought word back that they wanted ten more, and in the afternoon I sent him with ten more—he came to the counter and had them booked—here is the book in which they are entered—on the Monday morning he was to take their bill—on the Monday I gave the bill—shortly after breakfast Mrs. Norie sent to my shop and said they had not got the bill—I questioned the prisoner, and he said he supposed he had left it at another customer's—I made out another, and in the course of the day they came and said there were ten loaves too many—the first opportunity I had, I questioned the prisoner about the loaves, and he said, "I suppose I forgot to book them"—I said, "Very well, go in the evening and set it right"—he went out and did not return.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Had he to take loaves out to other customers? A. Not at that particular time—I am quite sure he said Mrs. Norie wanted ten more loaves—I am sure he did not merely say that he wanted ten.
ANN SAVAGH . I am servant to Mrs. Norie, who lives in Bear-alley—I received fifteen loaves, and they were put down in the book, which I have here—the prisoner brought word in the morning that Mr. Hooke could not spare any more.
Cross-examined. Q. What ire you? A. Mrs. Norie's sister—I had these loaves to sell—we keep no servant—MRS. Norie sometimes takes in the bread herself—the man that brings it makes the entry in the book—I always take care that he enters it—I am always in the shop, and was there the whole day.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18400406-1259]

JOHN BERRY . I am a builder living in Chapel-street, Stockwell—I left my coat in my chaise on the 9th of April just on one side of the Mansion-house—I was away about half-an-hour, and when I came back it was gone—( produced)—this is it.

1260. MARY GRAY was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of April, 1 plate, value 1 s., the goods of Robert Gordon and others.
MAJOR JOHN ABBOTT . I am a private in the first battalion of Grenadier Guards—I was on duty in Whitehall-gardens, and saw the prisoner come from the Reform Club-house—I saw the print of a plate through her apron—I had seen it previous to that on the sand bin in the yard—I went to the yard, and the plate was gone—I returned and took her with it—she said it was an old broken plate she had picked up—it was not broken then, it is now.
MATTHEW SAUNDERS . I am servant to the Reform Club-house—this
plate is the property of Robert Gordon and others—Robert Gordon is secretary to the Lords of the Treasury.
HENRY BENTLEY ( police-constable A 193.) I took the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I went and picked up bones, and this plate was among some sand.
NOT GUILTY .

WILLIAM SPARROWE . I keep an auction-room, and live in Smith's-row, New-road—I had a time-piece on the 8th of April—this is it—it had been with me several days for sale—I had seen it about a quarter of an hour before it was missed.
ELIZABETH SAXON . I saw this time-piece at Mr. Sparrowe's on the 8th of April—I saw the prisoner take it up and put it under the left flap of his coat, and go out—I made an alarm—he was brought back with it.
GEORGE DUDNEY . I was at the auction-room on this evening—I heard Saxon call, "That man has stolen a time-piece"—I went, and saw him throw it into a piece of land—I took it up, and gave him into custody.
Prisoner's Defence. I came out with the rush of people, I did not take it.

1262. JOSEPH HANNEY was indicted for embezzlement.
CHARLES WYMAN . I keep a public-house in Curzon-street, May-fair. The prisoner was my postman—it was his duty to receive money paid on my account—if he received 1 s. 3 d. on the 22nd of March, he has not paid it to me, which he ought to have done that day.
MARY FOSBURY . I live in Spring-square, and deal with the prosecutor. The prisoner brought me a pint of beer, and half-a-pint of rum on the 22nd of March—I paid him 1 s. 3 d. for his master.
Prisoner. I own taking the money, but it only came to 1 s. 2 d.; the whole money was 5 s. 1 d.—I used to account to his wife twice a day, and I accounted to her for this.

1263. JOHN WILSON RIDOUT was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of March, 1 cream-ewer, value 5 l.; 1 bed-tick, value 3 s.; 1 set of bed furniture, value 2 s.; 1 sofa-cover, value 2 s.; 2 castors, value 1 s.; and 6 bolts, value 1 s.; the goods of Abraham Soloman, his master.
ABRAHAM SOLOMAN . I am a furniture-broker, and live in Little Queen-street. The prisoner was a French-polisher, and was at my house on the 31st of March—he left that evening, and did not return the next morning as he ought—I lost the cream-ewer on the 31st, and the other property prior to that time—I had used the ewer that day at breakfast—this is it—it has a crest on one side, and initials on the other—( looking at it.)
JOHN DAVIS . I live with Mr. Wells in Queen-street. About ten o'clock that night, the prisoner came to the George public-house—I saw him with the milk jug—I heard Mr. Soloman had lost one, and gave Information.
RICHARD PEARCE . I am potman at the George. The prisoner came on the evening of the 31st of March, into the tap-room, sat down, and threw down some bed-furniture, and in about ten minutes after, he said, "I have
got something at the next house, which I want to get away, I got it from a sale"—he went and got a milk-ewer like this.
Prisoner's Defence. I could not return, as the officer took me, and the bed-tick does not belong to Mr. Soloman.

1264. JANE LACY was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of March, 4 sovereigns, 2 half-sovereigns, 10 shillings, 1 watch-guard, value 2 s.; and 1 pair of boots, value 7 s.; the property of John Swanson.
JOHN SWANSON . I am a seaman, and lodge in Gravel-lane. On the 25th of March, I went to Bluegate-fields with the prisoner—I had four sovereigns, two half-sovereigns, ten or twelve shillings in silver, and a silver watch-guard—I took off my clothes, put them on a chair, and went to bed—I sent the prisoner for some gin and porter, which she brought—I sent her for a light—she said she had no money—I told her to put her hand into my pocket, to find some coppers among the silver, which she did, she went out, and did not return—I got out of bed, overhauled my things, and found all this property gone, and my shoes—no one else had been in the room—nobody else could have taken them.
GEORGE WILLIAM GRAVES ( police-sergeant K 11.) I went to Tothillfields Bridewell, and saw the prisoner, I told her she was charged with stealing 5 l. 10 s. and a pair of shoes, from the prosecutor—she said he must be a very false man, she had only taken 3 l. 10 s., and knew nothing about the shoes.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Six Months.

1266. JOHN DUDLEY, FREDERICK HOEFLER , and THOMAS TAYLOR were indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 3 loaves of bread, value 7 d., the goods of George Beland; and that Hoefler and Taylor had been before convicted of felony.
MARTHA BELAND . I am the wife of George Beland, and live in Charlotte-street, Whitechapel. About half-past one o'clock, on the 6th of April, I was in my parlour—I heard a rustling at the window, Bowden called me—I went out, and missed three loaves—I ran down Nottinghamplace, and saw the three prisoners running—Dudley delivered some loaves to the other two—he was then secured—he said he had done nothing—I said, "Come back, I will see what you have done"—Hoefler and Taylor got off at that time.
ANDREW BOWDEN . I live in Duke-street. I was in Charlotte-street, near the prosecutor's window—I saw Hoefler open the window, and each
of the prisoners take a loaf—I went and told—MRS. Beland overtook them—they hid the bread—I am sure I saw them each take a loaf.
ROBERT MIDDLETON ( police-constable K 216.) I took the prisoners—I do not know what became of the bread.
Hoefler's Defence. I was in Whitechapel about ten o'clock that evening, and they took me.
ROBERT CHAMBERS . I produce a certificate of Taylor's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read —I know Taylor—he is the person.
EDWALD WELSH ( police-constable H 180.) I produce a certificate of Hoefler's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—Hoefler is the person.

[trialAccount t18400406-1267]
1267. LOUISA PUDDICK and HESTER CURRY were indicted for stealing, on the 10th of April, 154 yards of lace, value 3 l. 17 s., the goods of Thomas Hendy.
THOMAS HENDY . I am a laceman, and live in Hereford-place, Commercial-road. On the 10th of April, about half-past eleven o'clock, the two prisoners came to my shop and asked my daughter-in-law to serve them—I saw the thread lace box on the counter before them—they asked my daughter to go to the other end of the shop for something else, and while she was gone, Puddick took five cards of thread lace off the counter and gave them to Curry, who concealed them under her right arm, under her shawl—I saw them—I was in the parlour at the back of the shop—I came up and fixed my eye on Curry—I saw she had the lace under her right arm as she was sitting on the seat—seeing she was discovered, she burst into tears, took the lace from under her arm, and put it on the counter.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What were you doing? A. I was about to shave, but had scarcely commenced—I was looking into the shop through a glass window—I could see distinctly what took place on the counter.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Were you about to put the lather on your face? A. Yes—I dare say I was—I was not looking in the looking-glass at the moment I saw the property stolen—my daughter Elisabeth was serving them—Cooper, my shop woman, was in the parlour—there is a looking-glass in the parlour, by which you can see what is going on in the shop—it was not that glass at which I was shaving, but another.
SARAH COOPER . I am shop woman to Mr. Hendy. I was in the parlour and saw the prisoners looking at some lace—MR. Hendy desired me to come out—I saw Curry take the lace from under her right arm, and put it on the counter—that was about five minutes after I came into the shop.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What was your master about? A. Going to shave—he was looking into the shop—I was sitting in the parlour opposite the door—I did not see Puddick take the lace—she had her back to me—I was about a yard from Curry behind the counter, when I saw her take the lace from her shawl under her right arm—I saw it distinctly—she was in front of it.
ELIZABETH BURRELL . The prisoners came to the shop and asked to look at some thread lace—they then asked for some stomachers, and while my back was turned to get them, one of them took the lace out of the box—it was there when I went, and it was gone when I came back—there was 154 yards of it—MR. Hendy came into the shop soon after, and stood behind them watching them—they bought three-quarters of a yard of lace at half-a-crown a yard, and paid for it—they had not bought any before—I saw the lace produced and thrown upon the counter.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Had you not placed these five cards on the counter for them to look at? A. Yes, and missed them on my return—it was impossible to avoid it—they were looking at some others—it is not unusual for one person to hand lace to another, who turns to the light to look at it—I have stated that my father was shaving and looking into a glass at the fire-place which reflected from the shop.
COURT. Q. But you were not in the room where he was? A. No—there were five cards of lace on the counter, but the box was by the side of it—the pieces that were taken were taken from the box—the five remained on the counter.
THOMAS HENDY re-examined. The box contained from fifty to sixty cards—they were selecting the cards of lace—my eyes were never off them—Puddick had selected them, and she took them from the counter and gave them to the other.
(Puddick received a good character.)
PUDDICK— GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Six Months.
CURRY*— GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18400406-1268]
1268. JOSEPH WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 purse, value 1 s.; 1 shilling, and 1 penny, the property of William Christie, from the person of Louisa Christie.
LOUISA CHRISTIE . I am the wife of William Christie, who lives in New North-street, Red Lion-square. I was in St. James's-street, Pall Mall, on the evening of the 9th of April—there was a crowd there—I felt something—I turned and said to the prisoner, who was in a blue coat, "You have robbed me"—he had his left hand in my pocket—he took it out and put it in his own pocket, and made off—I put my hand in and missed my handkerchief and purse from it.
Prisoner. I had not a blue coat on—it was the coat I have on now.
Witness. I am sure you are the man that took it—you ran a few yards, and was brought back by the police.
SMUEL WRIGHT ( police-constable P 172.) I watched the prisoner for ten minutes behind the prosecutrix—I saw him put his hand in her pocket, and draw out this handkerchief—he ran about ten yards—he took this handkerchief from his pocket and threw it down—I threw him on some steps, and took him.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Three Months.

1269. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 5 s., the goods of Thomas Cooper, from his person; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
THOMAS COOPER . I live in Chapel-street, Pentonville. On the evening
of the 8th of April I was in Little Britain—I received information, turned, and saw the prisoner, and accused him of having stolen my handkerchief—he denied it—he was searched in my presence, and this handkerchief found on him—it is mine, and was safe a quarter of an hour before.

WELCOME COLE . I was walking opposite the prosecutor. I saw the prisoner and another walking behind the prosecutor—I saw the prisoner raise the tail of his coat, and take the handkerchief—I crossed, and told the prosecutor—I hastened after the prisoner, and took the handkerchief from under his waistcoat.

1270. CATHERINE COHEN was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 30th of March, of an evil-disposed person, 3 forty-franc pieces, value 4 l. 18 s.; 19 twenty-franc pieces, value 15 l. 10 s. 4 d.; and 12 imperials, value 9 l. 16 s.; the monies of Christian Bager; well knowing the same to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
ELIZABETH MORGAN . I am the wife of James Morgan, and keep a beer-shop in Duke's-court, Bow-street. On Monday or Tuesday last the prisoner came to my house with a girl, and called for a pint of ale—they stood and drank it, and offered my daughter a sovereign, as I thought, and asked if I could give change—I said "No," and I told my daughter to go and get change—she went and came back, and said it was a French sovereign—I said I should like to see one—I took it, and gave it back to the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you know a person of the name of Thwaite? A. Yes, two or three—one Samuel Thwaite lives in Lascelles-place, and there is Peter Thwaite, who lives with his father, in Market-court, Russell-street—I have known him a good many years, not as an acquaintance, but as a neighbour's child—he is no relation of mine—my daughter married a brother of his—I have been told that he lived with the prisoner—I have not known it—I have not heard him make a statement about this prosecution—he did not offer to make a bet about the result of it, or tell me he wanted to get rid of her—I never heard from him how long he had lived with her, nor that he promised to marry her—I heard of his having thrown himself into the Thames—I saw him last about ten days ago—I never heard him lay a bottle of wine about this—I never saw the prisoner but once before she came to my house for this pint of beer—she was at my daughter's wedding.
CHRISTIAN BAGER ( through an interpreter.) I went to a house—I had three forty-franc, nine twenty-franc pieces, and twelve imperials—I saw the prisoner and two more in the house—the prisoner left the room—my money was safe after she left—I afterwards made a complaint of being robbed—the
prisoner came in again—she pointed out ray money to me—I got fourteen pieces out of the thirty-seven—there were two girls on the bed.
NOT GUILTY .

1272. SUSAN HARRIS, WILLIAM JONES , and WILLIAM HARRIGAN were indicted for stealing, on the 8th of April, 6lbs. weight of bacon, value 4 s., the goods of Henry Coe.
HENRY COE . I am a cheesemonger, and live in Fitzroy-square. On the 8th of April, about half-past eight o'clock, these two pieces of bacon were taken from my shop.
GEORGE ANTHONY . I saw the three prisoners in Marylebone-street standing at a cheesemonger's door, about ten yards from yards from Mr. Coe's—they then walked on to Coe's shop—Jones looked in several times, then walked in, and brought something out—Harris stood opposite, and then he went in—I missed Harris, and then I saw Jones give something to Harrigan—they walked on, and Jones whistled—Harris crossed, and tied up the bacon in a handkerchief—I got an officer, who took them.
Jones. He says he saw me go into the shop; it is false. Witness. I have every reason to believe that the boys are the dupes of the female prisoner—I have seen her about.
Harris's Defence. I did not know the boys before; they gave me the bacon, and asked me to carry it home for them.
HARRIS — GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Three Months.
JONES — GUILTY . Aged 15.
HARRIGAN — GUILTY . Aged 14.
Confined Nine Days and Whipped.

1274. JOHN MONTEITH and ANN MONTEITH were indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 24th of March, of certain evil-disposed persons, 31bs. weight of mutton, value 1 s., the goods of Thomas Seabrook, well knowing the same to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
THOMAS SEABROOK . I am a butcher, and live in Harrow-road, Paddington. On the 24th of March a breast of mutton was stolen from me, about half-past twelve o'clock in the day—I was informed of it by a neighbour—I went out, and saw some boys—I followed them—I then went to the prisoners' room with Ridley, and asked if they had not got a boy there concealed with some meat—they said they had not—I said I was convinced that they had—they said if he was there he must be in the area—I went, and there was no one there—I looked about the room, and found the boy Young in the turn-up bedstead and this meat—John Monteith was sitting by the fire smoking his pipe, and did not say any thing.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did both the prisoners speak to you? A. No, only the woman—I have heard that they are husband and wife.
JOHN YOUNG . I am fourteen years old, and live with my father in Little Carlisle-street—he is a policeman. I know the boy Ridley who stole the meat—I was with him and two others—Ridley took it from the shop, and gave it to me—I was two yards off when he stole it—the prisoners live in Devonshire-street—I took the breast of mutton there—I had not seen the prisoners before, but the other two boys told Ridley to take it there, and I took it—I found the two prisoners there—I set it down, and asked if they would buy it—they said they would—I said I wanted 10 d. for it—they said they would give me 8 d.—I said I would take 9 d., I would not take less—they said they would not give me more than 8 d., as they could buy it at a shop for 1 s.—the male prisoner was putting his hand into his pocket to pull out the money to pay me 8 d., when Ridley and the prosecutor came in—(the female prisoner had dealt with me, but the man was going to pay me)—I saw the policeman have hold of Ridley, and I said to the prisoners, "Here is the butcher, where shall I get to?" and the woman told me to get into the bed—I went into the bed directly, which was turned up.
Cross-examined. Q. You have been tried for this, have you not? A. Yes, and convicted—I was fined 1 s., and discharged.
THOMAS SAVORY ( police-constable D 58.) I took the prisoners in charge.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you made any inquiry about them? A. I have heard that the man is a watchman, and bears a very good character.
JOHN MONTEITH— GUILTY. Aged 73.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Six Months.
ANN MONTEITH— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18400406-1275]
1275. THOMAS ROBERTSON was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of February, 4 boxes, value 30 s.; 1 bag, value 6 d.; 1 drawer, value 2 s. 6 d.; 30 tablets, value 9 l.; 60 paper-knives, value 6 l.; 20 fans, value 5 s.; 2 knives, value 1 s.; and 2 forks, value 1 s.; the goods of Joseph Smith, his master: and SAMUEL WILKINSON , for feloniously receiving the same well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute.
&c.; to which Robertson pleaded
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Seven Years.
JOSEPH SMITH . The prisoner Robertson was my errand-boy for four or five weeks—I know nothing of Wilkinson, but part of my property has been discovered, and is here now—what is produced is mine.
GEORGE MONSON . I am a sergeant in the 14th Light Dragoons. On the 29th of February I met the prisoner Wilkinson in the Canteen at Hounslow-barracks—I had never seen him before—I purchased this small paper-knife of him—( looking at it)—he asked me 1 s. for it—he had a great number of other things for sale as a sort of pedlar.
JOHN PRICE . I am a servant in the 14th Light Dragoons. I was at Hounslow-barracks, and saw Wilkinson there on the afternoon of the 28th of February—I had seen him before, two years ago last November, when he was in the Blues—he came from the same "town that I do—he stopped with me from the afternoon of the 28th till the 29th, and when he was going away he made my children a present of a small knife and fork each—these are them—as he was going away he dropped this box, and left that with me.
MARY TURNER . I live at Hounslow-barracks. I saw Wilkinson there on the 29th of February—I had never seen him before—he came to the mess kitchen with a number of things—I bought these three work-boxes of him for 10 s.—he told me he was a manufacturer of them.
ROBERT MYERS . I am a retailer of beer, and a grocer, and live at Turnham-green. Wilkinson came to my house, and had a pint of beer—he had a number of goods, which he exposed on the table for sale—I said they were very splendid, and I was sorry to see such a sacrifice—I bought nothing of him but this bag ( producing it.)
JOHN WILLIAM JONES . I am a pawnbroker, and live at New Brentford. I have three ivory tablets, pawned on the 28th of February by Wilkinson, in the name of John Williams—he said he was the maker and seller of the goods, and he had token nothing all day—I lent 2 s. 6 d. on them.
JOHN GILBERT . I am a shoemaker, and live at West-place, Turnham-green. I saw Wilkinson in a beer-shop—he offered me this drawer ( producing it), and asked if I would give him a pot of beer for it, which I did.
HENRY JOHN BEAUMONT . I am a pawnbroker, and live in Edgeware-road. I took in two tablets of Wilkinson on the 28th of February.
JOHN HUGHES . I am a pawnbroker. I produce one tablet, which I
took in pledge of Wilkinson on the 28th of February in the name of John Williams.
Wilkinson's Defence. I came out of the country on the 26th of February And on the 27th I was at a public-house with some friends about ten o'clock; I met with the prisoner Robertson there; I heard them talking about a raffle for an accordion, they put my name down for one; I saw Robertson again that night, he raid he made fancy goods and toys, and we might get a living by selling them, that he could get plenty of goods; I said I would sell them; the next morning, at ten minutes before eight o'clock, he came to my lodgings, and said he had got some things; I went and pawned some of them, then went down the road, and sold the rest.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Transported for Fourteen Years.

1276. THOMAS MAGEN was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 4 s. 6 d., the goods of Robert William Elliott, from his person.
ROBERT WILIAM ELLIOTT . I am an accountant in the Excise-office. On the 9th of April, between four and five o'clock, I was in Shoreditch with two ladies—we stopped at a jeweller's shop window to look at some jewellery—I had a handkerchief in my pocket, and I had scarcely got to the Window when I saw the ruffling of my handkerchief over my left shoulder—I turned, and saw the prisoner with my handkerchief in his hand close behind me—I pursued him—he carried it some distance, and then threw it down—the officer took it up—I pursued, and he was stopped—this is my handkerchief—( looking at it.)
Prisoner. I was not behind him at all—I never saw him till I was taken. Witness. I never lost sight of him.
MILES DOWLING ( police-constable H 135.) On Thursday last I saw the prisoner take this handkerchief in his hand, probably from the prosecutor—he ran down Bateman's-row—I pursued him till he threw it down—I took it up, and followed till he was taken.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going to look for work; some person stopped me, and somebody said, "That is the boy that took the handkerchief;" I had never seen the gentleman.
GUILTY .* Aged 18.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18400406-1277]
1277. JOHN GROUT was indicted for a misdemeanor.
WILLIAM SQUIRE . I am a manufacturer of manifold-writers. Mr. Thomas Varty, a stationer, in the Strand, deals with me—I had a recollection of seeing the prisoner as having come from Mr. Varty—on the 13th of March he came and had two of our writers to show to a gentleman in Piccadilly, which I delivered to him—he came again in the afternoon for another, but I had in the meantime received information, and secured him—he told me he had come from Mr. Varty, and on that credit I gave him the two Writers, thinking Mr. Varty had sent for them.
THOMAS VARTY . I am a bookseller and stationer. I deal with Mr. Squire—the prisoner lived a little better than three years ago in our service—I did not send him, on the 13th of March, for these writers—he had no authority from me to get them.
JAMES COLLINS . I am a pawnbroker. I took in one manifold-writer of the prisoner on the 13th of March—this is it.
GEORGE FARMER . I am a pawnbroker. I took in this other writer of the prisoner on the 13th of March.
Prisoner's Defence. I was out of a situation; I obtained them, being in distress, for the purpose of paying my rent; I thought I should be able to return them, as I expected some money the next Saturday morning.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Twelve Months.

1280. JOHN HARRISON was indicted for a misdemeanor.
JONATHAN PEARSON . I am an ironmonger, in partnership with Thomas and John Pearson. I deal with Mr. Griffiths—on the 26th of March the prisoner came and said he wanted one of the best bottle-jacks—I got him one to look at—he asked the price, and said it was for Mr. Griffiths, of No. 2, Burton-crescent—he said he would have that one, and asked to see a lamp which was in the window—he said it was just the sort of article Mr. Griffiths had been speaking about—I wished to send it home, but he said he would take them, and said, "That is an excellent guard you had put up in Mr. Griffiths's winery"—I let him have the articles, believing he came from Mr. Griffiths—I had some recollection of having seen him before.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. What pas the value of the bottle-jack? A. 14 s., and the lamp 7 s. 6 d.
THOMAS PEARSON . On the 26th of March, after the prisoner had been with my brother, he came and said he wanted the lamp he had just looked at—I said, "Which do you mean?"—he pointed to one on the counter, and said it was for Mr. Griffiths—I said, "You had better let us send it"—he said, "No, you had better let me take it," and I gave it to him—between half-past four and five o'clock he came again for a dozen table knives and forks, he said Mr. Griffiths had a party that day, and they were all in a bustle—I asked what sort be would have—he said what Mr. Griffiths had were balance ones—he selected a dozen at 36 s., and took them away with him—in two hours suspicions were raised, and I went to see Mr. Griffiths—I parted with the goods, believing he came from Mr. Griffiths—he also said to me, "That was an excellent guard you put up a twelve-month ago."
GEORGE STOKEHOUSE GRIFFITHS . I am a solicitor. I deal with Pearson and Co.—I did not authorise the prisoner to go for these articles—I knew nothing about it—I never received the goods—he did not live
with me—I have known him some years—he is a connexion of mine by marriage, and knew I dealt with Mr. Pearson—he bore a very excellent character till lately.
WILLIAM RIDEWOOD . I am a broker and curiosity-dealer, in Cleveland-street. I bought this bottle-jack, and lamp, on the 26th, of the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. What did you give for them? A. 5 s. for the two—I had seen the prisoner two or three times before—he had sold me trifling things on each occasion—I think the time before that he sold me an old box, and some old newspapers—I gave him 1 s. or 1 s. 6 d. for the box—he sold me an old shot-belt and powder-flask for 1 s. together—I do not recollect what I bought of him besides—I do not keep an account of what I buy—he was dressed as he is now—he brought me the bottle-jack about two o'clock, and the lamp at the same time—I know these knives were offered to me, between five and six o'clock—I said I did not want them, and offered 5 s. for them.
JOHN DOWLING . I am a clothes-salesman, in Cleveland-street. I bought the knives of the prisoner, for 1 s. they were quite new.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you seen him before? A. Yes, several times—I bought some trowsers of him, and other things.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Baron Alderson.
[trialAccount t18400406-1281]
1281. RICHARD GOULD, alias Arthur Nicholson, was indicted for the wilful murder of John Templeman.
MESSRS. JONES and BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH THORNTON . I am single, and live with my mother, in a cottage in Pocock's-fields, Islington—MR. Templeman lived in a cottage facing us. On Tuesday morning, the 17th of March, I went to Mr. Templeman's cottage, at half-past eight o'clock, with two sheets of writing-paper—I knocked at the door, but received no answer—MR. Templeman used to sleep in the further room from a cottage inhabited by Mrs. Jarvis, and the further room from the door—the door opens into one room, and then you go into the other—there is a window to the bed-room—when I received no answer I went to the bed-room window, and found it shut down as usual—there was a short curtain to it inside—I was too short to see over the curtain—I then went to the sitting-room window, and found it open—I then returned home, and told my mother what I had observed.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. How far is your cottage from Mr. Templeman's? A. It may be about a yard, as far as I can call to mind—it faces it—our door is not opposite his door—it is about as far from it as from me to the bench of this Court—the window which was open looks towards our cottage—there is no yard before it—it is all one piece of ground—there are palings all round our house, and Mr. Templeman's too—our bed-room window faces towards Mr. Templeman's—I recollect that there was a pane of glass broken for some time in Mr. Templeman's window, and there was a piece of paper over it, when my mother used to go backwards and forwards, but there was not that morning, it had been thrust through—there are three cottages within about the same distance of Mr. Templeman's as ours—there are four cottages standing together, Jarvis's, ours, Mr. Templeman's, and Mrs. Downes's—MR. Mustow's cottage is at the back—that makes five—it was a beautiful moon-light night the night before I went—I think it was frosty—the ground was dry by the window where I was standing—it
was more like clay than gravel—I think it was soft—I did not look to see if there were any foot-marks—I went home directly, and told my mother.
MARY THORNTON . I am the mother of the last witness—she resides with me in a cottage in Pocock's-fields. On Tuesday morning, the 17th of March, I sent her to Mr. Templeman's cottage with some writing-paper—she returned, and made a communication to me—I then went to Mr. Templeman's cottage, and knocked at the door—no answer was returned—I then went to the bed-room window—it was quite closed—there was a curtain and drawn-blind to it—I looked over the curtain, and saw the bed—there was no one in it—I saw Mr. Templeman on the floor, about a yard from the bed, lying with his feet towards the fire-place, and his head towards the parlour door—I then went round to the sitting-room window, and found it was open—I had known Mr. Templeman nearly four years—three years and a half full he had been in the neighbourhood—for about two months I had been in the habit of washing and charing for him, fetching his errands, and such like—the parlour window was always closed previous to this morning—I never saw it open—it used to be fastened with a little button just below where it was broken—it had been broken a long time—it was quite a small hole, nearly close to the button—there was no difference in the hole that morning, except that the paper was gone, and, I believe, a bit of glass—it had been fastened with a bit of glass, and paper on it—when the paper was gone any body could put their hand in and open it—the window is quite big enough for a man to get in when it is open—I had been to the house the evening before, at a quarter before six o'clock, and left about five minutes before six o'clock—he usually went to bed about six o'clock—he never was up afterwards, unless any body was there—he did not have any beer on Monday evening, to my knowledge—I have known the prisoner about four or five months—he had been pot-man at the Barnsbury Castle public-house—I have seen him serve Mr. Templeman with beer—I know the Jarvises—they live nearly close to Mr. Templeman's—it is one of the four cottages—I have heard that the prisoner was on visiting terms at Jarvis's, and I have seen him go in repeatedly—I think it must be nearly a fortnight before this affair that I saw him there—I did not see any thing of Mrs. Jarvis on the Monday evening—I saw her in the fore-part of the day at her own door, or else in the ground—she had her child with her.
Q. Did you see any money that day at Mr. Templeman's cottage? A. Yes, he showed me some, and told me had been receiving some rents—it was silver, and was in a little mahogany box—I had never seen that box before—he put it on the table where he took it from to show me the money—the table stood in the middle of the room—he had never shown me any money before.
Cross-examined. Q. You have said to-day you have seen the prisoner serve Mr. Templeman with beer; is that quite correct? A. Quite—I never said I thought I had—I might have said to, but I can say that I did see him, and that will settle it—I might have said I thought, although I was certain—the prisoner used to come round to serve the neighbours with beer—it was seldom I had any beer, but when I did he served me—there are four cottages besides Mr. Templeman's—my son-in-law and daughter live with me—I do not take lodgers occasionally—we have no room for ourselves hardly—Mr. and Mrs. Downes live in their cottage—she does not take lodgers that I know—how should I know other people's
affairs?—I have not seen a strange man going in and out of Downes's cottage, to my knowledge, unless it is acquaintances going, who are strangers to me—I do not know what sort of persons they are—I never trouble my head about it—I saw people go in there—I do not trouble my head with my neighbours' concerns—I have not seen any strangers going in and out of Mrs. Palmer's, or Mrs. Mustow's—they might have visitors—I never take notice of other people's affairs—I might have seen people going in and out—I do not trouble my head about it.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You were examined at the Police-office and before the Coroner? A. Yes—I never mentioned having seen the prisoner at Mr. Templeman's to any body.
FRANCIS CIPRANI . I am a night-watchman at Sadler's Wells theatre, and live with my mother-in-law, Mrs. Thornton. I used to work for Mr. Templeman, digging in his garden—on Monday evening, the 16th of March, I received 7 s. from him for my labour—it was six shillings and two six-pences—I received it about a quarter or twenty minutes before six o'clock, to the best of my recollection—I know a man named Jarvis, and I have seen his wife—I saw her, about half-past four o'clock that day, come away from her cottage, and go to Mr. Templeman's cottage, and stand just by the door talking to him, to the best of my recollection, for five or ten minutes—I did not see what became of her then—I was busy at work—I missed her from the door—I did not take notice which way she went.
Cross-examined. Q. You were first taken up on this charge, is that true?— A. Yes—on Tuesday, between twelve and one o'clock, I was discharged next morning before the Magistrate—no evidence was given against me—the Magistrate said he discharged me without a stain on my character—I accounted for how I passed my time—I brought forward the call-boy at our theatre, to prove that I let him out at half-past three o'clock—I rent the cottage myself, but keep my mother to look after it—I do not know that Mr. Templeman kept any tools in his house—he called me in that morning, and sent me to fetch a basket of coals for him—he used to sit in the parlour, opposite the window—I never saw him sit with that window open.
COURT. Q. When Mr. Templeman paid you the 7 s., did he take it out of a little mahogany box? A. Yes, it stood on the table—I saw a quantity of half-crowns, shillings, and sixpences in it—that was between a quarter and twenty minutes before six o'clock—I was not at home when the girl came to inform my mother-in-law of the window being open—I did not leave Sadler's Wells theatre till about five minutes to nine o'clock in the morning.
COURT to ELIZABETH THORNTON. Q. When you went back to tell your mother the window was open, what did she say? A. She said, "Good God! I hope nobody has got in, and murdered the old gentleman"—that was before she went to the house.
WILLIAM KEAR ( police-constable N 131.) On Tuesday morning, the 17th of March, about twenty minutes after eleven o'clock, I received information, and went to Mr. Templeman's cottage, in company with two surgeons—I found the door fast inside—I went in through the sitting-room window, and opened it—it was single-locked—I unlocked it, and let the, surgeons in—it was locked from the inside, and the key was in the door—there are two rooms to the house—there is only one front door, and a door to each room—there is no back door—the two windows are in front of the cottage, one on each side of the door—there is no back window, and no way of getting
out of the house behind—you must come either through the door or one of the front windows—there is a wooden partition between the two rooms—I saw the body of Mr. Templeman lying on his back on the floor, not far from the bed—his room is small—his feet laid towards the fire-place, and his head towards the window—his hands were tied across with a cord in front of him—there was one cord round the wrists, and a smaller cord tied round the other cord—a stocking was over his eyes, and tied behind—there was another stocking on the floor—both stockings were covered with blood—I suppose the blood on the one over his eyes came off the floor where he lay—there was blood on both sides of the stocking—Sergeant Collins came to the cottage soon after, and he found some of the deceased's clothes on a sofa—he had nothing on but a shirt—I did not observe whether it was a night-shirt or day-shirt—his legs were not tied—there was a chest of drawers in the sitting-room—the two top-drawers had been forced open, it appeared to me, by a chisel—in one of the drawers was a box open, which Collins has—I do not know what wood it is made of—I suppose it is mahogany—I should think it is about six inches by ten or eleven—there was nothing in it—in the bed-room Mr. Roe, the surgeon, picked up a tooth off the pillow, and one off the chair, and I myself picked one up off the floor—there was a pool of blood close to the body—the body lay on the top of the blood—most of the blood was about the head—the tooth was in part of the blood close to the head—the pillow of the bed was all bloody—the sheets were not—the most blood was on the pillow, and the rest on the floor.
Cross-examined. Q. I wish to know exactly what time it was you went to Mr. Templeman's cottage? A. About twenty minutes after eleven o'clock—I went at the request of Mr. Roe, the surgeon—my beat is not in Pocock's-fields—it was Harris's, No. 112, beat that morning—he is not here—he went on duty at nine o'clock that morning, and would continue there until two—I do not know who he relieved at nine—there was a man who left at nine—he went on duty at six—the man he relieved would be on duty from nine the night before till six in the morning—I do not believe that either of the men who were there that night and morning are here—I know Pocock's-fields—there are a great number of cottages there—they are principally inhabited by a working class of people—MR. Templeman's cottage is surrounded by other cottages.
COURT. Q. Then you don't know who the men were—what is their beat, how far does it go? A. It is called the Liverpool-road beat—it belongs to that beat—I came on at nine o'clock that morning, and was going off at two o'clock—I was at Highbury—I do not know how near the policeman in the Liverpool-road beat would have to go to the place where this happened—I heard him say he passed the cottage about ten o'clock, and heard nothing then—I examined the deceased's wrists—I did not observe them when the cord was taken off—I cannot tell whether the cord was put on after or before he was dead—he was bruised about the knees, and on the breast—the pillow-case was all over blood—it appeared that a blow had been given him while he was lying there, by the tooth being on the pillow.
MARY THORNTON re-examined. Q. To whom did you first give any information concerning the state in which you found the cottage? A. To my son's landlady, a person named Pershouse, in the next cottage to ours, about three minutes after I first found it out—my son returned from the
theatre about ten or twenty minutes after nine o'clock in the morning—I had heard Mr. Templeman mention that he had a grandson living in Mortimer-street, and I knew him well—as soon as my son came home from the theatre, I sent him to give information to Mr. Templeman—that was in consequence of what the deceased had told me during his life time.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see Mr. Templeman's body through the window? A. I did—I said so to Mrs. Pershouse—she did not accompany me to the window, and I told the baker, who came up with Mr. Templeman's bread—I do not know the policeman who was on duty at the time—I never saw him—I have seen policemen there, but do not know who was there that morning—they very seldom came there—I did not attempt to find one.
EDWARD ROE . I am a surgeon, and live in Brown-buildings, Islington. I accompanied Mr. Lord and a policeman to Mr. Templeman's cottage, about twenty minutes after eleven o'clock—the policeman got through the window, opened the door, and let us in—I saw the deceased with only a shirt on, lying with his feet towards the fire-place, and his head towards the door of the room—the body was in the bed-room, towards the door, which is between the two rooms—he was lying on his back, rather inclining to the left side, lacing the window—his eyes were firmly bandaged with a stocking—if he had been alive, he could not have seen—there was a good deal of blood on the stocking, which I think must have been saturated with the blood on the floor, after it was tied on his eyes—I have no doubt his head had fallen on the floor, and the stocking got into the blood—his hands were securely tied by a cord in this posture—( describing it)—a small cord secured the other cord fastened between the wrists, through a noose in the cord, to make it more secure—I did not notice the wrists then—I did after the cord was removed, and they were galled, and the hands and nails blue—that would unquestionably show that it had been put on during life—I have no doubt he struggled—the blueness would be occasioned by the pressure of the cord—I observed several bruises on his face, a severe blow over the left temple, and a cut on the nose—the lower jaw was fractured on both sides—I observed four clean cuts on the back of the head, and one or two bruises on the breast bone, in conjunction with the collar-bone—his knees were much abraised, rubbed up, as he had, no doubt, been struggling on the floor—there was some blood on the floor immediately under the deceased's head—I should say perhaps ten ounces—the wounds would most likely continue to bleed some time—there was no contused wound on the left temple to cause the blood—I have no doubt a good deal of the blood came from the jaw, and also the clean cuts on the back of the head—the blow on the temple was most probably given by a blunt round instrument, similar to a policeman's staff—it might be done by a piece of wood—the same instrument might have produced the fracture of the jaw, and one blow might produce both fractures—I think the wound on the nose was from a kick, or something of that description, not done by a sharp instrument—the wounds on the back of the head were inflicted by a very sharp instrument.
COURT. Q. Were they inflicted as with a knife, as if a person had cut, or a hatchet, as if they had struck? A. Neither—I should say like a chisel, as if a chisel had been struck on the back of the head.
MR. JONES. Q. Did you find any teeth? A. I found one on the pillow, with a great deal of blood round it, and another on a chair, with a few spots of blood—I was present when a third was found by the policeman
—I examined the jaw, and found three teeth had been forced from the sockets—they were not front, but tide teeth—I think that was very probably done by the blow which produced the fractures—there were several blows about the mouth, and I think one while he was lying in bed, from the circumstance of the tooth being found on the pillow, and the blood there—the tooth on the chair was as if it had fallen out when he got out of bed—I found a small piece of stick, about two inches and a half or three inches long, with hair attached to it, and saturated with blood—it was the hair of the head—it was dark hair—I can scarcely call it grey—this is the piece of splinter—( produced)—there is hair on it now—a kick from the heel or toe of a shoe might have made the cut on the nose, if there were nails in it, and I think it might be done by a shoe or boot without nails—from the appearance of the body when I saw it, I should judge it had been dead fire or six hours—it retained a little warmth about the region of the heart—the body was quite cold—the time in which a body gets cold depends occasionally on the kind of death the person meets with—a body has retained warmth occasionally two days after death—ten or eleven hours is not at all an unusual time—I have no doubt, had the body remained in bed, it would have been warm a long time after it was—MR. Lord accompanied me—he was also present at the post mortem examination—the injuries which I saw on the body, produced death—I have no doubt the death was owing to the violence I have described.
COURT. Q. How was the stocking fastened round the head? A. It was tied in a knot twice—the knot was formed in the stocking itself—the blow on the temple was above the stocking, over the temple.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it your judgment, as a man of experience, that the body had been dead about five or six hours? A. It is—I should rather say a body would be more likely to retain heat two days where the party came to his death by violent means—a body would of course be more likely to get cold on the floor than in bed—it was a very cold night on Monday, and morning too—I should think there was not sixteen ounces of blood altogether on the pillow and floor.
Q. I believe the piece of stick is mahogany? A. I am no judge—it is saturated with blood, and the character of the wood is altered—it is splintered off a round stick—there is no doubt of it.
MR. JONES. Q. In your judgment, would the blows you saw on the deceased occasion instant death? A. I believe the blow over the left temple would occasion instant death—I did not observe whether there was any matting or carpet on the floor—he might have been dead more than five or six hours—it is mere matter of opinion—saw nothing to render it improbable that he had been dead a longer time.
COURT. Q. It may have been fifteen, ten, or five or six, but it must have been five or six, that is what you mean? A. Yes, it could not have been less than five or six—I do not know whether it might have been fifteen, it is a very difficult question to answer—I should rather not pledge myself to a few hours, more or less.
ALFRED LORD. I am a surgeon, and live in Trinidad-place, Islington. I was present at Mr. Templeman's cottage with Mr. Roe, and saw the state in which the body was—I attended the post mortem examination—in my judgment he died of the wounds I saw—the blow on the left temple would alone be sufficient to produce death—it produced concussion of the brain, and extravasation of blood—he would not be able to stir after that,
the same blow producing a fracture of the skull, he could not struggle afterwards—the struggling with the arms and knees must have been before—after the blow which fractured the jaw there might have been a good deal of struggling.
COURT. Q. Did the deceased appear to be an athletic strong man? A. He appeared to have been so at one period of life, and from my observation of his body he was rather a stout man—he appeared rather a strongish man, and one who would struggle a good deal, I think—the hands were blue below the cord—the hands and nails—I do not think one man could have tied his hands unless another held them.
JOHN COLLINS ( police-sergeant.) I went to Mr. Templeman's cottage on. Tuesday morning, and was there with the surgeons—I have produced the splinter of wood—it was cut at the police-office—I have had it examined, according to the prisoner's request, by a turner, who gave his opinion that it is "red saunders"—he described it as a very heavy close wood, coming from New South Wales—I searched the drawers in the deceased's sitting-room—the top drawer on the left hand side had been broken open apparently by a chisel or small crow-bar or something—I found in it two boxes, a quantity of papers, and two notes of the Bank of Elegance—one for 50 l. and the other for 5 l.—( producing them)—this is the mahogany box—it has a lock to it—it is not broken—it was shut when I found it, but unlocked—I found the key of it in the deceased's trowsers pocket—I do not think any violence had been offered to the lock—there are also a quantity of papers, bills, and memorandums—I found no money in the drawers, but in his trowsers pocket was a purse with three sixpences—that was in the same pocket with the key—the papers were apparently tumbled about a good deal—these notes, folded up in this way look like notes of the Bank of England—I thought they were so when I first found them—behind the door of the sitting room I found a chopper and a hammer in the corner between the outer door and the bed-room door—I do not think they had been used—I also found a hammer in the cupboard on the top of some clothes—that did not appear to have been used—I found no piece of wood except this—I found no weight except two clock weights—they were round at the bottom and flat at the top—they were in the further corner of the sitting room, attached to the clock—I did not find any other weights in the bed-room.
Cross-examined. Q. How many persons have you taken up on this charge? A. There have been four persons, Jarvis and his wife, Ciprani, and the prisoner—Pocock's-fields is included in my duty—I was on duty on the Monday night—I did not visit Pocock's-fields—Thomas Peacock is the man who was on duty there—he is not here—Harris came on duty at nine o'clock—I do not know who went on duty between six and nine o'clock—I know Pocock's fields—I should think Mr. Templeman's cottage is a mile, or nearly so, from the Angel, at Islington.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How far is it from the Rainbow public-house? A. About a quarter of a mile.
JANE LOVETT . I am the wife of Jonathan Lovett, and keep an eating-house at No. 2, Northern Buildings, Somer's Town. We occupied the house as tenant to Mr. Templeman—on Monday the 16th of March Mr. Templeman called on me at a quarter before twelve o'clock—I had known him about three years—he appeared generally poorly in the winter, he had better health in the summer—he appeared rather poorly that day—he had a
cold, I imagine—I paid him 3 l. that morning—it consisted of five or six half-crowns—I am positive there were five, and one half-sovereign, the rest in shillings, and to the best of my knowledge six sixpences.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know where you got that money? A. I took it in business at different times—I took the whole of it within two days in our shop.
HANNAH MORGAN . I live at No. 18, Skinner-street, Somer's Town—I knew the deceased—he called on me on the 16th of March at half-past eleven o'clock in the morning, and asked me to lend him 3 l., which I did—I gave him fourteen half-crowns and twenty-five shillings—he gave me 1 s. for apiece of mutton to carry home to make him some soup—he dined with us, and left at half-past three o'clock—he seemed in very low spirits and bad health, labouring under a very bad cold.
COURT. Q. Did he give you any reason why he borrowed the money? A. He said he wanted it to make up an account of money—he left at a quarter before twelve o'clock, and came in again and dined with us afterwards.
MR. JONES. Q. Did he ask you to lend him the 3 l. before he went away, or afterwards? A. He asked for it as soon as he returned to dine, which was about a quarter before twelve, or from that to twelve—I do not live near him—he left at half-past three o'clock in the afternoon—Skinner-street leads into the New-road, nearly opposite Judd-street—I cannot tell how far that is from Pocock's fields—I never was there, only at the Barnsbury Castle public-house—I suppose it is two miles from there—I know Mrs. Lovett—she lives about three doors from me—he gave me the shilling after he asked for the 3 l.
JANE LOVETT re-examined. I did not observe whether he had any other money about him when I paid him the 3 l. for the rent.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did he put it? A. In his right hand trowsers pocket.
JOHN BUSH . I am a labourer, and live in Goodwin-street, Barnsbury-park. I had a cottage in Pocock's-fields, called Spring Cottage—about seven months ago the deceased offered me 25 l. for it—he never brought any money forward—we did not come to any agreement at that time—I saw him about a week or nine days after—there was an agreement between my wife and him—he said he would give me 25 l. if I would take it—I said it was no use, I wanted 30 l.—we only had one conversation on the subject—it was against his own gate—I stood outside and he inside—I mentioned to several people about what had taken place between Mr. Templeman and myself about the cottage.
COURT. Q. This conversation took place between you and him alone? A. Yes.
Cross-examined. Q. How far from Mr. Templeman's cottage do you live? A. I suppose two hundred yards.
BENJAMIN HAWKINS . I keep a beer-shop in Bride-street, Islington, not far from Mr. Templeman's cottage—I knew him—I do not remember any communication between Bush and Mr. Templeman about a cottage—I saw a sum of money with Mr. Templeman at the time he was about to buy a cottage—I do not know how much it was—it is as much as three or four months ago.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know John Bush? A. Yes—that is not the circumstance I refer to.
COURT. Q. Then this money had no reference to Bush's cottage? A. No, it was for Mr. Game's cottage.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you know Jarvis's cottage? A. Yes—I knew Mrs. Jarvis and the prisoner—I saw him at Mrs. Jarvis's once, sitting by the fire—I do not know whether he was smoking—it it two or three months ago.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you happen to know a man named Henry Wright? A. Yes—I believe he is a pot-man—he lodged at Allen's at the same time as Gould lodged there—that is a good while ago, I should think about last Christmas—Wright was out of employ then—I do not know where he is pot-man—when I first knew him he was at Mr. Stroud's, at the King's Arm's public-house, Liverpool-road—that is twelve months ago, I believe—it is about a quarter of a mile from Pocock's-fields—he was pot-man there—I think it was after that that he went to lodge at Allen's—I cannot recollect distinctly the time he lodged there—I am not sure whether it was before Christmas or after—I do not know how long he lived there—he very seldom frequented my beer-shop, he did once now and then to take a glass of porter—I do not know any other place besides Stroud's where he lived—he was a good bit out of employ, I know, but how long I could not positively tell—I have not seen him about Pocock's-fields lately with beer—I did at the time he lived at Stroud's—he was not living there at the time I heard this about the money, it was before that—I do not know whether he was living at Allen's at that time—I cannot say.
MR. JONES. Q. You say Wright and the prisoner lodged together at Allen's. A. Yes—I do not know whether they were intimate friends—I do not recollect that I ever saw them together away from Allen's.
JOSEPH MUSTOW . I live at a cottage at the back of Mr. Temple-man's, close to his—I do not know the prisoner, but I have seen him about with beer from the Barnsbury Castle public-house where he was pot-man—I cannot say how long it is since he left there—I know I took beer of him before Christmas, but I do not think I have since—I do not know whether he supplied Mr. Templeman with beer—I have seen him about the cottages supplying people with beer—I knew Mr. Templeman very well—I have heard that he was in the habit of talking to people about his money, and he has showed it to me several times—he has paid me small sums of money—he generally took it out of his pocket—he took out more than he wanted—he appeared fond of showing his money—on the Tuesday before he was murdered he showed me this 50 l. note—it was doubled up so that I could just see the amount at the corner—it was in his room—he went to the drawer and fetched it out to show it me—I did not mention the circumstance to any body till after his death—I have seen the prisoner at Mrs. Jarvis's, when he lived at the Barnsbury Castle public-house—I cannot say that I have seen him there since, or in the neighbourhood—I have two dogs—I keep one at the end of my house where I keep my pigs—that is just at the back of Mr. Templeman'a cottage—it is always out at night chained up—he is a sort of lurcher—any body he knows may do any thing with him, but to strangers he is not so civil—I kept that dog there while the prisoner was at the Barnsbury Castle—the prisoner was not acquainted with the dog—I do not think he knew him—he might have seen him, but not to be near him—I do not think the prisoner was ever on my premises.
Cross-examined. Q. When were you subpoenaed to attend here? A. Last night—that was the first time I was applied to.
COURT. Q. Did your dog make any noise on the night of the 16th? A. No—the dog was about as far from the window at which the persons got into the house, as across this court, or half as far again—I am sure the dog would begin to bark at a stranger at that distance, if he could hear his footsteps, but it would be ten to one if the dog heard them, because that window is in front, and the dog was behind.
HENRY WRIGHT . I am potman at the Duchess of Kent public-house, Devereux-street, Dover-road, where I have been seven weeks—it is about four miles from Mr. Templeman's cottage—I have known the prisoner from seven to eight months—I saw him on the 12th or 13th of last March, I am not positive which—I think it was the 12th, Thursday—he came to the Duchess of Kent public-house about half-past seven o'clock in the evening, and called fur half-a-pint of porter, and put down a penny, saying he had no more money—I did not take the money—I brought him a pint of porter—I then went out to deliver my beer—I returned about half-past eight o'clock—the prisoner was then standing on the outside, opposite the house, talking to a young man, a potman in the neighbourhood, named Richard Squires—there was no conversation between them after I came up—we had some porter together—as soon as we had had the pot of porter between us three, the prisoner and I walked up the street together—while we were walking together he said he had been ill, and was without money, and for want of money he was getting worse—he stated that he knew an old man who had got money—I asked him how he knew he had got money—he said he had heard he had bid 25 l. for a cottage, and he had flashed a 50 l. note, and said if that was not enough he had got more—the prisoner said that he had put the notes into a drawer, and it would be like a gift to him, for he knew where to put his hand on it—he said he should like to have a right one along with him—I said, "A right one!"—he said, "Yes, a right one, or I could do it myself"—I asked him where the old gentleman lived—he said, "Oh, not far."
Q. Not far from when? A. From where Gould lived.
COURT. Q. Did he say so? A. Yes; he said, not far from him, that it, signifying where Gould lived—he said, "Not far from home"—those were the words be used.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Now, is that all you remember of what passed, or was any thing else said? A. That is all I remember—when he was in the tap-room he named to me that he had been to a publican whom he once lived with, and that he should endeavour to get a situation, and try that man if he would give him a character, that he would send to him—that was before I walked with him—he said nothing further than I have stated with reference to the old gentleman's money.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know where Mr. Templeman lived? A. Yes, in Pocock's-fields, at the back of Liverpool-terrace—a sawyer lived in the next cottage to Mr. Templeman's when I served him—I do not know who lives in it now—I think the sawyer's name was William Izod—he worked in the City-road—I served him—I served most of them near Mr. Templeman's cottage, all round the field—I served the principal of the cottages—I think there were not ten persons who did not deal with me when I carried beer round there—nobody lived with Mr. Templeman then—I do not know who took care of his house—this cottage was building
at the time, and after Izod left, he lived in this cottage till his cottage was built—MR. Tiffin was building this cottage for him while he lived in Izod's—he took possession of the new cottage about two years back—I have known him as long as that—that is the only cottage into which he shifted—when I first knew him I was living at Mr. Ralph's, the William the Fourth public-house, at the corner of Park-street and Minerva-place—I left there because I got another situation—I did not like it—there was no other reason for my leaving—I went after that to the Craven Arms public-house, Bayswater—I was about twenty-six weeks at Mr. Ralph's the first time—I did not go with a character to the other place—I was eleven months at the Craven Arms public-house—after leaving there I went to work for Mr. Ralph a week, till he got a man, and then I went to the Hope and Anchor public-house, Islington—the landlord left the house, and Mr. Walton took it, and there were a few words between us, and I left—there was no charge against me—the words were about stopping out a few hours—I stopped out between four and eight o'clock—I went away of my own accord—the words were with Mr. Walton, who keeps the house—there had been no charge against me of any kind—there has been a charge, but it was not from Mr. Walton—it was from the Duke of York public-house, Gloucester-street—I asked permission to go out; while I was out, a cash-box was lost, and when I returned home I was taken up on suspicion, and charged with stealing it—that might be between four and five years ago—there has been nothing further—I never was in custody before, nor yet charged with any thing—I lived at the City of London public-house—I was not in custody respecting any thing there—it it at the corner of Sydney-street and Dalby-terrace—I left there to take a beer-shop, after nine years' service—I left of my own accord—there was a cellar there—nothing was said about my conduct in that cellar—that I swear—that is somewhere about the time after I came out of Hatton-garden office—I went to live there again—I lived there about ten months—I opened the beer-shop before I went to the Duke of York public-house, but it did not succeed—I know Mr. Jarvis's cottage very well.
Q. How long before the night Mr. Templeman lost his life, were you in Pocock's-fields do you think? A. On the 9th or 10th I was there—I went to visit Mr. Allen of Wilson-cottage—I had lodged there some time before—there are two rooms in Allen's cottage and, an old back kitchen, and a wash-house adjoining it—there is about half an acre of garden—the privy is in the wash-house, adjoining the bed-room—you can go from the parlour into the little, kitchen into it, and you can go into the garden to get to it.
