Q. You mentioned something about what the prisoner said about a drawer, do you mean to say he said that? A. Yes—I recollected that in my statement at the time I was at Hatton-garden at my last evidence—I think I recollected it at the time I was at Hatton-garden—I am certain I did—I have been twice to Hatton-garden—the first time was on a Saturday—the Saturday after Mr. Templeman's body had been found on the Tuesday—I did not recollect it when I first went—I do not know any thing of this little mahogany box—I am not positive that I ever heard of a mahogany box—I think, but I have never mentioned it, because I was not positive of hearing of it—I can read—I have read an account of this in the newspapers—I read the newspapers between my first and second
examination—I did not read any account of where the money was kept.
Q. On your oath, did you not read in the newspapers where the money was placed? A. I have read the papers, but I speak from the words Gould named to me, that the money was in the drawer—I have no recollection of reading in the papers where the money was placed.
COURT. Q. What did you read the newspapers for, was it to refresh your memory? A. No.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Was it a Sunday paper you read? A. I have read the "Dispatch" once, but not the whole of it—I think I read it the Sunday after my first examination—I do not know that I did—I am not certain that it was the Sunday after, because in our business I catch hold of a paper, and lay it down again—I did not read the whole account of the evidence—I had not the opportunity of doing so—I might read part of it—I recollected about the drawer after I had been to Hatton-garden on the Saturday.
COURT. Q. Had you mentioned about the drawer till you read the "Dispatch?" A. No.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. How old are you? A. Twenty-seven—the last time I lodged at Allen's is about seven weeks back—I left Allen's to go to the situation I am now in—I was lodging there last Christmas, and Gould lodged in the same house—he did not lodge there at Christmas—he was at place—he had been at place a week or two, at Mr. Bartlett's, the Barnsbury Castle public-house—I was living there alone at Christmas—I knew Mr. Templeman's cottage, and the window, but never took particular notice of it—I cannot say whether it was a whole window, or whether there was any thing the matter with it.
Q. Were you aware that in the conversation you say took place a robbery was intended? A. Yes—I understood his intent was to commit a robbery—it was mentioned only in my bearing—I did not suppose any violence was to be done to the old gentleman—I understood him to mean by "a right one," somebody to assist him, that he could depend upon—I understood there was to be a robbery only.
Q. Were you shocked at it? A. Why, I took very little notice of it—I named it to Squires when I went round at nine o'clock, and said I did not know whether it was intended or not—Squires lives at the Two Brewers public-house, the next house—I did not name it to any policeman—Squires said he did not think if he had any intention he would have named it—Squires is not here.
Q. Did you think it was a joke? A. I did not think he was joking—I took no notice of what he said, till I named it to Squires at nine o'clock, and neither of us took notice of it—it never entered my mind till I read—till I heard of the murder.
Q. Why name it to Squires if you thought nothing about it, and if you did think any thing about it why did you not name it to a policeman? A. I named it to Squires, and it did not enter my mind afterwards till Squires told me he had read in the paper of a murder, and I read of it when I got home—I first heard of it on the Wednesday—Squires had read the paper, and named it to me—I do not know James Rogers.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You have been asked about a cash-box—had some charge been made against you about it? A. Yes—only suspicion—I think I was in custody a week—I was afterwards discharged by the Magistrate
—I did not come to trial upon it—except on that occasion I have never been in custody in my life, and I never bad any charge of dishonesty theft of any kind made against me.
Q. Did you give evidence in this case of your own accord, or were you called on to do so? A. I had not time to come over—I sent word over to Mr. Allen on the Wednesday, about an hour after Squires made the communication to me—I went before the Magistrate on the Saturday—one of the officers called on me—that was after I had made the communication to Allen—I stated at the office all of the circumstances as near as I could recollect—I never had any quarrel with the prisoner—I had always been on friendly terms with him—Squires is living at this place now—( a messenger was here sent to fetch Squires.)
JOHN RICHARD JOHNSON . I live at No. 7, Dotset-street, Spitalfields, and am a print-colourer—I colour caricatures, and principally religious prints—that is the way in which I get my living—I have known the prisoner between four and five years—I saw him on Friday evening the 13th of March—I left off work about half-past seven o'clock that evening, and when I came down stairs there was a person in the passage inquiring for Jem —I did not see him—I heard him, and when I came down in the passage the woman below said Jem was not at home, and then I saw the prisoner "Arthur Nicholson"—that was the name he gave me—"a private in the 11th Light Dragoons, Canterbury"—the prisoner was present when I wrote this paper I have in my hand—that if ever he should be tried as a deserter, I might know his right name—he gave me this, as near as possible, twelve months ago last December—he desired me to write down his name that I might know if he was ever tried as a deserter, he was the man—it is my handwriting—he saw me write it—I put it into the drawer, and there it remained till this occurrence took place—when I saw him in the passage, I said, "Dick, how do you get on?"—he said he wanted to see Jem —I asked him what he wanted to see Jem for—he said he wanted to see him, for he wanted to borrow a screw —I asked him what for—he said he was going to serve an old gentleman in a lonely cottage—I told him he had better not do any thing of the kind, if he did he would be sure to get transported—he told me he might as well be transported for that, for if he was takes as a deserter, he was sure to be transported—at the end of that conversation, a fight took place at the bottom of our street, and I and the prisoner went down together to see it—seven or eight rounds were fought—the police came up to disperse the mob, and I and the prisoner returned back again about as far as our door, and I wished him good night—he said, "You had better not go yet, for I want to see Jem very particularly"—I asked him what he wanted to see Jem so very particularly for—he said he wanted a darkey, that he had left there some time ago, and it might be handy—I told him if he wanted to see Jem so very particularly, he had better go over the way to the public-house—he said he could not go over the way, for he had no money—he further stated, that if he served the old gentleman, as was his intention on the Saturday, he was going over the water to see his aunt or cousin, and would give me a call on Sunday, and let me know.
Q. Let you know what? A. Whether he had robbed the old gentleman, I suppose—that is the inference I drew from what he said—he did not say he would rob him—he said he would call on Sunday and let me know.
Q. You have said he spoke about a screw and a darkey, what is meant
by a screw? A. I cannot tell, only from what I have been in the habit of reading—I have read "Bell's Life in London"—I know what screw means according to the defamation I have heard in the book—according to the defamation I have read in "Bell's Life in London," it means a picklock-key—I bid the prisoner good night, and we parted—I saw no more of him—I afterwards saw an account of the murder at Islington in the newspaper, and the day after I gave information to a policeman of the H division—that division is in the neighbourhood of Spitalfields—I then stated what I have to-day, I believe, word for word—I was examined before the Magistrate, after recognising the prisoner—I was taken by a policeman to be sure of him—Waddingtoo the jailer said, "You are to recognise the prisoner"—I believe I said "Yes," and I pointed to him, and said, "That is the man."
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known Gould? A. Between four and five years—that was as near as possible my first acquaintance with him—I only knew him then as Dick or Richard, nothing further—that was at Mr. Webber's, at the public-house at the corner of our street—I never inquired his other name—I should say I knew him three years as Dick.
Q. Where was be living when he got you to write this card? A. Why, I gave him a lodging, to tell you the truth, at Now 7, Dorset-street—I have not known him up to the present time—he has been absent some time—I have not seen him but once previous to the Friday, for eight or nine weeks—that was at the Weaver's Arms public house— Jem is a party who had moved in below me—his name is James Rogers—he had moved in very likely two months—it is not my house—I am only the tenant of one apartment—I have known Rogers, I should say, fourteen or fifteen years—it was not by my introduction that he came into the room below—if I had had the letting of the house, I would not have let him in at all.
Q. You knew him too well? A. No, I never knew any dishonest action of the man—he is a labouring man—he has only a small apartment at 1 s. 3 d. a week—he gets his living among the wharfs, on board the barges, to carry flour or corn, or any thing on shore if barges come up, and if they wanted any body to unload them, they would as life employ him as any body—he may have regular masters for any thing I know—I do not know that he has.
Q. What has he been during the fourteen or fifteen years you have known him? A. He got his living by going about buying and repairing old umbrellas, portering, or any thing like that—I do not know whether he comes in at good hours at night—he never lived in the same house as me before—I had no objection to having him in the same house, but I have not the letting of the house.
Q. Why should you object to let him the house if you had the letting of it? A. Because he is a man so often out of work, and could not afford to pay his rent—I have been in business about twenty-four or twenty-five years, not always colouring religious prints, but latterly I have done them—I have done some thousands of prints for Mr. Fairburn, No. 110, Minories, principally religious prints—I have occasionally done maps, plans, and charts—that is the only trade I have been in—I never kept a shop—I have been eleven years in my present lodging—I have a wife and family living with me—I have three children out of eight—they live as well as they can since my work has been stopped—my work has been stopped since this fatal occurrence—I have had one little job since.
Q. Why could you not colour prints notwithstanding this? A. On account of my giving evidence—it got into the newspapers, and my master stopped the work—I suspect so—I do not buy and sell things at all—I never did to my knowledge—I bought this coat the other day, and I might sell it to-morrow—I bought every thing I have got on.
COURT. Q. Do not answer in that foolish way—did you ever buy and sell to make profit? A. Never.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. You never bought anything? A. No, it was never said that I did—I was never charged with buying any thing—I was never in any scrape about a robbery, I am certain—I will tell you, the prisoner and I were together at a public-house, a man charged me with robbing him of a shilling, and the prisoner went with me to the station-house, and offered the man 5 s. to let me out—I was not let out—the man came between one and two o'clock in the morning, said he had found the shilling, and wanted the man to discharge me, but he said he could not, as the charge for felony was signed—the man never appeared afterwards—it happened at the Bluecoat Boy public-house, in Dorset-street, within a door or two of my own house—the man was a butcher—I have known him in the neighbourhood for years—that is not quite two years ago, I think—I was once locked up for being drunk and disorderly—I have never been in any difficulty about other people's property, except about the shilling.
Q. When the prisoner said he was going to commit a robbery, did you endeavour to persuade him not to do so? A. I said if he did he would be sure to be transported—I did not endeavour to persuade him not to do it—I did not believe him—I understood what he meant by a screw, by reading "Tom and Jerry" and "Bell's Life"—I have seen that expression in "Tom and Jerry" many times—I have borrowed the book once or twice.
Q. If you thought he really meant nothing, how came you to tell him he would be transported if he did it? A. Why he would if he did it.
Q. I thought you considered he was in fun? A. I did not think he was in much fun—I did not think he was in earnest—he has laughed and joked with me many times before.
Q. Did you really think him in fun or earnest? A. I should firmly believe now that he did mean it, but I did not believe he meant it at the moment—I should sooner say he was in joke than in earnest.
Q. Why persuade him not to do it, then, and tell him he would be transported? A. I said if he did it he would be transported.
Q. Now, while you were pausing to give me an answer, did not you hear what that gentleman (MR. Ballantine) said—did you not give me your answer after hearing what that gentleman said? A. I did not hear what he said, I did not hear him speak—I could see his lips go, that is all—I did not shake hands with the prisoner when I parted with him—I have shook hands with him many times—I did not see Jem that night, nor tell him he was wanted—I left the prisoner at the door when I bid him good night—I did not go up to my own room—I came back between eleven and twelve o'clock, I believe—I do not know whether Jem was at home—I did not see Jem at all before I read any thing in the newspapers—he was in the back-room, I suppose—we have to go down the cellar into the yard, and there is the back-room there—I saw Jem once before I read the newspaper—I think he came home as usual.
MR. JONES. Q. Had you any other reason for not liking to have Jem in the house except his poverty? A. That is all—I have never been in custody except on the occasion I have mentioned.
Q. You have been asked whether you believed the prisoner was serious when he said he was going to commit a robbery; did you believe it at the time? A. So many jokes have passed between me and him, I did not know how to take it—I believe it now, from what has happened.
COURT. Q. What time was it he came to you? A. About twenty minutes to eight o'clock, as near as possible—at half-past seven o'clock I sent my wife home with three hundred prints—I had just got my coat and shoes on and came down, and judge it to be that time—he was with me altogether about a quarter of an hour—he left me before eight o'clock—as I passed the station-house to go into Church-street, the bells struck out eight, that was the moment I had parted with him—I did not see Jem during all the time I was with him.
JAMES ROGERS . I live at No. 7, Dorset-street, Spitalfields—I am an umbrella maker by trade, but I do not follow it—I work at the water-side as a labourer—I have known the prisoner about five years, or it may be six, by the name of Richard or Dick —on the 13th of March was the last time I saw him, barring seeing him at Hatton-garden—I saw him at my door, where I live, when I was going home between eight and nine o'clock in the evening.
COURT. Q. Between eight and nine o'clock? A. Yes—I believe I once said a quarter before eight o'clock—I now say it was between eight and nine.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Where were you standing? A. I was coming home, and he was standing by the window as I was coming, just between the window and the door—the first observation he made to me was, "Jem, you are just the chap I want to see"—I said, "What do you want to see me for?"—he said, "I want for you to lend me two screws and a darkey" —I should say he meant skeleton keys and a dark lantern—I said he might as well ask me for a £500 note as to ask me for such things as them—nothing else was said on that subject—I said I had no money for beer or else I would treat him, and he said he had none—my brother-in-law was along with me—he had not been with me all the time—he came up along with me when I came to the door, but I don't think he heard any thing of what he said, but I cannot say whether he did or not—I said to my brother-in-law, "Bill, you stand a pot of beer"—he said, "I have not got any money without going home to fetch it, but go over to the public-house, call for a pot of beer, and I will come and pay for it"—we went to the Blue-coat Boy public house in Dorset-street, and had a pot of beer; my brother-in-law paid for it—I once kept an old iron shop—that was while I was acquainted with the prisoner, and he knew it.
Cross-examined. Q. Where was it you kept the old iron shop? A. In Little Paternoster-row—I did not keep the house—I had the lower part nine or ten months, then necessity compelled me to leave it—I sold locks and any thing in that way—I bought and sold old iron, and sometimes bottles and rags, not any thing that came to hand, because there might be things that came in a different sort of way—I was very careful of what I bought—I used to go and collect things—I mostly bought in Petticoat-lane—I have never been in any scrape arising from the old iron shop, not at all—I was never taken up—if I were to say I never was in any prison, I should say false—when I was a boy I got a month for sleeping out in the open air—
that is seventeen or eighteen years ago—I was in no business then—I was sent to Coldbath-fields for a month—I cannot recollect any thing else—I became acquainted with the expressions darkey and screws by reading "Bell's Life" newspaper—I don't know whether Johnson reads it—I am sure I have seen those expressions in "Bell's Life"—I never read it in "Tom and Jerry"—I do not know that I ever took up that paper—I have known Johnson, I suppose, about ten years—he lodges in the same house as me, with his wife—he has got three or four children I believe—I very seldom go up into his room—I believe he was a print-colourer when I first knew him—I may have been up in his room two or three times.
Q. How long before Gould visited you had you seen Johnson? A. I suppose not for a month—I go in and out of the place—I live down stairs—I have not lodged there altogether, I believe, above four months—I have seen Johnson out of the house, but not above once in the house to my knowledge—my brother-in-law's name is William Crane—I found the prisoner standing between the window and the door—Crane was with me—he went home to get the money, returned and paid for the beer, and then retired to look after a sweetheart of his—he was standing within a yard or two till he went away to get the money—the prisoner did not whisper—he spoke loud enough—Crane might or might not hear it—I cannot say—I dare say he might—I had no notion what the darkey and screws were wanted for—I never gave it a thought—there had been no darkey left with me—I did not see Johnson at all that night—I do not think I saw him at all after, till I had been up to Hatton-garden—I am not positive of it—he and I had no conversation—I never kept such things as skeleton keys when I was in the iron trade—nothing of the kind, nor darkeys —when I first knew the prisoner he was pot-boy at a house at the bottom of the street.
Q. Did you think there was any thing wrong in asking for these? A. I did not give it a thought whether there was any thing wrong or right—I did not trouble my head about it—I passed it by as a joke—I get my bread by the water-side when I am in employ—I do not work for any body in the umbrella business—I work for myself, but I am an occasional man in the other job—I had a job on Saturday—I sometimes go out very soon in the morning, and work from eight or ten o'clock in the evening till twelve o'clock, when we are landing ale, and such things—I was examined at the police-office.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Were you compelled to go before the Magistrate, or did you give evidence voluntarily? A. I saw it in the paper, and I went up to Hatton-garden the same day, but there was no Court then—it was not the same day I saw the account of the murder, but the same day I saw my name in the paper—that was on Monday, and I went up the same day, but the examination was not on—it took place the next day, and I attended and gave evidence—I stated all I knew—I have been on friendly terms with the prisoner, but I have not seen him above twice for two years.
COURT. Q. When you went to be examined you knew Johnson had been examined? A. I was told he had been the week previous, but I did not see Johnson till after I had been examined—I think I saw his examination in the newspaper before I was examined, but I will not be positive.
RICHARD SQUIRES . I am waiter at the Two Brewers public-house, George-street, Horsemonger-lane, and have been so four years. I know
the prisoner, but no more than by seeing him three times in my life—I saw him last, I think, on the 17th of March—he came over the water to see Wright—it was on the Thursday evening, to the best of my recollection—I cannot swear whether it was Thursday or Friday—it was about half-past eight o'clock in the evening, or it might be nine o'clock—I saw him close by where Wright lives, at the Duchess of Kent public-house—he was not with Wright at that time—I know Wright by seeing him, and drinking with him now and then—we drank together that evening, me, Wright, and Gould—I left first, I believe, but the other two went away directly—I went home.
COURT. Q. And went to bed? A. After I had done my business—I had to wait on the company in the tap-room—I did nothing more—I saw Wright again, I believe, that night, at the Duchess of Kent public-house, where he lives.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Had you any conversation with Wright when you saw him again, if you did see him? A. I do not know whether it was the same evening or next morning, it might be next morning he saw me, and we might stop and ask how we got on, and he brought it up about Dick —he mentioned Dick's name.
COURT. Q. Who came for you to-day? A. I do not know his name—he said I was wanted here—he did not mention for what, to my knowledge—he did not tell me what I was wanted for—he said I must come to the Old Bailey, or something of that sort—he said he wanted me along with him—he did not say what about, nothing at all.
Q. Do you mean to swear you had no idea what you were gong to be examined to before you went into that box? A. Yes, I knew what it was—I was up at Hatton-garden before, but my evidence was of no use—I was not examined—I stated what I knew, and it was of no use.
Cross-examined. Q. You stated what you had got to say, and then you were sent away? A. Yes—I came here to-day in a cab, with one of the servants, I expect, from this office—he found me at the Two Brewers public-house—I had very little conversation with him in our way—I have not seen Wright here to-day—I heard him examined at Hatton-garden the last time—he and I have talked about the matter since—we have sat together and talked—I was at Hatton-garden the last time he was there, on Tuesday, a week ago—I had never been there before—when I went up I found Gould standing near the Duchess of Kent public-house—Wright was not there then—he came up about half a minute alter I met Gould—I cannot say what was said while we were talking together, nothing very particular—we drank a pint of porter, or whether it was a pint or a pot, I cannot say—we drank it at the opposite corner to the Duchess of Kent, where Wright lives—I mean outside the Duchess of Kent—we were about three minutes together there, it might be four—we did not talk much—we continued drinking till the pot was out—no one paid for the beer at that time—we had beer out with us from my house and from the Duchess of Kent—I was carrying beer out—I cannot say whether the pot was taken out of my beer or out of Wright's—we must have paid for it when we went home—I paid say mistress for mine—I cannot say how much was drank—I cannot say whether it was a pint or a pot—I cannot swear whether it was my master's beer, or Wright's master's, that we drank—I might have paid my mistress for it—I paid for all I brought out—I cannot say how much I paid her—no money was produced by any body while we were drinking—nothing was said about money,
to my knowledge—I believe, when I saw Wright he was coming along with his porter—I was talking with Gould.
Q. When they saw each other, did they ask each other how they did, or what was said? A. No, it appeared they had been together before—Wright might have said, "Halloo"—I do not know that he did—I cannot recollect any thing that was said—it might have been half-past eight or nine o'clock—I should say it was half-past eight o'clock, as near as I can recollect, because I was out with my porter—I go out with it at eight o'clock, and I was just going home—I might have some porter left—I generally have—I cannot say whether I had or not.
Q. Did you toss for any porter? A. We might have tossed, I do not recollect tossing—we might have been a pint each, or stood a pint, either one of us—I have no recollection whether we tossed, or whether we were a pint each, or whether one of us was a pint—I cannot say we did not toss—we might have tossed, and I forget it—I immediately went in, and was waiting on the customers after that—I came out again that night—I might come out with beer after that—that would be at nine o'clock, if it was half-past eight before—if it was after nine o'clock I did not come out—I do not sleep in the house—I sleep with my wife, in St. George's-court, Dover-road—it might be twelve o'clock when I went home—I cannot recollect when I went home that night—I cannot recollect who I saw when I was going home.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You were taking out beer for your mistress—what is the method? do you account for the number of pots you take out? A. I have to pay for all the porter I take out every day—I never return any—I pay for all every night—the person who came to me this morning did not produce any thing, but had a cab for me to ride over—I live 200 or 300 yards from the public-house I work at—I cannot swear whether I left that night before or after the house was closed.
CHARLES ALLEN . I live at Wilson's Cottage, Pocock's-fields: I am a boot and shoemaker—I have seen Mr. Templeman's house since his death, but not before—I know it now—I should say it is between 300 and 500 yards from where I live—I have known the prisoner, as near as I can tell, about twelve months—he has lodged at my house several times—I cannot possibly state when he commenced lodging with me the last time—he lodged with me at least seven nights before this occurred—I know he had very little money, if any, before the 16th of March; and I was confident, from circumstances which occurred, that he had no money—when he has come home of an evening he has said he had no money; and I considered by his getting up of a morning, and not having breakfast before he went out, that he had none, for few people like to go out without food, if they have money to purchase it—on the Saturday night before this occurred he had none, because he made an observation when he came into the house that he had no money, but he should have some in the course of to-morrow, which was Sunday—on the Monday morning following that Saturday, I think he went out between eight and nine o'clock—he had no breakfast with us before he went out that morning—I believe he was dressed something similar to what he is now, but I cannot positively swear whether he had that coat or a jacket on—as far as I can tell, I believe he was dressed much as he is now—he had on a pair of shoes, which I made—I did not make them for him—they were misfits, but I sold them to him—they were ancle-shoes, similar to what I have on now, shoes the leather of which comes above the ancle—they had nails—I saw Gould
nailing them about a fortnight before—they were then getting the worse or wear, still they were wearable—I had seen them everyday on his feet—at the time I saw him nailing them they were fit for wear, I am certain, and would have lasted much longer for nice fine weather than for wet—I have no recollection whether he went out alone or in company that morning—I have no recollection of seeing any body before he went out—the next time I saw him was, as near as I can tell, on the Tuesday morning about three o'clock—I was then lying in my own bed, and my wife likewise—he came into the house—it was very light at the time, the moon shining—it was the lightness of the moon—the instant he came into the house he went into his bed-room, as the door opens into the room—he opens his bed-room door and closes the front door while he passes to his bed-room—my wife said to him, "Richard, it is early," or "late," I am not sure which, and his reply was, "It is early"—nothing more passed—the next time I saw him was, I believe, between eight and nine o'clock on the Tuesday morning—we have no clock to judge the time, but as near as I can tell I saw him between eight and nine—I got up between six and seven—he got up between eight and nine—I was in my sitting room—he came into it, passed through, and went to the wash-house—the wash-house leads to the privy and to the back-yard—he remained in the wash-house, as near as I can tell, from twenty five minutes to half-an-hour—when he came out of his bed-room he had his coat and waistcoat on his arm, and I believe his boots in his hand, but I am not certain whether they were on his feet or in his hand—when he returned, he came through the room where I was sitting at breakfast, and went out at the front door—he went straight from the wash-house to the front door and went out—I observed nothing particular about him when he went out—I believe he had on the same clothes he has now—he had on the same shoes as I had made him, but they were blacked—when he was in a situation he blacked them every morning, but not at other times—how he got the brushes to clean them I do not know, but the brushes were in the back place when he was gone—they were my brushes and blacking—if he used it, it was my blacking.
Q. Can you tell whether, on this night, he used any candle or rush-light to go to bed? A. I never saw Richard have a candle in our house all the time he was in it, unless he asked for such a thing, and that was very seldom—if he wanted one it was merely for a minute to get something out of the room, and he was supplied by our candle which stood on the table—I have not found any candle or rush-light about my premises since that night—he returned about a quarter past seven o'clock on the Tuesday evening, much about that time—I had heard of the murder about half-past ten, or near eleven o'clock—when the prisoner came home I was gone to fetch a pint of porter and a rasher of bacon for supper—when I returned he was sitting in my room talking to my wife—the conversation when I came into the house was, I believe; on the subject of the murder—the first time, I believe, I spoke, or Richard spoke to me, was, "It is a shocking thing: do you consider he could not have done it himself?"—the reply I made was, "Richard, how could it be possible for persons to do such things themselves, having their hands tied and their eyes blindfolded; is it likely?"—that is all the conversation I remember between Richard and me, till afterwards—he was sitting at the table a few minutes after, then got up and went into the back place—he went through the wash-house, as if he was going to the privy—he said his inside was out of order, and he went out at the door as if
going to the privy—he did not remain there more than six or seven minutes—I mentioned to my wife a circumstance which had occurred, and when he came back and sat down I was eating some bacon on a piece of bread—I cut off a piece of bacon—it fell on my knee, and went a contrary way to him—I then cut another piece, and it went towards his legs—I did it purposely, to satisfy myself of something I thought concerning him, that he had got a new pair of shoes—I had a suspicion that he knew something about it—then I did a thing which I have no doubt he understood, I took the candle off the table, and put it between his legs—he directly put up his legs and hid his shoes behind the legs of the chair, and then began the conversation—he said he had been over the water to see his cousin, who had a pair of shoes which did not fit him, and he had jumped into them—before that, he said he should like to have something to eat, and asked if I would go and fetch him a slice of bacon and a pot of beer—I said, "Richard, why not go yourself and fetch it?"—he said, "You know I don't want to go out"—I knew that there was an assault warrant out against him—I fetched him a slice of bacon, a pot of beer, and half-an-ounce of tobacco—he gave me 1 s. to pay for it—he said he had been over to Clapham, Limehouse, Blackwall, and that way, and called on hit cousins and aunts, and they had given him some money—he did not say how much—after I had fetched the beer we had some conversation respecting the preceding night, what a shocking, dreadful affair it was, how any body could have committed such a horrible crime—it was no more than common conversation respecting the murder—the conversation took place chiefly with my wife—I did not pay attention, my attendon was engaged at that time—I recollect asking Richard how it was he was so late—he said he had been to Mr. Frimley's till twelve o'clock, and when he came from there he lit on some friends who detained him—I should say he was in bed before nine o'clock on Tuesday night, or about that time—when he had gone to bed, in consequence of suspicions I entertained, I bolted him in—I did not give information at all—the policemen came at eleven o'clock that night, as near as I can tell, and took him—I had at one time a cheese-knife in my possession, which I have since found—I had also a piece of wood in my possession—I believe it was plum-tree or damson, rather better than a foot and a half long, and about two and a I half inches wide, with sharp edges, and flat—it was very smooth—the edges were sharp—it was to set seams with—it is a heavy wood—I used it in my business.
Q. Have you told us all the conversation, as far as you recollect, that took place between you and the prisoner on the subject of the murder? A. As far as happened, except part that my wife was speaking to him—there was a conversation on Scripture—I recollect in one part Richard said, if the ten commandments were just, or the Mosaic law was right, he had broken them all, for adultery he had committed many times, and drunkenness—and then that conversation dropped with me, and went on with my wife—I got up to go into the yard—that was at the time we were talking of the murder—it was all in the same conversation—I saw some money produced in a stocking at the station-house at Islington—neither the money or the stocking was my property—I believe one farthing was all the money in the house at all that day—I have never been in the habit of keeping money in the wash-house or privy—we have not had any to do it.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you present during all the time your wife
was in conversation with the prisoner that evening? A. Certainly not—I went to fetch some beer, therefore I could not—I went to fetch the beer and bacon after he came home—it was not his bacon I cut when I discovered his shoes, it was my own bacon—I did that while the prisoner was sitting at the table, before I went to fetch his bacon—I believe I ate my own supper before I fetched his.
Q. At that time had there been any conversation about the murder? A. The conversation might have been about the murder, but I took very little notice of any of the conversation, therefore am not bound to answer the question.
Q. Now I ask you to answer the question, had any conversation taken place about the murder before, as you say, you cut the bacon, and saw his new shoes? A. I have already answered that the conversation when I entered the house was on that subject, but I paid so little attention to the conversation between my wife and the prisoner, that probably I could not utter fifty words of the conversation the whole evening, unless the prisoner spoke to me—I mean, I could not utter fifty words of the conversation between the prisoner and my wife, except I had the leading point to lead to it, named—there was a good deal of conversation between him and my wife—I had some suspicion at the time I discovered the new shoes—I cannot say whether it was after that that I went and fetched the bacon for the prisoner—I believe I had eaten my own supper before I fetched it, but I will not swear it—I am not confident whether the bacon was cooked before or after.
Q. At the time you went out to fetch the bacon, had you any suspicion as you have stated? A. Certainly I had at the time, and before—I had before he came into the house—I fetched it from a person of the name of——I cannot recollect—it was about a hundred yards off—I was absent about ten minutes or a quarter of an hour—on my return I found the prisoner sitting at the table in conversation with my wife—as soon as his bacon was cooked he had his supper, smoked his pipe, and went to bed—when I bolted him in his bed-room I said, "He is in, he shall never come out any snore by me"—if my face had been towards him I think he may have heard that, but my back being towards him, he might or might not—it is a question I cannot answer—I sever bolted him in before—I have merely put the bolt to to keep the children in of a morning, but not bolted him in—the window of his room looks into the front garden—it is about two feet and a half or three feet from the ground—I cannot tell whether he paid me for the old shoes which I made him—what I have no dealings with I never answer, I left it to my wife—he paid her in part, whether she had the whole I cannot tell, I know nothing of it—I have been ill this twelvemonth, and have had very little dealings with any person whatever—I have done very little shoe-making within the last twelve months—I was once in the police—I left it, I think, eight years ago—I went after the first division that went to Whitechapel—I was in it, as near as I can tell, a year and four or five months—I left it entirely of my own accord, without any complaint being made.
Q. Now, do I understand you distinctly that there was a great deal of conversation that you cannot recollect? A. Yes, distinctly, that I know nothing of whatever—if it were repeated, I believe, I might then tell, but not without, for I did not pay the least attention to it—I did not take any notice, very little indeed, for my thoughts were far beyond any conversation
—I did not want to hold any conversation—my two children slept with the prisoner—one is about six years old at Michaelmas, and the other four years old—they were in the bed with him that night.
COURT. Q. Why did you bar the door, I do not understand? A. Because I had a suspicion he knew something of the charge—barring the door would prevent his getting out.
Q. Could he not get out of the window? A. That he might, but he could not get into my room, and if he had got out of the door I did not mean to go to sleep—I was not particularly afraid of him, but I did not intend that he should have gone out of my house, without he did get up—if he did get up I should have given information—it was for the public interest as well as my own.
MARY ANN ALLBN . I am the wife of the last witness. The prisoner lodged at our house, and did so on the 17th of March last—I do not know Mr. Templeman's cottage, I never saw it—I know it is but a short distance from our house—I have never been to it, but I know where it is—the prisoner has been lodging with us nearly a twelvemonth, off and on—the last time he was with us for a whole week, the only week he has been with us since he had been at the Barnsbury Castle public-house—he was with us from Saturday night till the Tuesday week, a week and two days—he did not pay me—I never received any money from him for that, because he had none—he told me he had no money many times.
COURT. Q. Did he not pay you at any time during the twelvemonth? A. Yes—the last time he came to us was the Saturday week previous to the murder.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When did he tell you he had no money? A. Frequently—he did not take his meals with us generally since he has been out of employ—he did not take his meals with us at all in this week, only after the Sunday—he took his meals with us two Sundays—MRS. Jarvis brought him some bacon and eggs on the Sunday morning, as he came on Saturday night—on the following Sunday, (the Sunday previous to the murder,) he asked me if I had any breakfast, he wanted some breakfast—I did not answer him, because we had none in the house ourselves—that was between eight and nine o'clock—he said he should go over the water to his cousin's, but his handkerchief was so dirty, and I lent him a stock of my husband's—he then went out directly—he returned at one o'clock in the day—I was at home—he said he had been out, and drank too much beer, and it had made him very ill on an empty stomach—he went out, and was very sick—he came in again, and I asked him if he would have some dinner—he said he was too ill—he asked if he might lie down—I said he might, and he did so—my little boy called him up at five o'clock, for me to make the bed, and put the children to bed—he had gone to lie down in his own room—my children slept with him—he got up, and came into the room where I was sitting—he asked me if I had got any tea—I told him I had not—he asked me to let my little boy go and fetch him some, for he had taken 1 s. while he was out—my little boy did fetch him some—he said, "I have got 6 d. left"—he gave my little boy 6 d., and he fetched some tea and sugar—I made him some toast—he had his tea, and went to bed—on the following morning, (Monday,) before he got up, Mrs. Jarvis came, I think, about eight o'clock, or a little after—she came to my door and asked if Richard was up—I said, "No"—I opened the door, and she called, "Richard"—if the front door is open you can hear any body
speak at the door in his bed-room—she said, "Make haste and get up, it is breakfast time"—she then went out—she did not go into his room—he got up directly, and said, "I must make haste, if not I shall be too late for my breakfast," and he went out—that was the last I saw of him that day—I was ill that night, and went to bed before eight o'clock—my husband went to bed at the same time.
Q. Are you able to say at what time the prisoner came home? A. Yes, I think it was past two o'clock, I am quite sure—when he came in I said, "Richard, it is morning"—he said, "It is early"—he then went into his bed room—he did not fasten the door—he had no fastening inside—on the Tuesday morning he got up at half-past seven o'clock—I had been up ever since six o'clock, and was getting my husband's breakfast—I bad not gone to sleep after the prisoner came in—I was weaning my baby, and did not go to sleep any more—my baby was so restless, I got out of bed three times—it was soon daylight after he came home—it was moonlight when he came home, it was very light—I was up three times after be came in, before six o'clock, and I did not go to sleep—I began weaning my baby that day and she was very ill—I watched the morning, and I know it was towards morning when he came in—I am quite sure it was near three o'clock—we have no clock in the house—I am quite sure it was nearer three than two o'clock, but I say two o'clock.
COURT. Q. How came you to say between one and two o'clock before? A. I said "from one o'clock"—I was sure it was quite towards morning, because it was so soon daylight.
Q. That is no answer to why you should say between one and two o'clock? A. I cannot speak nearer than I have—I know it was soon morning, and I did not go to sleep after he came in—it was very bright moonlight when he came in.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You say you were in the room at the time the prisoner came into it in the morning? A. Yes, in my own room—he went immediately through my room into the wash-house—there is a privy connected with the wash-house—he went into the privy, for I heard the door, and the door makes a noise—about ten minutes or a quarter of an hour afterwards I went into the wash-house, and saw him standing by the table doing something to his trowsers—I did not stand long enough to notice what he was doing—I turned away, and went into my own room—it was to the top part of his trowsers that he was doing something—he was not stooping, but standing upright—I cannot say whether he was wiping or rubbing them, or what he was doing—I did not pay attention—I do not think he was buttoning them—he did not appear buttoning them—I did not stop long enough to notice whether the privy-door was shut—he was quite out of the privy, standing by the window in the wash-house—I then returned into the sitting-room—I suppose it was ten minutes, or not quite so long, before he came in—I did not pay any attention—he asked for a towel when he came in, which I gave him, and he wiped his face with it—it might be twenty minutes from the time he went into the wash-house till he came out, or it might be longer—it might be half an hour—I heard the noise of the privy-door once, when he went in—my husband was going to send me again, as he wanted something out of the wash-house, but I said, "No, I won't go in, for Richard is doing something to his trowsers, and I did not go—I do not know how long he remained in the privy—when he came out he bid me good morning, and went out—I did not pay any attention to his shoes at that time—he returned at a quarter-past seven o'clock in the
evening—I had in the mean time heard of the death of Mr. Templeman—my husband had gone out when the prisoner returned—I said, "It is well you have come in so soon to-night, Richard, I would not have left my door open any more, in consequence of the murder and robbery near me"—upon which he said, "I have just heard something about it at the Rainbow"—he had just called at Rainbow—I asked him how it was he had not heard of it before—in a few minutes after that my husband came in, and he stood by the table—he came in as I was speaking to Richard, and the conversation dropped—after my husband came in he went through my room to the privy—that might be a minute after my husband came in—it might be two, I cannot say—when he came back he said, "I don't feel very well, I suppose I am horror-struck like the rest"—he then asked my husband to fetch him a drop of beer, and he fetched him some beer, a rasher of bacon, and tobacco—while my husband was gone for the beer, I said, "This is a shocking affair, Richard"—he said, "It is; I know very little about it"—I asked him where he had been that day—he told me he bad been and called on his aunt, or seen his aunt, I do not know which, and he had had good luck, and the old lady was glad to see him—and he had called on his cousin, and his cousin had a pair of shoes that were too large for him, and he had given them to him—my husband came in during the latter part of this conversation with the beer and things he had been sent out for—I put the rasher on the fire, and broiled it for the prisoner, and he ate it—we then went on talking together—I asked him where he had been over-night—he said he bad been to the Rainbow public-house, that be went in with a pint, and came out with a pint—I said, "You could not have been there till that time in the morning"—he said, "I was gossipping"—that is all he said—there was other conversation—I do not know of any thing particular—he said, in speaking of his situation, "Many poor creatures like me would rather be hung than transported"—he was speaking of the difficulties he was in, that he was out of work, and in trouble, and a good deal of conversation to that effect—he had often said he had never known so much trouble as he had done this winter—I saw the stocking in which the money was found in the privy.
Q. Do know to whom that stocking belonged? A. I saw a pair of stockings rolled up on a box, at the side of the prisoner's bed on Monday afternoon, at the time I made the bed—I cannot say whether it was five o'clock, or what time—it was in the afternoon—it might be five o'clock—it was while the prisoner was out—I took hold of the stockings and laid them down, and thought to myself, they are Richard's stockings—I knew them to be similar to what he always wore—I washed for him—they were exactly like what he wore, and did not belong to me—they belonged to Richard—they did not belong to me, and are like what I used to wash for him—I believe them to be his—he had two pain of stockings when I washed for him, before he went to Mr. Bartlett's—I washed for him three weeks after he went there—I had washed a pair of stockings similar to those for him, just before Christmas—that is all I know—my children's clothes were not kept in that room—nobody's clothes but the prisoner's would be kept in that room—they were two odd stockings, I had mismatched them when I washed them, I think it must have been six week before Christmas when I mis-matched them—these were formerly two pairs, and I put one stocking of one pair to another.
COURT. Q. But he could have rightly matched them afterwards? A.
He never did while I washed for him, because they came off together, and were washed together.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you remember his coming home wet-footed one day? A. When he was out with baked potatoes he came home wet in the feet, and I gave him a dirty stocking to pot on, a dry one—that is the way they became mismatched—directly I saw the stocking in which the money was found, I said that was the one I had seen—I knew it belonged to Richard, and it was on the box on the Monday—I am quite sure of that—I am quite sure it was the same stocking I had seen by the side of the bed on Monday.
Cross-examined. Q. Who washed for him when he returned to lodge with you for the week? A. I do not know—his dirty things were not sent to the wash—I never saw any in the house—they were not pot up in a bundle—none of his things were put up in a bundle—I never said any thing about a bundle—I never said I opened a bundle, to ascertain whether they were two stockings, or odd ones—I have no recollection of saying any thing about the stockings being in a bundle—they laid folded together on the box, one turned over the other, rolled up, and the one produced to me was the one which laid outside—they were rolled up in a bundle, the same as a clean pair of stockings—I did not unrol the bundle—they were rolled up in the ordinary form of a pair of stockings, one doubled over the other—the one the money was produced in was the outer stocking—I did not unrol them at all on the Monday—I took them in my hand—I do not know who washed for him then—there was no mark on the stockings—I do not think the prisoner bad been out of place above a fortnight when he came to lodge with us the last time—he came and had a night's lodging now and then—I do not know how long he had left Mr. Bartlett, of the Barnsbury Castle public-house, when he came the last week—when he was in employment he paid me 2 s. a week—we never made any agreement this time—I knew he was out of emyloyment and never asked him for any thing—if he had got any employment I should have expected the same weekly sum—he did not board with us—it was the Sunday morning after the Saturday that he came that Mrs. Jarvis brought him some bacon and bread for his breakfast—it was on the Monday morning she came and called him to come to breakfast—he was in bed, and she was outside the house—it was after eight o'clock, it might be ten minutes after eight—he owed us some money for his former lodging—he often, asked me for an account, but as he had no money I never gave it him—I never cast it up—it might be 1 l. or more, if I east it up, because I often let him have his dinner, and any thing of that sort—he told me, several times during the week, that he had no money—he told me he was in great difficulties, and was without money—many times he mentioned the subject.
Q. You have ventured to say now that it was past two o'clock, were you always of that opinion? A. Yes, always of opinion that it was towards morning—I was always of opinion it was past two o'clock when he came in—I am quite sure it was after two o'clock, according to the time it was daylight afterwards—to the best of my belief it was after two o'clock—I cannot say whether I was certain of the time when I went to the police-office, as I was very much flurried and ill.
Q. Did you not believe, when at the police-office, that it was between one and two o'clock? A. Certainly it must have been two o'clock from
the time it was light—I think it was two o'clock—I believed it was two o'clock, because it got so soon daylight, but I had no clock.
Q. Since you have been at the police-office, have you conversed with your husband about the time he came home? A. My husband said, when he came home, "Mary, Richard did not come home till morning"—I believe my husband did not speak when the prisoner came in, because I spoke—I did not ask my husband whether he was awake, nor did I speak—I was out of bed three times to nurse my child—the prisoner got up between seven and eight o'clock next morning, and went through the wash-house to the privy—there is a catch to the privy-door—it is what I term a night-bolt—it shuts and bolts—there is another entrance to it from the garden—if the door is shut you must go through the wash-house—if the door is open you can come in that way—the bolt is in the wash-house—it is the wash-house-door bolt—the privy-door was off its hinges at that time—the privy joins the wash-house, and when in the privy you can go out of the privy into the garden—you cannot get into the wash-house without the door is left open—you can get to the privy, through the wash-house, without going into the garden—the privy has two doors—the door leading to the garden was off its hinges at that time—I heard of Mr. Templeman's death, I think, at twelve o'clock in the day—I had been out—my husband went up, and he told me all the particulars when he came home, of the old gentleman being murdered—I cannot remember whether be told me any thing about the state he was found in on the floor—I knew all about it, and began to talk to the prisoner about it before my husband came in—when he came in he broke upon the conversation.
Q. Was his going for the bacon and beer the only occasion when your husband went out that night? A. Yes—with the exception of that interval he heard every thing that took place—when my husband said Richard had got a new pair of shoes, I said, "I think not, I think he has blacked and shined his old ones"—he said so to me the moment Richard walked across the room, while he was gone to the privy—when he came back from the privy he asked my husband to fetch him a drop of beer, and I believe he went out to fetch it—I think he was eating his supper.
COURT. Q. When did your husband remark about his having new shoes? A. When he came in, he walked through the room, and went into the privy before he sat down, when he came in in the evening.
Q. I thought your husband was out when he came in? A. My husband was out, but he had stood talking to me by the table, and had not sat down when my husband came in.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Was it in consequence of your husband saying he had new shoes that he said he got them from his cousin? A. I do not know, but he said that—I did not ask him about the shoes—my husband looked at them, and so did I—I never asked where he got them—he said he had been very lucky that day.
Q. Was it not after that, he talked so despondingly as you say about being such an unlucky man? A. It was during the conversation.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You did not unrol the stockings by the bedside? A. No, I merely took them in my hand—when they brought me the stocking in, I said, "There was a pair of stockings on the box on Monday"—I am quite sure that was the outside stocking the money was tied in, and I am quite sure, by the feel of the stockings, that there was more than one.
COURT. Q. How could you tell there were two odd ones? A. I said to the policeman, "There is a pair of stockings"—the policeman found the other slocking in my room, behind the box—they were two odd stockings, and I said I had mis-matched them when I washed them.
JOHN FRIMLEY . I keep the Rainbow public-house, in the Liverpool-road, Islington. I know the prisoner—he was at my house on the 16th and 17th of March—on Monday, the 16th, he was there most part of the afternoon, and until twenty minutes before twelve o'clock at night—I did not see him come in in the afternoon—I saw him first about four o'clock—he was playing at skittles—he afterwards came into the tap-room—about a little after ten o'clock the gas was turned out in the skittle-ground, and he came into the tap-room—he remained there till twenty minutes before twelve o'clock, and then went away in company with the others that were there—there were five or six people, some of them witnesses—King and his wife, and Pizey, were there—on the following day, Tuesday, I saw him at my house between two and three o'clock in the afternoon—I saw him last at seven o'clock—he was then in the tap-room—I believe he was there after seven, but I last saw him at seven—my house is about half a mile from Allen's, and nearly the same distance from Mr. Templeman's cottage.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose you generally shut up about twelve o'clock? A. Yes—he and his party went away in consequence of my shutting up.
ROBERT KING . I am a chimney-sweeper, and live at No. 8, Felix-street, Islington—I was at Frimley's skittle-ground on Monday evening, the 16th of March—the prisoner was playing there—he began playing about half-past seven or eight o'clock—he said, when he first came into the ground, that he only had three halfpence in his pocket, and if either of as would bet odd or even, he might have enough for a pint, but nobody betted with him—he then played at skittles for a pot of beer, I believe—he had some beer to drink—I think it was very near eleven o'clock when we came out of there—I know he did not lose any thing, because I was in the ground all the time—he did not win any money—he won beer—he did not say any thing after playing that I heard—I went into the tap-room with him afterwards—when he first came into the ground he said he had three halfpence, and as he did not lose it he came into the tap-room with it—he bought a candle for a 1 d.—I did not see him buy it.
Cross-examined. Q. How much is a pot of beer? A. 4 d.—It is 5 d. in the skittle-ground.
ROBERT PIZEY . I live at No. 17, Elder-walk. I was in the skittle-ground of the Rainbow public-house on Monday, the 16th of March, and saw the prisoner there—I afterwards went into the tap-room—I was going out for some tobacco, and when I was going out the prisoner asked me to fetch him a rush light—he gave me two halfpence—I bought one, and gave it to the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you play with him at skittles? A. I had a game with him—he had not his coat off when he played—when we were shut out of the ground we went up to the tap-room.
MARY ELIZABETH KING . I am the wife of Robert King. I was at the Rainbow public-house on the 16th of March—I went there about twenty minutes to twelve o'clock, to fetch my husband—I saw the prisoner there—I remember the gas being turned off—the people did not all come out
together—I went out first—they came about five minutes after—I observed the prisoner particularly—he had on a fustian coat, the same he has on now—I noticed it because I saw something in his right-hand pocket—it was long, and thick at one end—it was in the outside side-pocket, like a shooting-jacket pocket—there were two pockets, one on each side, rather in front—no part of what he had was uncovered—it must have been about a quarter to twelve o'clock when I noticed it—it was at the time he was going away—I did not see Pizey go for the candle—the instrument was the length of his pocket—it was lying long ways in his pocket, across the bottom of his pocket—the ends seemed to be different—I saw this in his turning his back to the fire-place—after we came out, I and my husband went one way, and the prisoner went along towards the Chapel of Ease—that is in the direction of Mr. Templeman's cottage—it was before twelve o'clock.
Cross-examined. Q. How many of you were there came out of the tap-room together? A. Mr. and Mrs. Shepherd, Pizey, the prisoner, my husband, and myself—Mr. and Mrs. Shepherd went home—they lodge at our house—we all went home together, only they got home first—I have always said I knew which way the prisoner went—I have not said, I did not recollect which way he went.
JAMES MILLER . I am an inspector of the N division of police. I apprehended the prisoner at Allen's cottage, on Tuesday night, the 17th of March, at a quarter after eleven o'clock—I found him in the bed-room, in bed with two children—I said, "Gould, Gould"—he appeared to arouse from sleep, and said, "What, what?"—I said nothing more at that moment—a light was procured, and I again roused him, and said, "Gould, get up and dress yourself—he appeared still drowsy—I told him to get up—he then got out of bed, and on getting out of bed, he said, "Where is your warrant?" I replied, "I don't need a warrant"—he then said, "What do you want me for then?"—I said, "I suppose you have beard of the murder?—it is that I want you for"—he smiled, and said, "If I was as innocent of every thing as I am of that, I should not have much to fear"—I do not recollect his saying any thing more then—as he was packing up his clothes to dress himself, I found 9 s. in his trowsers' pockets, all in shillings—while I was counting it, I asked what money he had, and he said 9 s.—in his waist-coat-pocket I found seven lucifer matches, and two duplicates for a pair of trowsers and a jacket, dated, "22nd February, 1340, 2 s. 6 d.;" and "25th February, 1840, 35."—one in the name of Richard Gould, and the other in the name of Ann Jarvis—on the way to the station he said that a pot-boy at the William public-house had taken a warrant out against him for an assault, and it was that he supposed I was after him for—I had missed him for some time, and asked where he had been—he said, "To Norwich"—I searched him at the station-house, and took off his feet a pair of new shoes, or ankle-boots, which I produce—I asked him where he bought them—he said, in Kent-street—a constable who was standing by, said, "The Borough?"—the prisoner said, "Yes"—I then asked him what he had done with his old ones—he replied, "Thrown them away"—shortly after that, I minutely examined his clothes, and took from him this waist-coat, which he was wearing at the time—he had three waistcoats on—two besides this—this was the middle one—this waistcoat I noticed at the time was stained with blood, and it is also burnt in one particular spot, as if a cinder or a pipe had fallen on it—on my saying that the waistcoat
was stained with blood, the prisoner said his clothes had often been stained with blood—I afterwards went to Allen's cottage that same morning.
COURT. Q. Have you had the waistcoat examined in the same way as a bag was? A. No—the bag was examined, and discovered that what was upon it was not blood—this has not been examined in the same way.
MR. JONES. Q. Did you search the bed-room in which you apprehended the prisoner? A. I did, and found this single stocking there—it is a fine stocking—it was lying on a chair by the side of a box—it was dirty—the prisoner bad stockings on when he dressed himself—I have examined them since—they were a pair, I believe—I did not take them off—perhaps I did not take notice enough to say whether they were a pair or not—I judged they were a pair—I did not show that stocking to Mrs. Allen—I did not search any other part of Allen's premises, except the bed-room, where I took the prisoner—I found the stocking about a quarter after eight o'clock on the Wednesday morning.
Cross-examined. Q. You went there about a Quarter past eleven o'clock? A. I did—Allen opened the door to me—we were not more than a minute before we got in—Allen had no light with him—Allen sleeps in the room I first entered—I knocked at the door—he answered me from the inside, from his bed—he got out of bed in his shirt to let me in—after I had got into the prisoner's room, Allen got a light—I remained there about ten minutes—the prisoner put on all the three waistcoats—he put on every thing he had, to carry it off on his back—I opened his bed-room-door myself—I found two little children there—they were awoke by the noise—they both appeared aroused, but did not seem to know what was going on—he was sleeping between the two children—I knew there was an assault-warrant against him—I know that he occasionally fought—I have heard that the assault-warrant arose from a fight.
MR. JONES. Q. Did you observe how the prisoner's door was fastened? A. It was bolted outside—I saw the deceased while he was lying dead—I took the rope from his wrist—this is it—( producing it)—the noose was put over the right hand, and the other end tied to it—there was no knot tied at all—the hands were firmly bound.
COURT. Q. In your judgment, could that he done without two people? A. I think one person might do it, though the other was struggling, particularly if he was an old and feeble man.
RICHARD BRADSHAW . I am a policeman of the N division. I went to Allen's cottage on Wednesday with sergeant King, and made search of the premises—I saw King put his hand over the partition parting the privy from an out-house or wash-house, and find a stocking—it laid between the rafters and tiles of the wash-house—he stood on the seat of the privy, and reached it down—it was put over the wash-house roof—I did not see what the stocking contained—King took it away with him.
THOMAS HOBBS KING . I am a sergeant of the N division. I accompanied Bradshaw to Allen's cottage on Wednesday morning—I searched the premises, and found a stocking between the ceiling of the back-kitchen and the roof or tiles of the privy and back kitchen—I had to lean over, and put my arm round behind the rafter—seeing something white, I stood on the seat of the privy to reach it—it was by the first rafter belonging to the wash-house, placed round the corner—it it full five feet from the seat of the privy to the rafter—I should not think a short person could have put it there, standing on the seat—a person as tall as the prisoner could,
or as tall as Allen—I examined the stocking—it contained nineteen half-crowns, forty-eight shillings, and seven sixpences, making 4 l. 19 s. together.
Cross-examined. Q. When was it you went? A. About seven o'clock on Wednesday morning—I believe all that money has been examined very minutely, to find a particular marked shilling, but it could not be found.
COURT. Q. What marked shilling were you looking for? A. Mrs. Morgan or Mrs. Lovett stated that she had a particular shilling in her possession marked S, I think, but she did not know whether she paid it away before she gave the deceased the money or not—there is a marked half-crown, which cannot be spoken to—all the money in my possession has been very closely examined.
MR. JONES to MART ALLEN. Q. Look at that stocking, and see whether or not you know it? A. This is the stocking that was folded outside—there is no mark on it that I know it by—here is the other stocking—( looking at one)—this is exactly the same sort of top—that I mis-matched for the prisoner when I washed for him—he had a pair of them—I washed for him six months, but not during the last time he was there—there is nothing on either of the stockings to enable me to identify them—it is merely being alike.
COURT. Q. There is a difference in the length? A. I had footed them for him, and I may have taken them up then.
MR. JONES. Q. When did you foot them for him? A. When I washed for him—I cut the feet off, and put fresh feet on—I did not knit fresh feet, I cut an old stocking up, and put them—this one has been footed—I cannot say it is my work, but I footed stockings like these—they have been both done—I cannot tell my own work.
Cross-examined. Q. You know who washes for him now, do not you? A. He never told me—he took the washing away—I never saw him take his things from my house, and I never saw him put any on in my house—I asked him the last time, "Richard, don't you want your things washed? "—he said, "No, I have got somebody else to wash for me"—I guess it is Mrs. Jarvis—I do not know where Mrs. Jarvis is—I never saw her, only when she came to my house for Richard, nor did I know where she lived before the murder took place—I did not know whether she lived in Great Bride-street or the Fields, I knew it was in the neighbourhood.
JOHN ELLIS . I am a painter, and live at No. 11, Madras-place, Hollo way On Tuesday, the 17th of March, I met the prisoner about a quarter after two o'clock, as near as I can guess—I had seen Mrs. Jarvis before that, at nine o'clock in the morning, near Prospect-cottages, Thornhill-road, at the top of John-street—she was going towards Islington turn-pike—that is the road to town, you can go that way to it—when I met the prisoner about two o'clock Mrs. Jarvis was in his company—she had a child with her both in the morning and at two o'clock—I had not heard of the murder of Mr. Templeman when I saw her in the morning—I heard of it at one o'clock—when I met them at two o'clock I asked Mrs. Jarvis if she had been at home—she said, "No"—I said, "Well, there is a little bit of a stir at your village," (it is about a quarter of a mile from where I met them—they were going towards the cottages—I was going to work from the cottages, and they met me)—she said, "Is there?"—I said, "Yes," and then hesitated for a moment—I then said, the old gentleman,
I had heard, had been murdered—she said, "What old gentleman, do you mean, Mr. Templeman?"—I said, "Yes"—she said, "Then I will go home and see"—she then turned to the prisoner, and said, "Richard, or Dick, you had better not go home with me now then"—"Yes," said he, "I shall go and see what is up"—they then turned, and went on—I did not see how far they went—that was all the conversation that passed between us.
RICHARD BRADSHAW re-examined. My attention was directed to Jarvis's cottage on the Tuesday. I watched it from about two o'clock till eight o'clock at night on the 17th—I saw Mrs. Jams about a quarter before three o'clock at the entrance of the cottage cutting some wood to light the fire—she had a child with her.
THOMAS BOTTOM . I am a mathematical instrument maker. On Tuesday, the 17th of March, between half-past twelve and a quarter to one o'clock, I went into the tap-room of the General Wolfe public-house, Little Gray's-inn-lane—when I went in the prisoner was there smoking a short pipe and drinking—he had something in a pewter pot, I cannot say what—while I was there another man came in, who I since know to be Marsh—a person tapped at the tap-room window, the next window to me, where the prisoner was sitting—the prisoner beckoned the person in, and a female came in with a child in her arms—she was a young woman—she sat down in the room—she shortly afterwards went out, and the prisoner went two or three minutes after—it was about two o'clock when he went away, a minute or two before or after.
COURT. Q. Did the people speak when they were in the room? A. No.
RALPH WILCOXON . I am a boot and shoe maker, and live at No. 93, Tottenham-court-road—I consider this pair of boots produced by Miller, were purchased at one of my shops—they are marked 92 and 8, which is a private mark—the 8 is the size, the 92 is the private mark—I have seven shops, one in Howland-street, another in Tottenham-court-road, two in Oxford-street, one in Regent-street, one in Walker's-court, Berwick-street, Soho, and one in King William-street, London-bridge—I live in Tottenham-court-road—I was there on the 17th of March—my daughter resides there—she is occasionally in the shop when I am up-stairs.
Q. Now do you remember on the 17th of March, your daughter bringing you a half-sovereign? A. To the best of my recollection she brought me a half-sovereign to take 7 s. 6 d. out of it—to the best of ray recollection it was between twelve and one o'clock in the day—the price of these boots' is about 7 s. 6 d.—that is about the selling price—we might sell them for 7 s.—I was in the Howland-street shop, up-stairs, when my daughter brought the half-sovereign to me—that and the Tottenham-court-road shop communicate—it is half a mile from there to Gray's-inn-lane, or perhaps three-quarters—7 s. 6 d. would be the price we should ask for the boots—92 means the cost price—if I was selling the boots I should ask 7 s. 6 d.
Cross-examined. Q. Who marks the cost price? A. Sometimes one, sometimes another—all of my shop men know the mark—when my shop-men leave my service, they know it just as well, and I now know that they use it as well—I did not before—I only know one who uses my mark, that is Mr. Wright, who has a shop in Lambeth-walk, where I sold him the business and the stock; and I believe he has another shop at the Marsh-gate, Westminster-road—I now know he uses the same mark as
me—I have, I should say, 40,000 pairs of shoes in a year—I have now a stock of 40,000 for my different shops—the shoes are all made in the country, and Wright deals with the same manufacturer, but I did not know that till to-day—I have brought Mr. Wright here with me.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you know any thing about his goods being marked the same? A. Yes—I went into the shop and saw his mark.
HANNAH WILCOXON . I am the daughter of the last witness—I was in the shop in Tottenham-court-road on the 17th of March—I received a half-sovereign that day from Mr. Wills, my father's shopman—I took it up to my father to change—I did not see the half-sovereign given to Wills.
Cross-examined. Q. Was Wills serving in the shop at the time he gave you the half-sovereign? A. Yes—I do not know how many customers there were.
SAMUEL WILLS . I am foreman to Mr. Wilcoxon in his shop in Tottenham-court-road.
Q. Do you remember on Tuesday, the 17th of March last, any boots or shoes being bought at that shop? A. I cannot charge my memory with the article—the 1 s. 6 d. taken to him was taken by me of a customer, I do not recollect who, or for what article.
Q. Did you receive a half-sovereign in the course of that day? A. I can only state that from the evidence of Mr. Wilcoxson I must have taken it—I cannot trace it from my own recollection—I was in the habit of making entries in the day book of goods sold—I have the book here—( producing it)—but it merely mentions the price—I cannot tell who I received it from—it appears from the book that I received 7 s. 6 d., not a half-sovereign.
JOHN WALTER . I serve in Mr. Wilcoxon's shop in Tottenham-court-road, and am in the habit of marking shoes—the figure 8 on this boot is not much unlike my 8—I cannot say any thing about the 92—I would not be positive the 8 is my figure—I see no mark on the other shoe—it is usual only to mark one of a pair—we may mark two by mistake—I remember seeing a half-sovereign on the counter on the 17th of March—Wills the foreman had it—I did not see who he received it from—he gave it to Miss Wilcoxon, and she took it up-stairs to her father—I do not know whether she brought any thing down.
HENRY WILLIAM SMITH , clerk to Messrs. Horton, boiler and gas-meter manufacturers, Wharf-road, City-road bason, gave the prisoner a good character.
NOT GUILTY .
NEW COURT.— Tuesday, April 14 th, 1840.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18400406-1281a]
1281. THOMAS CHAPPLE and CHARLES KING were indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Porter, on the 7th of September, at St. Giles-in-the-Fields, and stealing therein, 14 spoons, value 12 l.; 1 ladle, value 5 l.; 3 rings, value 3 l.; one watch, value 5 l.; 1 seal, value 2 l.; one chain, value 1 l.; 1 bag, value 2 s.; 1 coat, value 5 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 2 s.; 8 sovereigns, and 3 100 l. and 12 10 l. Bank-notes; his property: and CHARLES CHAPPLE , ELIZA PLANT , ANN KING , HENRY COX , and SOPHIA COX , for feloniously harbouring the said prisoners, well knowing them to have committed the felony aforesaid.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN PORTER . I am a solicitor, and carry on business in Lincoln's Innfields, in the parish of St. Giles-in-the-Fields—I occupy the ground floor for offices—my servant sleeps on the premises, and takes care of the house—I let the other part out in chambers. On the 7th of September last, the prisoner Thomas Chapple was acting as porter to my establishment, and his wife Sophia was the laundress—they slept on the premises, and had the charge of them when the gentlemen had left—the front room on the ground floor I occupy as my own private room—that communicates with the back room, which is the office, by means of double doors—one of them is covered with red cloth—there is a side door also from my room leading to the passage—that is within the hall doors—as you go in there are the hall doors then, a side door on the left hand to go into my private room, and then there are double doors from my room into the back room or office—it is usual to keep the hall door fast during the day, because the street door is generally open during office hours—the hall doors are fastened by a spring lock, which cannot be opened on the outside without a key—it is generally opened by means of a pulley in the office—my side door it secured by a spring lock that cannot be opened from without except by a key—no one can get to my office by the side door, unless they have a key or it is opened from within—there are two keys of the spring lock which is on the side door—one of them I kept myself, and the other was kept by Thomas Chapple, with strict orders not to admit any one but himself and his wife, to clean the room—I did not allow even the clerks in my business to go in there—on Saturday, the 7th of September I went up-stairs to dinner between twenty minutes to five and five o'clock—I should rather incline to think it was about ten minutes to five o'clock—I dined in my drawing-room, which is the room over the ground floor—before I went up I bolted the double doors leading from my room to the office, according to my usual practice—I went out at my side door and drew the door to after me, which fastened it so that it could not be got at but by means of a key—Thomas Chapple waited on me at dinner till about half-past five—I staid up-stairs altogether about an hour and a quarter—I came down about ten minutes or a quarter past six o'clock—I took the key of my room out of my waist-coat pocket, put it in the lock, drew back the lock as usual, and went in—immediately on entering, my attention was called to the door of my iron safe, which was standing wide open—I have a writing table in the middle of the room—I passed by that on one side, and saw several keys, some of which I have since understood to be skeleton keys, lying on a bundle of papers on the writing table, and a small piece of iron—I found the
