OLD COURT.— Tuesday, April 14 th, 1840.
Third Jury, Before Mr. Baron Alderson.
[trialAccount t18400406-1285]
1285. CATHERINE MICHAEL was indicted for the wilful murder of George Michael: she was also charged on the Coroner's inquisition with the like offence.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
SARAH STEVENS . I am the wife of john stevens, and live at No. 22, Praed-street, Paddington. I know the prisoner—I received her child into my care nearly nine months before its death—it was a fortnight and a few days old when it came to me—it died on the 31st of March, or Wednesday morning, the 1st of April—it was very well on the Friday before its death.
Q. Had it been poorly before that time? A. Not lately—on the Friday before it died, in consequence of a letter I received, I went to the prisoner, who was living at Mrs. Kelly's, No. 49, Hunter-street, Brunswick-square, as wet nurse—I saw the servant first—the prisoner came down stairs to me, and asked me how I was, and how the child was—(the child's name was George Michael)—I said the child was very well—she asked me to walk into the parlour, which I did, and she said she had an old frock for the baby, and a bottle of medicine—she gave me a bottle which was full—this looks like the bottle—( looking at one)—it was tied down with a bit or paper inside the cork, and a piece of white paper outside round the bottle, and sealed with wax—the paper was not fastened on the bottle—the cork was tied round—the paper was wrapped round the bottle—I could not see what was in it till I got home—when she gave it me she said it would do the baby's bowels good—I said the baby was very well, and did not want medicine—she said it had done her mistress's baby good, and it would do her baby good—she said I was to give it one tea-spoon full every night—
she said if any thing happened to the child I waft to let her knew directly—I do not recollect that she said any thing more—she said I might expect a doctor on Monday next, about five o'clock—I rather think she laid Dr. Reid, but I am not certain of the name—this was on Friday, the 27th of March. The prisoner paid me for taking, care of the child—she gave me 7 s. a week for the first five months, I believe, and then I spoke about falling it to 6 s. a week—I lowered it to 6 s. a week for one month, and then I fell it to 5 s.—that waft the rate when the child died—the doctor did not call on the Monday—when I got home I took both papers off the bottle—I did not open the cork—the contents looked dark, something of a treacle colour—I never took out the cork—I took off the paper directly, to see if there were any directions—I never gave the child any of the contents of the bottle—I stood it on the mantelpiece directly, where it continued till the Tuesday, not intending to give it to it at all—I keep the house, but other people lodge in it—on the Tuesday afternoon I left the room at half-past four o'clock—there was myself, my daughter, who is eleven years old, the baby, my little William, who is five years old, and Richard, my son, who is three years old, in the room up to that time—those were all the persona in the room—about half-past four o'clock I was called out of the room—the baby was very well indeed then—all the persons I have named were in the room then—I could not say whether the bottle was on the mantel-piece at that time, but it had been there all that day—I had cleared the mantel-piece about eleven o'clock, and it was there then—I did not remove it afterwards, nor any body else to the best of my knowledge—when I saw it at eleven o'clock it was in the same state, as to the contents, as when I received it—I kept it for fear the doctor should come—I thought, in consequence of what she said, a doctor might come, and I meant to ask him how I should give it—when I went out of the room at half-past four o'clock I left the baby sitting on the floor, playing with my children—it was awake and lively at that time—at six o'clock I went into my parlour—I was in the kitchen, and I heard Richard cry—I thought he had fallen, and went up into the parlour—I found the baby then lying on the sofa asleep—I went and took him up in my arms, and his hands Were clenched—I could not open his fingers—I spoke to him, and endeavoured to rouse him—I said, "George, George"—he looked up, and closed his eyes again, aft if he was going to Sleep—I told my girl to go and fetch a pennyworth of castor oil, and gave the child half of it—he then went fast asleep—I looked at the mantel-piece directly I came in—the bottle was on it, and half the stuff gone—I sent my girl for a doctor, and called up a midwife who had been with me at a labour in the house—the medical man who came first was Mr. Sellick, assistant to Mr. Philpot—they looked at the child, and asked me what I had been giving him—I told them I had given him castor oil, but my little boy said something.
Q. Was the attention of Mr. Sellick, ox Mr. Philpot called to the half-empty bottle? A. When I came into the room, I took the bottle in my hand, and smashed the stuff into the fire in my passion—this was after my little boy had made a statement—I went to the bottle to see whether he had done it—I smashed the stuff on the fire Before Mr. Sellick came—there was nothing to show him but the bottle—I had done nothing else to it—I had not rinced it—there was a good many drops in it When they came—I told Mr. Sellick and Mr. Philpot what my little boy had told me
—MR. Sellick took the bottle with him—he brought something and gave the child directly, and ordered hot water and things for it—I gave the things they advised—they ordered it medicine and a hot bath—I did every thing in my power—he seemed in convulsions, but quite asleep—he never awoke up all night—he died about twenty minutes before five on Wednesday morning—I sent Mary Batty for the prisoner about nine or half-past nine o'clock on Tuesday night—she came—I said, "Look what trouble I am in"—she said she could not help of it—I drew her attention to the child—she went and looked at it—I told her what my child had told me—she came and sat down by me, looking, at the child, which was on another person's knee, who lived up-stairs—I said the child was very bad, and told her my little boy had given it the medicine out of the bottle—I asked her if the doctor had given it to her, and she said, "Yes"—I sent for Mr. Philpot while she was there—he asked her where she got it—she said the doctor near there, or something of that sort, I cannot exactly say what.
Q. Did she say where she got it from? A. She said something about round the corner—she stopped about a quarter of an hour—she came again next morning, about ten o'clock—the child had been dead some hours then—I asked her to go into the other room and see it—at first she objected—she never made no notice—she said, "No," but after a little while her cousin, Esther Jones, who lives near me, said, "Go, Kate, and see the child"—she then rose up, and went with me and her cousin into the room—she looked at the child—she said, "It is better off," or something like that—she did not say she was very sorry—MR. Philpot was not sent for that morning—she went out, and said she was going to Mr. Philpot's house—nothing had been said about him—she was absent about a quarter of an hour—MR. Philpot lives about two minutes' walk from me—when she came back, I asked her if she had seen Mr. Philpot—she said, "Yes"—I asked her if it was all right, if she had had the medicine from the doctor—she said, "Yes"—I asked her whether it was doctor Reid—she told me, "Yes"—I did not ask her any thing else—she said nothing else—she stopped from ten till two o'clock that day—I did not see her again till she was brought to our house by a policeman, on the Saturday night—when she gave me the bottle, she told me to give it one teaspoonful of the stuff every night.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How many children have you? A. Eight—I have had eight nurse children before—when they have required medicine I administered it—I am acquainted with the ordinary diseases which children are subject to—I have never given laudanum for diseases of children—I have never known it to be given or seen it given to children in my life—I have seen it given to ladies in drops when I have been with them—I have never given it to ladies—I have had a pennyworth for the tooth-ache, often enough to know the colour of laudanum—I do not think I should know the smell of it—I do not think I have taken it above twice for the tooth-ache—I can swear I do not know the smell of it—I do not think I should know the taste of it, although I have taken it for the tooth-ache—I have just put it with a feather to my tooth, that is all—I could not see the cork of the bottle when it was delivered to me—it was tied down with a white piece of paper outside—there was a little piece of paper inside—I will not be sure whether it was white, or what colour—the seal was outside on the cork, with the white paper—the string was round the neck of the bottle over the white paper inside and not outside—it was coloured paper
inside, but I could not tell what colour, as it was nine o'clock at night—the white paper was fastened with red sealing-wax outside, which I was obliged to break to open the paper—it was sealed on one end, which retained the ends of the outside white paper over the cork—I found a piece of paper tied over the cork inside, folded up very neatly—the child was in very good health when I had it—it had not been otherwise for a month or five weeks—I had complained to its mother about its bowels—the last time was about a month or five weeks before this—she did not give money to get the child medicine then—she did when it was about two months old—she then gave me 5 s. to get medicine, and 5 s. she made me a present of—the was at that time paying me 7 s. a week for nursing the child—I received 10 s. from her on that occasion—she came there and gave it to me—I had complained of the child's state of health then—it had been very ill some time—I bought some stuff from Mr. Philpot's assistant—I took it there two or three times—I paid 10 d. one day, and I think 14 d. another day, that left 3 s. out of the 5 s. but I think I had some powders at the same time, and some castor-oil, in addition to the medicine from Mr. Philpot's shop—I was not served by Mr. Sellick—I bought them myself—I had two pennyworth of castor-oil once or twice, and I was always giving him something at that time—I took him down to Ramsgate for change of air—I did not take any of my own children down to Ramsgate—I paid for it—this was the only occasion on which I received money from the prisoner for medicine—I never received 4 s. from her—I am quite certain that is all the money I received—when I got home I took the paper off the bottle, expecting to find directions how to give the medicine to the child—I put the bottle on the mantel-piece over the fire-place, which is rather low—it is above the fire-place—it is rather a low mantel-piece.
COURT. Q. I suppose your boy could reach the mantel-piece? A. He must stand on a chair to reach it.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was there a fire on this day? A. Yes, and a fender before it—I have frequently seen my child stand on a chair—when I came into the room my little boy was sitting down by the fire-place—the child was lying on the sofa asleep at the time—the child five years old is not here—the prisoner came on the Tuesday night, and also on Wednesday morning—I asked her to go in and see the child—she said, "No"—there can be no mistake at all in that—I am certain she said that, and that only—I am certain she did not say, "I am very sorry"—if she had said so I could not avoid recollecting it—I have not a very good memory—I will undertake to swear what the prisoner said to me when she gave me the bottle—there was nothing at that particular moment to fix it on my mind—I will undertake to say the words without any mistake—she asked bow I was when I came to the door—she said, "Here is an old frock and a bottle of medicine for the baby"—she said, "Give my baby one lea-spoonful every night"—I said he was very well, and did not want medicine—she said the doctor would most likely call on me on Monday, and she thought she had better apprise me of it—she gave no further directions as to taking the medicine.
COURT. Q. What was the doctor to call on you on Monday for? A. To see the baby, about five o'clock, and I waited till the doctor called before I would give the child any thing—I was not waiting for the doctor's directions particularly—I meant to ask him how it was to be taken if he came—I am not sure she did not tell me to ask for the doctor's directions—she said the doctor would most likely come to see the baby at five o'clock.
Q. Are you quite certain she did not say you were to wait for the doctor's directions before you gave it? A. No.
MR. RYLAND. Q. With the exception of the castor oil you gave the the child the night it died, did you ever give it any medicine except under the direction of a medical man? A. Never, except a little powder I got myself—at one time it had other medicine—at one time, when first I had it, she sent me four powders—I could not be certain whether she sent them with the clothes, or whether the nurse at the hospital gave them me—I gave it one of them—the child was very ill at the time, and it did it good.
COURT. Q. At the time she told you to give the child the teaspoon-ful of this every night, did she tell you you were to give it a teaspoonful partly of this medicine and partly of water? A. No—she only said a tea-spoonful every night—I did not give it to the baby because I did not think it wanted medicine—I expected to have directions on the bottle how to give it, whether in water, tea, milk, gruel, or any thing, but I did not think of giving it to the child at all—I never kept medicine on the mantel-piece—I did not know I had poison in the house—she mentioned something about steel medicine to Mr. Philpot, not to me.
JANE STEVENS . I am going on for twelve years of age—I go to church sometimes, and say ray prayers and catechism—I know it is wrong to tell a lie—God knows whether I tell lies or not—he will punish me if I do so when I die—I am afraid of that—( sworn.) I am the daughter of Sarah Stevens—I knew the little baby George—my mother left me in charge of it the afternoon before it died—she left my two brothers and the baby and me in the room—after my mother had been gone out some time I went out for a loaf—I left the baby sitting down in a corner awake and very well, and my two brothers in the room—I was gone about ten minutes—the baby was sitting in the same place when 1 returned—my brother Billy said something to me when I went in, and in consequence of that, a little while after I looked upon the mantel-piece—I bad seen a bottle on the mantel-piece before I went out for the loaf—it was such a bottle as the one produced—I had seen it about a quarter of an hour before I went out for the loaf—it was on the mantel-piece then, and was full then—when I looked at it on the mantel-piece after what Billy said, it was half full—it was in the same place—I took up the baby and it fell on my shoulder and went to sleep—I sat up till about twelve o'clock—it did not awake up to that time.
Cross-examined. Q. Where was Billy when you came in? A. He was just by the baby—he was playing with it, patting his hands—I had not been out more than ten minutes—the baby was just in the corner, just by the mantel-piece, on a little carpet on the floor.
COURT. Q. Billy was very fond of the baby, I suppose? A. Yes—I did not smell Billy's lips, nor the other boy's.
MARY BATTY . I am single—I am a dress-maker, and live in Praed-street, Paddington. I know Mrs. Stevens and her children, and the baby which she had to nurse—on the night of its death I was sent for to Mrs. Stevens's—I went about nine o'clock, and saw the baby—it was very ill—I went for the prisoner by Mrs. Stevens's request—I did not see her first—I did afterwards—MRS. Stevens had told me to ask her to come and see the baby—I told her she must come and see her baby, as it was very ill
indeed—she said she would ask leave to go—she went for the purpose of asking leave, and went with me to Mrs. Stevens's house—I did not go into the room with her.
RICHARD GODFREY SELLICK . I am assistant to Mr. Philpot, a surgeon near Praed-street, Paddington. I was sent for to Mrs. Stevens's on Tuesday evening, the 31st of March—I got there about five o'clock, or it might be a quarter before—I saw an infant there—I examined it—it was perfectly insensible—I smelt its breath, and could perceive the odour of laudanum—this bottle was shown to me—I examined it—there were a few drops more than there is in it now—it is pure laudanum I should say, from the smell and taste of it, and from what I see here—it is such as they sell in the shops for laudanum—the state of the child was such as might be produced by taking laudanum.
Q. What quantity of laudanum administered in that state would produce the effect you found? A. One drop has been known to do it—it is never safe to administer one drop to a child—one drop would not produce the effect I found, but one drop has been known to poison a child—a tea-spoonful would—about sixty drops is an ordinary teaspoonful—that if rather a large dose—this bottle would contain about an ounce—half of it would be about half an ounce—a teaspoonful administered to an infant nine months old, such a baby as I saw, would be certain to destroy life—I gave it some medicine—castor oil would not be likely to do harm nor good unless it acted as an emetic—if it had, it might have done good—mustard and water would have been better—it appeared a hopeless case from my first seeing it—I first saw the prisoner on Wednesday morning—she came to Mr. Philpot when I was there, and said, "My poor little one is dead"—she asked if I was the gentleman who had seen the child—I told her yes—she said she was sorry to lose the child when it had attained that age—she then asked if there was likely to be an inquest—I said in all probability there would be an inquest, and asked her whether she had inquired into the mistake—(I had heard people talking about one medicine having been given for another)—she said yes, she had been to Dr. Reid's, and that Dr. Reid had told her it was his young man's mistake—she said the young man had given it in mistake for another mixture off the counter—I then left her.
Cross-examined. Q. Is the bottle precisely in the same stole in which it was handed to you? A. Yes—it has rather evaporated—there might have been two or three more drops than there is now—there is now very likely one drop—there was about three times as much as there is now—the Coroner tested it—it has been very trifingly diminished by him.
Q. Are you able clearly to say that the mixture which is in this bottle has not been adulterated? A. I cannot say that for certain—if adulterated by water, whatever quantity was given would certainly produce less effect.
Q. If laudanum has been in a pure state in a bottle, and afterwards mixed with water, would the spirit remain, or would it amalgamate? A. It would if shaken together.
FREDERICK PHILPOT . I am a surgeon, and live in Upper Southwicstreet, Paddington. On the evening of the 31st of March I went to Mrs. Stevens's house—I found my assistant there, and saw an infant which I examined—it was in a state of complete insensibility in a woman's lap in the room—it could not be roused—the pupils of the eyes were contracted—I
smelt its breath, but could not detect clearly at that time the odour of laudanum—the child had had two or three emetics given it before I saw it, but they had not operated—the stomach was insensible to all emetics—I attribute the state the child was in to a strong narcotic—laudanum would produce the effect I saw—every thing was done that could be done for it—I was shown this bottle, and examined it—there was a small quantity of laudanum in it—there was enough to enable me to form a judgment of it—I am convinced it is laudanum—one or two drops would be sufficient to destroy the life of a child in health—a tea spoonful would be quite sufficient—I afterwards made a post mortem examination—I found all the symptoms of poison by laudanum, by opium—I attribute its death to opium, laudanum—I have not the least hesitation in saying the child died by taking laudanum, from the symptoms during life, and the appearances after death—the prisoner came to the house in the course of the evening—I saw her—she said she was the mother of the child—I told her the child was labouring under the effects of laudanum—she asked me if I thought it would recover—I told her I thought it could not even live many hours—I then asked her respecting the medicine, and who had tent it to her—after some hesitation she told me that Mr. Reid, of Bloomsbury-square, had sent it to her—I asked her for what purpose Mr. Reid had sent the medicine, and if she knew the nature of it—she stated that it was some preparation of steel, and was intended to remove some offensive discharges from the bowels—I merely expressed my surprise that a medical man should send out that quantity of laudanum without a label, and shortly after left, leaving her there—about ten or between ten and eleven o'clock next morning, she came to our house, and told me her little boy was dead—I said it was an unfortunate occurrence—she said she had seen Mr. Reid—(she had stated on the over night that as the went home she would call on Mr. Reid to inquire into the mistake)—I asked her how he accounted to her for the mistake—she stated that Mr. Reid regretted the circumstance, and that he said it was a mistake of his young man's; that the laudanum was intended for another patient of his, a lady who was in the habit of taking it, and could not sleep at night—she owned then that it was laudanum—she expressed her regret at losing the child, after having reared it to that age at her own expense'—I asked her if her mistress was aware of the circumstance, I thought she had better acquaint her mistress with the fact, as in all probability there would be an inquest, and her attendance would be required.
JAMES REID . I am a physician. I live in Bloomsbury-square—I know the prisoner—she was confined in Queen Charlotte's lying-in hospital last year—I procured her the situation of wet nurse in the family of Mr. Kelly—I never furnished her with any medicine for her child in March—none was furnished by my house to my knowledge—she did not call at my house on Tuesday evening the 31st of March to my knowledge—I did not see her at all—on Wednesday evening the 1st of April, a little after five o'clock, I found her waiting in my room, and asked if she had come with a message from Mrs. Kelly, who is a patient of mine—she said she did not, that she had a favour to ask me—(I had not then heard of the death of the child)—as she hesitated very much I inquired what it was—she then said that her child was dead, that a coroner's inquest would likely be held upon it, and that she wished me particularly to attend the inquest—I inquired if the child had met with an accident—she said it had—I mentioned
that as I was not conversant with the facts of the case, my attendance would be of no use, but I would attend if I was summoned—she then hesitated good deal, and stated that it was not at her own request exactly that she wished me to attend, that the child had had laudanum given to it by a child in mistake, and that the medical man who had seen the child said it would be necessary for me to attend—she made use of the expression, "If I am hanged for it, I could not support the child on my wages"—I then asked her the circumstances of the accident—she said she bad sent some laudanum in a bottle to be given to the child, and that it had neither label nor direction upon it—recollecting she had mentioned that a child had given it laudanum, I said to her, "But of course you directed what quantity should be given, or was to be given"—she replied that she had directed a teaspoonful to be given at night—I then told her I was exceedingly sorry I had asked her the last question, and still more sorry that she had answered it, that if I was summoned on the coroner's inquest I should be on my oath, and should have to repeat the whole conversation—I advised her to say no more to me on the subject—I felt it my duty to communicate what I had learnt to Mr. Kelly, and she was dismissed from their service—she came to me on the Friday to inform me that she had been dismissed by Mr. Kelly, and to ask me if I had heard any thing about the coroner's inquest, and that, if there was to be none, would I intercede with Mr. Kelly to take her back again—nothing more passed then—on the Saturday evening I saw her again—she came to consult me professionally on the state of her milk—I gave her advice, and she then asked me if I had heard of the coroner's inquest—I had found a summons on my table the moment before to attend the inquest—I had just come in—I showed her the summons, and told her so—she asked me if she might call the next morning to inquire the result—I told her I should be happy to give her any professional advice, but on the other part of the subject I had rather she would inquire up at Paddington.
Cross-examined. Q. You state you are a physician, do you dispense medicines? A. I do not at present—I formerly did—I did not at this period—the prisoner was very seldom in the habit of being at my house about her mistress—she knew the house well.
COURT. Q. She knew that you did not dispense medicines probably? A. No, I think she did not—I was formerly a general practitioner, and she did not know that I had changed—I have made use of my diploma lately—I have had it many years—I have put "Dr. Reid" on my door now—before it was Mr. Reid—I did that on the second of April—the plate was off on the Tuesday.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You have told us a remarkable expression, that if she was hanged she could not support her child on her wages, were those the exact words she used? A. Yes—I feel some doubt as to the precise time at which she made use of that expression—I had not told her it was a very serious affair, or any thing of that kind.
COURT. Did that expression make any serious impression on your mind? A. It certainly startled me at the time.
Q. Was the expression merely, "I will be hanged if I can support it on my wages?" A. No—I am quite certain her words were—"If I am hanged"—the expression was so inapplicable to the previous conversation, I could not understand it myself—I went on talking to her—it was not till she had named a teaspoonful of laudanum that I began to suspect.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do I understand you have no doubt the expression was used before she mentioned the teaspoonful? A. Yes, I have no doubt of that—I am quite sure it was before she said she bad sent some laudanum in a bottle—I did not ask her whether the bottle had any label or direction—she seemed rather anxious to impress upon me that there was no label on it—I had recommended her to Mr. Kelly's family—I had a good opinion of her as a wet-nurse—I knew very little of her, except having been servant in another family—I believe she was seduced.
COURT. Q. You select wet-nurses from their physical qualities? A. Unless I know any thing of their moral character—I believe the common people administer laudanum very rashly—there is a great consumption of opium among the lower classes in London—they deal very rashly with that drug—the same as ardent spirits, they give it to children, and to children of a certain age it is poison, if they give a certain quantity.
WILLIAM JONES . I am a chemist and druggist, and live at No. 34, Tavistock-place, Russell-square—I know the prisoner—I have often seen her in my shop—she came to my shop one afternoon about the middle of March—she was alone—she purchased three articles, one was laudanum, the other acidulated drops, and the third I do not recollect—she had a small square bottle of laudanum—this bottle is very similar to it—it was the same size as this—she asked if I was in the habit of selling laudanum—I said, "I do sometimes spare it, but I always require to know for whom it is, and for what purpose"—she said her mistress, Mrs. Kelly, had desired her to ask if I would spare a small quantity, a shilling bottle—I said, "I suppose I must not refuse Mrs. Kelly"—I knew Mrs. Kelly—I did not ask her for what purpose it was—she said her mistress was in the habit of taking it, being a bad sleeper—I put two labels on the bottle, "laudanum," "poison"—it was wrapped up in a piece of paper like this—( producing a bottle)—I am not certain whether it was sealed or tied—the cork was secured like this—when I gave it her I begged her to give it into the hands of Mrs. Kelly, and desire her to keep it under her own care—it was pure laudanum—I heard of the death of the child between Tuesday and Wednesday, at the end of March—I did not see the prisoner at all on Tuesday, the last day of March, nor on Wednesday, the first of April—she never stated any thing to me about any accident happening by means of the boy—I never advised her what quantity would be proper to give to any person.
Cross-examined. Q. You have not a bottle folded up as the one you gave the prisoner on the outside? A. No—a person unaccustomed to the trade cannot fold up bottles—it is not a common habit with me to seal them—I very rarely seal them—I do sometimes, certainly—sealing would confine the ends of the paper.
Q. Do you think a person not in your business could fold up a bottle in that manner, and seal the ends in the way you send it out? A. They could a bottle which had been previously wrapped up, they could wrap it up in the same paper, and seal it—it would not be so neatly folded—the labels were fastened on with gum.
FREDERICK FESTUS KELLY . I hold a situation in the Post-office, and reside in Hunter-street—the prisoner was in my service as wet-nurse.
JOHN MORTIMER BOWIE . I am a medical student. In March last, and the beginning of April, I resided in the house of Dr. Reid, as his boarder, not as an assistant—he had no pupils or assistant in the house
besides myself—I saw the prisoner at his house on Saturday—I did not see her there on the Tuesday before the Saturday—I never supplied her with any medicine at Dr. Reid's—she never complained to me of any mistake made in delivering medicine to her.
GUILTY. ( Strongly recommended to mercy by the Jury, conceiving there were some extenuating circumstances in the case, being seduced and thrown on the world.)— Judgment Respited.
NEW COURT.— Wednesday, April 15 th, 1840.
First Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18400406-1286]
1286. CHARLES DUNECLIFT was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM PEDDER . I keep a book-stall, in Carnaby-street, Golden-square. Some time ago I was indebted 1 l. 13 s. to a person named Scott, for which I received a summons—I attended the Court of Requests, at Westminster, and was ordered to pay the sum in four weeks, with 3 s. costs—I went to the Court, I think, on the 28th of November, to pay the money, and saw the prisoner in the Court, opposite the place where the money ought to be paid in, where they call people's names over—I was looking about, and the prisoner said to me, "What is your business?"—I said I had come to pay in some money which I was sued for by Mr. Scott—I had the order with me, and showed it him—he said, "It is all right, I can take your money; it will be perfectly light"—I had not known him before, nor did I employ him—I understood him that he could take my money for the Court—I firmly believe he said, he was an officer of the Court—he said he could take my money, and it would be perfectly right—he said he had not got a pen and ink there, but if I would walk with him over the way, he would take my money and give me a receipt for it—I went over the way with him, and paid him the money, because I believed him, from his own words, to be a lawful officer of the Court to receive the money—I paid it under no other impression—I paid him 1 l. 13 s. for the debt, and 8 s. for the costs—he gave me this receipt on a card—( read)—"Received the debt and costs due to Mr. Scott, of the Gas Company, 1 l. 16 s. Charles Duneclift. Nov. 28"—when he gave me this card, with this receipt on it into my hand, I said, "Now, sir, is this perfectly right?"—he said it was perfectly right, and I should never hear any more of it—on the 9th of March a distress was put into my house for the same debt, and 8 s. more costs, and I had to pay it twice over—I never taw nor heard any more of the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you go to the Court afterwards? A. No, he did not point out at all the place where the money was to be paid—I was never summoned in my life before.
CHRISTOPHER CUFF . I am the chief clerk of the Westminster Court of Requests. I am the proper person to receive money paid into Court there—there is no other person authorised to receive money—the prisoner had no authority to receive money, or to act in any way—an order was issued for William Pedder to pay the debt and costs, and it was not paid in—there are two orders up in the Court against agents receiving money.
GUILTY . Aged 38.— Confined One Year.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18400406-1287]
1287. ALBERT BAHN was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN GEORGE SMITH . I keep an eating-house in High-street, Wapping. On the 26th of August the prisoner came to lodge at my house—I never knew him before—he represented himself as a ship-chandler, that he had taken a warehouse, and said he should be a permanent lodger for many years, he hoped—he was to pay me 15 s. a week for board and lodging—he has not paid me one farthing, and he is my debtor for 75 l., as a loan—I had a Spanish certificate from a debtor named Newton, who bad been engaged in the Spanish war—I got another certificate from Mr. Clark, of the Spanish Commission, in exchange for it—the one I got from Mr. Newton was for 27 s. 6 s. 5 d.—it was a transfer similar to one I hold in my hand—I was in September last showing the document to a friend—the prisoner was at my house at the time my friend called—there was something stirring respecting this certificate—he thought there would be some interest likely to be paid—the prisoner said, "Have you got a Spanish bond, Mr. Smith?"—I said, "I have a transfer, or certificate, as they call it"—he said, "I should like to see it"—I said, "I can let you see it"—after my friend was gone I showed it to the prisoner—he said, (giving it me back again,) "If you like I will take it to my banker's to ascertain the value of it for you, as it may be useful to you at any time"—he said Messrs. Coutts in the Strand were his bankers—I told him I did not want money just then, nor want to part with it, but I said, "Whatever you do, do not take any thing on it"—I gave it to him again, and cautioned him not to make any thing on it—I would not take 19 s. 11 d. in the pound, because it would be paid some day, but he might inquire the day price on it if he liked—he took it, and said he should be going up to his banker's in the course of the day, and he would inquire—he afterwards said that if I liked, if I doubted it, he would give me cash for it—I said, "No, my own document is what I want"—he said, any time I wanted 100 l. or 1000 l. I should have it, that he had 2000 l. standing at his banker's—I was out when he came home—I did not come home till the evening, and did not see him that night—I believed him to be a respectable person—I never asked him for his board and lodging up to that time, but I have latterly—two or three days after I gave him the certificate I said, "Respecting that transfer that I gave you to ascertain the value of, you have not given it me again"—he said, "Oh dear, I did not see my banker, he was not within, but at any time you shall have it"—we were to go to his banker's, but we could not go then—he said, "If you are afraid I will give you the cash"—I said, no, I wanted my own property—he said, "I will go and inquire about it"—(I have never had it since)—I went with him to Coutts's about a month after the transaction—he said he was going up Leadenhetll-street, and asked me to take a walk with him—I went, and he went up and spoke to one of the clerks at Coutts's—I thought it was on business—that made me contented, thinking my document was safe at Coutts's—he was not apprehended on my premises—he was taken on another charge.
Prisoner. Q. Did you give me the certificate, or did your wife hand it over to me? A. I did—I know a gentleman named Miller—I have not seen him in this Court—I never offered him this bond to raise 20 l. on—I gave it you to ascertain the value, and bring it me back—I sent my wife to two of my relations to borrow money on my own account—I never intended to raise 20 l. on this bond.
COURT. Q. Did you give him this bond, or allow him to have it in, order to raise any money on it? A. No, by no means.
Prisoner. Q. Did you, in presence of your wife, request me to raise money on it, or not? A. No—I have asked you for it a score of times—I did not want 30 l. for this, and 20 l. more to send over prohibited machinery—I went with you to Calais for a week's recreation—I did not take machinery there—you did not pay my bill to get me out of a bad House there—I did not tell you not to tell my wife—I took 10 l. to Francs—you paid one days' expenses, and I the other—I swear that I never offered this Spanish certificate to any one to raise money, and never showed it to any one but you—I solemnly swear I gave you this certificate to ascertain the value of it—I never gave it you for security for 30 l.—you did not pay 15 l. 10 s. 6 d. for me—I know nothing about any machinery—I let you have 20 l. in gold, because I thought you were a respectable man, and you gave me the keys of his warehouse for security till the deeds were drawn up, with a prospect of going into partnership—I did not give a bond for security of 30 l.—I did not tell you to keep it—I never inquired the market price—I trusted you with it, and to this present moment you did not offer it back if I paid you 30 l.—you did not offer to give it me back on payment of 8 l.—a stranger called on me, and said if I would pay 8 l. the certificate should be forthcoming—no one called on me to induce me to bring this present charge.
SAMUEL CLARK . In April, 1889, I was employed as accountant in Mr. Black's office. I produce, a copy of the original document, which I got by desire of the prosecutor—it is for wages due—they are properly called Spanish certificates—I have heard them called Spanish bonds.
JOHN BLENKINSOP . I am in the service of Counts and Co., and have been so twenty-six years-storing that time we never had such a name as the prisoner's in our books—we had no account with him in April, or any time—I have examined the books for years.
Prisoner. Q. If a person presented a cheque at your house, it would not take above five minutes? A. That depends on the nature of the cheque.
GEORGE SIMMONDS . I receive an annuity from government, and live in Richmond-buildings, Soho-square. I have known the prisoner seven or eight months—I met him in the City on the 1st or 2nd of January—he said he had purchased a Spanish bond for 27 l.—that he was short of money, and should part with it again—he offered it we for a sum of money, but I do not recollect the amount—I did not purchase it.
Prisoner's Defence. I solemnly declare the bond was given to me as security for the payment of 30 l.—the prosecutor stated before the Magistrate that he was not in want of money, and be now says he tent his wife to borrow some.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Transported for Seven Years.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)

[trialAccount t18400406-1290]
1290. PATRICK ROACH was again indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 2 coats, value 6 l.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 1 l.; 1 hat, value 10 s.; and 1 handkerchief, value 6 s.; the goods of Michael Doherty.
MICHAEL DOHERTY . I live in Bell-alley. I had the articles stated, in my box, I broke the key of it a fortnight or three weeks before the 6th of April, and I gave the articles stated, in charge of the prisoner's wife, who lived in the same house—I missed the trowsers, the coats, and other things—they are now here—the pawnbroker has them.
JOSEPH JOHN GOODE . I am a a pawnbroker. I have two coats and a pair of trowsers, which I took in of the prisoner on the 6th of April, which he said were his own property—it was between four and six o'clock.
Prisoner's Defence. I pawned them; I went out that morning at six o'clock to work; the prosecutor took my wife and made her drunk; and when I came home I found him and my wife in the act of adultery. I went to the fire, took up the poker, and gave him a pretty good share of it. I went out, and when I returned I asked my wife for some money; the prosecutor said I might take some clothes, and make money, which I did.; and in the evening he got sorry about letting me have them; he asked me for the money, and the money was scattered between them.
MICHAEL DOHERTT re-examined. I did not permit him to pledge them, I did not know he had them.
GUILTY . Aged 63.— Confined Three Months.
ESSEX CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18400406-1291]
1291. ROBERT WALKER, alias Smith, and JOHN RUFFETT, alias Smith, were indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of February, 1 work-box, value 1 l., the goods of Mary Ann Parker: and 2 pillow-cases, value 5 s.; 4 yards of muslin, value 14 s.; and 1 napkin, value 1 s.; the goods of William Henry Parker.
ANN PARKER . I am the wife of William Henry Parker, and live at Plaistow with my brother, Thomas Gethin. I have a daughter named Mary Ann, who is fifteen years old. On Tuesday, the 25th of February, I delivered a work-box belonging to my daughter, and various parcels, containing linen, books, and other things, to James Wood, in. Lothbury, to take to Plaistow—they were the property of my brother—I am not living with my husband, he is in Wales—this is the box and other articles which I delivered to Wood ( looking at them.)
JAMES WOOD . I am a labouring man. Mrs. Parker delivered the work-box and other things to me, to take to Plaistow, on a Tuesday night—I cannot tell the day of the month—I saw the prisoners on the road, conversing together—I drew my cart up by the side of the road, about ten minutes' walk from the Black Lion, and asked Walker to mind my cart, which he consented to do—he and Ruffett were both in company together then, but on my return from where I went, to inquire for the house, Ruffett had disappeared—Walker was still there—I had been gone about ten minutes—I gave Walker some halfpence for taking care of my cart—I know these articles were safe when I left the cart, for in getting out I put my hand upon the box, which I had particular directions not to
get wet or spoiled—I afterwards missed the box—I did not know what the handle contained—the things were taken out of it—I had other things in the cart—nothing was packed—they all laid loose in the body of the cart.
THOMAS BARTLETT ( police-constable K 286.) On the 26th of February I was in Stratford, and saw the prisoners coming towards town—Ruffett had a bundle under his, arm, apparently in the shape of a box—I suspected and watched them—they went into Mr. Lamb's, in the Broadway, Stratford, and came out in about a quarter of an hour, without the bundle—I immediately went up to them, and asked what they had done with the box they had had in their possession—they both said they had left it at Mr. Lamb's, who had advanced 1 s. on it, and that they had found it in the New-road, going from Barking, a fortnight before—I asked if they had made inquiries about it—they said they had inquired all round Plaistow, and could find no owner for it—I took them back to Mr. Lamb's, found the box there, and took them into custody, on Walker was found a duplicate of some muslin and pillow-cases, pledged at Mr. Bressy's—I have not got the duplicate here, I gave it up after the second examination, but I have a receipt which they gave me for it—I got Walker to sign it.
Walker. We were out of the shop a quarter of an hour before we went back with you. Witness. It is false—I watched them out, and immediately went and stopped them—I bad got about two hundred yards from Mr. Lamb's shop when I stopped them—I questioned them for about five minutes, and then took them back to Mr. Lamb's.
RICHARD LAMB . I am a jeweller at Stratford. The prisoners came to my house on Wednesday, February 26th, between one and two o'clock—they brought a work-box, and on asking them where they got it from, they said they found it on the New-road, leading to Barking, that a coach was on before, and they thought the box had dropped from that—I asked how long they had had it in their possession—they said nearly a fortnight—I asked if their friends knew they had found it—they said yes, and said they had been all over Plaistow to different parties to try to find out the owner for the box, but could not—I said I should not buy it of them, I thought I knew the party it belonged to—I said if they would call on Friday night, or the Saturday, after I had made necessary inquiries, I would tell them more on the subject—when they brought the box they wanted me to purchase it—I am sure they asked me to buy it—it was tied up with string, in brown paper, very carefully as if it was packed.
Walker. Q. Did we not ask you if you knew any owner for the box, when we came? A. I do not think you did—I did not state so at Lambeth-street—they said they were cold and hungry—I gave them 1 s., thinking if I could find the owner of the box, I should not be at the loss of the 1 s.
CHARLES HENRY LAWKS . I am shopman to Mr. Bressy, pawnbroker of Stratford. I have four pieces of muslin, two pillow-cases, and a napkin, pawned by a boy, apparently seventeen or eighteen years old—on Wednesday, the 26th of February, he asked me for 1 s. 6 d. on them—I lent him 6 d.—I have no recollection of him—I firmly believe it to be Walker, but I will not be positive—Stratford is above a mile and a half or two miles from Plaistow—MR. Lamb's is about a mile and a quarter from Plaistow.
Walker's Defence. I did not take them from the cart; I had the cart in my charge.
Ruffett's Defence. I was not there.
WALKER* — GUILTY . Aged 16.— Transported for Seven Years.
RUFFETT* — GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18400406-1292]
1292. HENRY HEIDEN was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Oliver Fain, on the 31st of March, and stealing therein, 1 watch, value 3 l.; 1 watch-chain, value 6 d.; and 1 seal, value 6 d.; the goods of William Burgess.
WILLIAM BURGESS . I am foreman to Mr. Oliver Pain, of Broadway, Stratford, in the parish of West Ham. I had ft watch on the 31st of March, which I saw safe at eight o'clock at night, in the drawer in the bakehouse, which is part of the house—I came back at eleven o'clock—It was gone—I immediately informed the police, and told my suspicions—the prisoner was taken into custody on the following morning—this is my watch, chain, and seals—( looking at them)—the bakehouse-doors were shut when I left it—I shut them after me—when I returned, they were still shut—our people go in and out there.
FRANCIS HEAPHY . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody, and told him Mr. Pain wanted to speak to him—he did not ask me what it was for, but accompanied me to Mr. Pain, who told him, in my presence, that a watch had been stolen out of the bakehouse, belonging to his man, and that he, the prisoner, was strongly suspected—he said the watch was stolen between nine and eleven o'clock—the prisoner remarked, "I was in-doors at a quarter to ten o'clock"—there were foot-marks in the garden—he afterwards said that he went into the parlour, stole the watch, and put it into his boot—he pulled off his boot, and the property was found in it—he said he was sorry for it.
Prisoner. It is the first time I was in trouble; I throw myself on the mercy of the Court.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY of Larceny only. Aged 23.— Recommended to mercy.
Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Baron Alderson.
[trialAccount t18400406-1293]
1293. GEORGE HARVEY was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 1 gelding, price 6 l.; 1 set of harness, value 10 s.; 1 cart, value 4 l., and 3 pigs, value 3 l. 19 s.; the property of John Regan.
JOHN REGAN . I live at East Ham, in Essex, and deal in pigs, which 1 take about in a cart. On Monday, the 6th of April, I met the prisoner at the Duke's Head public-house, Walthamstow, about half-past twelve o'clock—he was generally about that house—I had three pigs in my cart, which had a pony to it, with harness on—I had to show some pigs to a lady, but I was taken very ill, and the prisoner said he would mind the cart, if I gave him a pint of beer—I asked him to take the horse and cart, and show the pigs to the lady—he was to have returned in a quarter of an hour, but I saw no more of him till he was in custody at Barnet—I never told him to sell the pigs, but to show them to the lady—the policeman afterwards showed me two of my pigs.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Suppose the lady took a fancy to them—the price of two was 28 s., and the other, 24 s.? A. Yes—he was to have a pint of beer when he returned.
CHARLOTTE WHITE . I live in Marsh-street, Walthamstow. About four o'clock on the 6th of April, the prisoner drove up to my door with a pony-cart and pigs—he asked if I wanted to buy a pig—I said I did—I went to the cart to look, and the one he put out he asked 25 s. for—I gave him 23 s.—I had seen him round with Regan's cart before—he said he was selling them for Regan, who was ill at the public-house.
Cross-examined. Q. Has Regan seen the one you bought? A. Yes.
ROBERT BURROWS . I bought a pig of the prisoner about half-past three o'clock—he had three at that time he asked me 30 s. for it—I offered him 29 s.—I gave him 15 s. deposit—I asked where Regan was—he said he was ill at the public-house.
Cross-examined. Q. Where is the pig now? A. At my house—I told him he should have the remainder of my money to-day, and if Regan did not like it, he might come and take the pig back—he had part of file pots of beer at ray house—he was net drunk.
JAMES M'CULLOCH . I am a policeman—in consequence of information I followed the prisoner to Tottenham, and took him at Barnes, which is about twenty miles from Walthanstow—I found the horse and cart about three-quarters of a mile from where I found the prisoner—them was another man in his company who came from Wathamstow—they were both together waiting in a field where a booth was erecting for a fair—they appeared cooking a dinner—the last pig was sold there—the prisoner told me he had sold it for 18 s.—I took him in charge and found a sovereign and a half in gold and four pence in silver on him—he said he had had a pleasant time of it and seemed to enjoy himself very well—he said he did not mind coming back as soon as he had his last dinner; that he had sold the last pig, and that was all the money he had.
Cross-examined. Q. He was going to return the horse and cart when he had dined? A. Yes—there was a dinner cooking, and, I believe, for him—he seemed half drunk.
JOHN HUNT . I am a policeman—I saw the prisoner in custody—he said, in going along, "If you had not come down we should have sold the horse and cart," meaning himself and the other man who was with him.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not the other man in the cart? A. Yes—he was not drunk—at the time he made use of this expression, we were on our road home to Walthamstow.
JOHN REGAN re-examined. The pig he sold to Charlotte White was one I valued at 28 s.—he would have to go past Burrows to go to the lady's—he was quite solid; if he had been drunk I would not have sent him—he was only to show the pigs to the lady—I told him the price, as he asked me what they were if she should ask him—he was not to sell them, only to see if they were the right size for the lady—she would not have bought of him—he was not trusted to sell them for me and receive the money—I would not have trusted him with the money—he was to bring the pigs back to me in a quarter of an hour, whether she liked them or not—I only mentioned the price as he asked me.
Cross-examined. Q. Suppose he had said the lady meant to keep two of them; would you not have been satisfied? A. I should have gone back to the lady to get the money—he was not to leave them—I did not send him to take the money—I nave never sent pigs by another person and received the money myself.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY of stealing the Pigs only. Aged 30.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18400406-1294]
1294. SARAH BURKE was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of February, 2 combs, value 5 d., the goods of Daniel Donovan; and 1 purse, value 6 d.; 12 yards of ribbon, value 2 d.; and five yards of net, value, 1 d.; the goods of Henry Hunt, her master.
FRANCES SARAH DONOVAN . I am the wife of Daniel Donovan, and lodge with Mrs. Hunt, at East Ham—the prisoner came on the 28th of February, on liking, as a servant to Mrs. Hunt—she had no business in my apartments—on the 29th of February I lost two combs off my side-board—on the 6th of March my husband took her, and she gave these two combs out of her bosom—I have no doubt they are mine.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did she attend on you? A. No—I used to shut my door at night.
ANN HUNT . I am the wife of Henry Hunt—the prisoner was in my service for two days—I lost a purse, twelve yards of ribbon, and five yards of net out of a muff-box where I had put them on the 27th of February—the box was in a room which the prisoner had to scour—I did not know the prisoner before, but I had seen her pass, and being a strong girl I thought she might suit me.
DANIEL DONOVAN ( police-constable K 319.) On the 29th of February my wife told me what she had lost, and on the 6th of March I saw the prisoner in East Ham, with my wife's side-combs in her hair—I took her to the station-house—in going along, she took the side combs from her hair and put them in her bosom—when we got there, I asked her for them—she said she had none, but took them out and gave them to me—on the next day I went with Mrs. Hunt to the prisoner's father, where I found this ribbon and net.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Three Months.

1296. SUSANNAH JANE CHEESEMAN was indicted for a misdemeanor.
WILLIAM WATTS . I am a draper, and live in Broadway, Deptford. On the 7th of February, between six and eight o'clock, the prisoner came to my shop, and requested to take some shawls to show Mrs. Collins, of King-street, Deptford—I asked if she came from Mrs. Collins—she said, "Yes," and I gave her two shawls—I think the price was marked on them—on the following day I sent Arthur Osborne to Mrs. Collins, and on his return I gave information at the police station-house—MRS. Collins had been a customer of mine for some time—I had seen the prisoner at my shop once or twice before—I gave credit to her representation that she came from Mrs. Collins, and parted with them for no other purpose than for her to show Mrs. Collins.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you make a memorandum of the conversation between yourself and her? A. I did not—I believe her
words were, "I want to take some shawls to Mrs. Collins, in King-street, Deptford"—that is as near as I can recollect—I inquired what sort of shawls she wanted, and I believe she said they were to be good ones—the two were worth from 64 s. to 65 s.—I believe this to be one—( looking at one)—they were both very nearly alike—the ticket has been taken off—one was found on the prisoner, and the other at the pawnbroker's.
ELIZABETH COLLINS . I am the wife of John Collins, a traveller, and live at No. 5, King-street, Deptford. I have known the prisoner four years—I did not send her on any message on the 29th of November last—I know Mr. Watts—I did send her there for some linen on the 25th, which was the first time she came to work in my house—I had a child dying on the 29th—I did not send her for any thing on the 7th of February—I never saw her between November last and the 7th of February—she did not bring any shawls to me—I never authorised her in any way to apply for them.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. A married woman I think—the prisoner was with me four days, from the 25th of November to the 29th—she came at ten o'clock in the morning, and left at night—she left earlier on the 29th, because my baby died that night—I did not see her again till I saw her before the Magistrate at Greenwich, when the duplicate of my watch, chain, and seals, was found on her—I was never before a Magistrate in my life—I keep a lodging-house.
Q. Is it a house of such description that young men and women come without your knowledge and sleep there? A. A gentleman may come—I do not allow strangers to sleep there—I consider it a proper moral house—I do not consider it an immoral house, unless I am taken before a Magistrate—I was brought here to prosecute the prisoner last Sessions, and gave evidence against her—perhaps I might have said on that occasion that I did not consider mine a proper moral house—I have come to state the truth—I may very likely let lodgings in this way when my husband is at home—I did not say last time that I had eleven children—I said I was the mother of eleven—I have eight now—I married a gentleman, but misfortunes may overtake you as well as me—the prisoner is a straw-bonnet and dress-maker—I knew she was a gay and unfortunate girl—she was frequently at my house, because her mother was committed to Maidstone gaol for starving a child to death, and she had nowhere to go—that was not the reason why I let her live in my house, but she had nobody to take care of her at home—I swore to the property at the last trial, and here is part of it round my neck now—I was shamefully treated last trial—I had not a vestige in me, I had not a feeling about me but what was numbed.
Q. You did not get the watch, did you? A. Oh, but she got it—I could bring forward the man that put my initials on it—I got the chain, and she got the watch—I take in married women and their husbands, and perhaps single girls and their husbands.
RICHARD PARRY . I am a pawnbroker, and live in Flagon-row, Deptford. I produce a scarf which I took in of the prisoner, on the 7th of February, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, in the name of Jane Cheeseman—I knew her before—I lent her 14 s. on it—I asked her if it was her own property, and she said, "Yes."
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know Mrs. Collins? A. I never saw her till she was at the office.
BENJAMIN LOVELL . ( police-constable K 15.) On the 8th of February I received information, in consequence of which I apprehended the prisoner,
on the 12th or 13th of February, in High-street, Woolwich, three or four miles from High-street, Deptford—I met her with another female, named Thurley—I told her I wanted her—she asked what for—she had this shawl on—MR. Watts, who was with me, laid hold of the shawl, and said, "That is one of my shawls"—I told her she had better come to Mrs. Thurley's house—we went there, and I said she must go to the station-house—she said, "Mr. Watts, I hope you will forgive me; I know I have done wrong; I intended to pay you, and will pay you now"—I asked if she had any duplicate—she said, "No"—MRS. Thurley said, "Yes, you have"—I found she had sent the duplicate to her mother's—I went there, and found the duplicate of the other shawl—she said she had sent 10 s. 6 d. to redeem the shawl—she said she met a female, who over-persuaded her to pawn the shawl, and she gave her the 14 s., as she was distressed, and she would suffer any thing sooner than tell who the female was.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you bear Mrs. Collins examined on the former occasion? A. Partly—I do not think I heard her cross-examined—she keeps a house for gentlemen and females to go to—it is a house never complained of—I should certainly call it a b——house—I have lived in the same street eleven years, and never heard a disturbance there.
WILLIAM WATTS re-examined. I believe this to be the same shawl—I went on the Monday following to the pawnbroker's to inquire about the shawls, and, among others, to Mr. Parry's, who had previously denied having any shawl pawned at his house—the person I saw at Mr. Parry's said he was perfectly certain nothing of the sort had been taken in.
RICHARD PARRY . re-examined. When Mr. Watts came to my shop I was not at home—he asked for two shawls—this is called a scarf—if he had said a scarf, they might have looked for a scarf—I took the shawl in myself.
(Harriet Loseley, wife of a carpenter in Cornwall-road, gave the prisoner a good character for honesty.)
GUILTY. Aged 19.—( Strongly recommended to mercy on account of her previous good character, and the disreputable character of Mrs. Collins.)— Judgment Respited.

[trialAccount t18400406-1298]
1298. ALFRED COLEMAN and BENJAMIN BUTTON were indicted for stealing, on the 10th of March, 2 window-guards, value 2 l., the goods of Hill Dodds.
HILL DODDS . I am landlord of the Prince Regent public-house at Deptford. On the evening of the 10th of March, from information I received, I ran down Effingham-place, and saw my boy running after the prisoner Coleman, who had part of these brass window-guards in his hand, which had been stolen from my house—they were worth 2 l.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you know where Coleman was lodging? A. I have heard since that he was lodging somewhere in Whitechapel—I understand there was another person taken up, who came to give Coleman a character—I saw that person, but I did not hear him give an address to the Magistrate—he represented that he lived in High
street, Greenwich—from the inquiries that have been made, and the result of them, I really believe that Button had nothing to do with this transaction.
BENJAMIN LOVELL ( police-sergeant R 15.) On the 10th of March, about nine o'clock at night, I saw the two prisoners looking in at the shop window of Mr. M'Lallan, a jeweller in Broadway, Deptford—I am certain they are the persons—I had never seen either of them before—they left there, and went down High-street, and just before ten o'clock they were both brought to the station-house.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you before the Magistrate? A. I was—I am not aware that the Magistrate wished to discharge Button—he wished to admit him to bail, but he did not express any reason in my presence—to the best of my judgment it was about five minutes before nine o'clock when I saw them at the shop-window in the Broadway—I will not swear that it was not ten minutes before nine o'clock, it was not a quarter before—a person came down two days running to give Coleman a character, and I took him into custody—he gave an address, which was a false one—I went and found he lived in Lanbeth-street, Whitechapel—no other part of the brass has been discovered.
JURY. Q. Was there any thing in their appearance when you saw them at the shop-window, which makes you know them again? A. Nothing particular, but I bad a reason for noticing them.
COURT. Q. You have seen the person who came to give a character to Coleman, are you able to say whether he was or was not the man who was with Coleman looking through the window? A. No, he was shorter than Coleman—I will swear the two prisoners are the two who were looking in at the shop-window.
JOHN FARQUHARSON . ( police-sergeant R 2.) On that Tuesday night, at a quarter past nine o'clock, I was going down High-street, marching the night relief along, and saw the two prisoners—one of them was at a public-house door, and the other talking to two females—as they were strangers to me, I took a sharp sight of them as I was going by—I had never seen them in that quarter before.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you made any inquiry about these men? A. No—I was present when the Magistrate expressed a wish to discharge Button on bail—there was a third person taken by Lovell, but I was not there—I cannot tell who preferred the charge against him—I thought I had seen Button once before, and asked him whether be recollected seeing me about Greenwich—he said he thought he had.
JOHN EVANS ( police-constable R 190.) I produce a piece of brass guard, which I got from Tanner—I took Coleman into custody—when I got about five yards from the corner, Tanner pointed out Button, and I took him—it was then about twenty minutes past nine o'clock, as near as I can guess—Button denied all knowledge of the charge—he said he had been to see a friend, and then to take a walk, and had no knowledge of Coleman.
Cross-examined. Q. Was any other brass found but what you got from Tanner? A. No—nothing was found on Button—I did not see Caroline Thompson that night—I never heard from her, or from any body, that she said she could not speak to Button—she was produced on the morning after the robbery—I never made a mistake about the identity of a person in my life.
CAROLINE THOMPSON . I am the daughter of Jam's Thompson, who lives in Pleasant-row. On the night of the 10th of March I went for a pot of beer to Mr. Dodds's, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening—I saw the two prisoners and two young women talking together—I am sure the prisoners are the two men—I then went into Mr. Dodds's to get the beer, and there was a person there I knew—I stood there a good bit, as they were serving some more persons—I went to the door to see if a person was coming home to our house, and saw Coleman standing by the window, looking in—I kept my back to the counter, and saw him unhook the brass guards, and run down Effingham-place with them—I am sure Button was the other person who was standing with Coleman and the girls when I went in.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you go to the station-house? A. I told Elizabeth, the bar-maid, and Mr. Dodds's pot-boy came for me the next morning—I had never seen Button before—I did not say that I was not sure of him, nor that I could not be sure about him—I never said that, to my knowledge—I never said that I had a doubt about it—I did not know Lovell before this—he has not talked to me about this before I came here.
Q. Did you never talk to him at all about Button since you have been here? A. I cannot swear to that—I do not know that he said any thing to me about Button—he might, or he might not—I do not think he asked me if Button was the man—I did not say to Lovell, or to any one, that I was not certain Button was the man—I am quite sure he is the man, because I saw him.
JOSEPH TANNER . I am servant to Mr. Dodds. Caroline Thompson gave an alarm, and I heard a cracking, like the breaking of tobacco-pipes—I ran out and saw the two prisoners breaking up our brasses—I am quite certain of their persons—I seized Coleman, and got this brass from him—he dragged me down Effingham-place, and threw me over the wagon-shaft—my master came up, and he was taken—Button stopped in Effingham place, and came back—Coleman spoke to Button, and then Button got into the crowd—as they were taking Coleman to the station-house, I looked round and saw Button.
Cross-examined. Q. When you were before the Magistrate did you say a single word about Button coming back to Coleman, and speaking to him? A. Yes—I do not know whether it was taken down—I did not hear it read over—my master was present when the prisoners spoke to one another—no brass was found on Button—he said he knew nothing about it—Coleman said he would punch my head if I did not let him go.
JURY. Q. When you went out and caught Coleman, was there light enough for you to see the other prisoner? A. Yes; it was gas-light.
MR. CLARKSON called
MARY ANN ALLEN . I am sixteen years old. Button's father is a shoemaker, and lives in London-street, Greenwich—Button has been lately working for his father—my mother does shoe-binding, and lives in the next street to Button—on the night on which Button was taken he came to our house, about ten minutes past eight o'clock, with a pair of shoes which he had been repairing for my brother—I asked him to stay, and he staid till between half-past eight and twenty minutes to nine—I
had a cousin there and a little baby—the children were in the house, and Button was nursing one of them.
RABITHA BALS . I live next door to Mrs. Allen. On the night that Button was taken I saw him coming out of Mrs. Allen's house—I think it wanted twenty minutes to nine—I wished him good night, and he said, "Good night, Mrs. Balls"—he is subject to fits, and I said to him, "Benjamin, if I were you I would take a bit of a run"—he said he thought he would—he was then alone.
MARY COLLINS . I live at Deptford. On the 10th of March I had been to London to inquire for a situation—I was returning home, and I met Button near Mr. Peart's, Broadway, Deptford, about five or ten minutes past nine o'clock—he was alone—I am sure it was as late as five or ten minutes past nine o'clock.
COLEMAN — GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Three Months.
BUTTON — NOT GUILTY .

CHARLOTTE ROBERTS . I am the wife of John Roberts—we keep a china-shop in the Broadway, Deptford. On the 8th of April we lost these plates from the step of the door—I did not see who took them, but the policeman brought them back, and I then missed them.
Prisoner. Q. Can you swear to these plates? A. Yes—we know our maker's make—I thought there was about a dozen, but there are thirteen.
DANIEL REDDEN . I keep a lodging-house at Deptford—the prisoner brought these plates to me, and I told him to take them away, because he said he had pulled them—he did not ask me to buy them.
