1302. EDWARD DADSWELL was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 35 sovereigns, and 10 half-sovereigns, the monies of James Moore, his master, in his dwelling-house.
JAMES MOORE . I keep the Goldsmiths' Arms public-house, Southwark Bridge-road. The prisoner came into my service on the 2nd of March, as pot-boy—on the 28th he was employed to clean the bar—I keep my cashbox in the cupboard in the room, and he saw it that day—I went out in the afternoon for a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes, and when I returned, the cash-box was on the table, where I had not left it—I replaced it in the cupboard—in the evening I went to it, and missed this gold—the key had been on the mantel-piece, in a chimney ornament, but I found it in the box—the prisoner left the house almost directly after I returned, and absconded—he had seen where the key was that day—I gave notice to the police—I missed about 40 l.—I had counted the gold the night before, and had seventy-one sovereigns besides 15 l. in notes—I paid none away—I found thirty-one left.
MARY MOORE . I am the prosecutor's wife. On Saturday, the 28th of March, I had occasion to go to the cash-box while the prisoner was cleaning the window—I took a half-sovereign out, and locked the box again, and placed the key in the chimney-ornament, on the mantel-piece, put the box in the cupboard, and locked the door, but left the key in the lock—I went out, and returned in about three-quarters of an hour—the prisoner turned round and looked at me at the time I put the key away—I observed that—he left an old jacket in his bed-room when he went away—he was taken on Sunday morning—he lodged at our house.
BARTHOLOMEW KING ( police-constable R 176.) On Monday evening, the 30th of March, the prisoner was brought to the station-house by a policeman, from Woolwich—he was locked in a cell—he knocked at the wicket of the cell-door, about ten minutes after I had been to him, and asked me if he could have any thing to eat—I said, "Yes, if you have any money"—he gave me a half-sovereign—I told the inspector, and he said, "Ask him if he has any more"—he said no—I went to him—he said the inspector of Woolwich had given him the half-sovereign to find him in provisions while he was remanded.
SARAH MOORE . I am the prosecutor's daughter. On the 28th of March I was left in charge of the bar while they went out—nobody had access to the bar-parlour except the prisoner, while I had charge of it—he was in the bar-parlour while I went out—he could get from there into the bar—I did not notice him touch any thing.
DANIEL RIERDON . I am a policeman. I conveyed the prisoner from the Magistrate's office to the station-house after his examination—he asked me if I knew what the Magistrate was going to do with him—I told him I did not—he asked if they were going to commit him for trial at Maidstone—he said he did not wish they would, as his poor mother lived at
Maid stone—he said, "My master has sworn I stole forty sovereigns—he has sworn false, for I took hut fifteen—it struck roe all in a moment, the key was in the box at the time, and if more has been taken out of the box, it was taken by the servant or somebody else"—I said nothing to induce e him to say this.
EDWARD PALMER . I am a policeman. On Sunday, the 29th of March, I apprehended the prisoner at Woolwich, and found 4 l. in gold, 6 s. in silver, and 3 1/2 d. in copper on him—he had new clothes on, and a silver watch—he wanted me to go to his brother—I asked, while I was searching him, if that was all—he said, "I have a parcel at my brother's"—I went there—they gave me a parcel—I said, "I want the money as well"—the sister opened a box and gave me a purse containing 3 l. in gold, and some silver—he told me he had paid 5 l. for his clothes—I got about 7 l. 7 s. altogether—I did not give him the half-sovereign—I stripped him at the station-house, and found none.
GUILTY .† Aged 16.— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Baron Alderson.
[trialAccount t18400406-1303]
1303. ELLIS COWARD was indicted for killing and slaying Charlotte Small.
MARY ANN CURRY . The child, Charlotte Small, was by a former husband—the prisoner is landlord of my room. On Monday, the 2nd of March, I came home, and found the room-door locked—it was fastened outside, and something put into the key-hole—the prisoner was on the stairs—he said, "MRS. Curry, have you any money for me, if you have not, I am b—if you go into your room again—I have fastened the door up"—I went into the room through my mother's room, which is the next to mine, on the ground-floor, and there is a door from it into my room—the prisoner went out into the street, and got in through the window, attacked me, and pulled me down, and tried to get me out of the room, between the two doors—he wanted to push me through the middle door to get me out of the room—he jammed my arm between the door—my child came and pulled hold of my petticoats, and said, "You will kill my mother"—he took the child by the throat, and dashed her against the grate—her head fell against the fire-place, and her back over the fender—she was very much injured—she was quite well before this, but was very ill the same night, like a burning coal, and complained of pain in her stomach all the evening—so she continued till the following Monday—I got her some medicine in the course of the week, and on Monday she brought up blood—I took her to Mr. Buck the doctor—she vomited blood again on Wednesday, and I took her again to Mr. Buck, and on the Thursday I took her to the Dispensary in Waterloo-road—she had advice there three days, and she died on the Saturday.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Are you married? A. Yes—my husband is here—he was at Hounslow at that time—two children were present, Mary Ann Small and Charlotte Small, when this happened—the medical man who attended her first is not here—it was not desired that he should be here—the child has never had a day's illness except head-ache—two years previously it fell out of the window, but nothing to hurt—it was taken to the hospital, but it was playing the next day—it fell on its hands and knees on the stones—I was struggling with the prisoner,
I owed him 10 s. 9 d. rent—my face was to the door, and when I turned to the fire-place, the child was standing up all in a tremble.
COURT. Q. Did you see any part of the child strike the grate? A. No—I saw him push it—I do not know whether it struck the grate or the fender.
EDWARD EVANS . I examined the body of the child after death—I never saw it alive—the chest and abdomen were very healthy, except a small portion of the lungs adhering to the chest—that would not cause death at all—I found the blood vessels of the head very much coagulated, and a quantity of water in the cavities of the brain, which was the cause of death—many causes would produce that effect, such as a blow on the head, or a fall on the head, and dentition with young children might cause it, or when the stomach and bowels are not in good order—it had all the teeth a child of that age would have—they were all quite cut, and had been so, I dare say, for a year—dentition could not produce the effects I saw in that child—a blow on the head, or disease of the stomach, might produce it—there was no symptom of disease of the stomach at all—the child appeared to have enjoyed very excellent health—if it was thrown with violence by the throat, and the head went against the fender or stove, concussion of the brain would be the consequence, and that, jointly with the terror of the child, would be sufficient to produce death—if the child was quite well before, I should judge that that violence was the cause of it.
Cross-examined. Q. Would excessive terror effect this alone? A. I believe it would—terror at seeing the mother ill used might cause unusual sensation in the brain which would bring on reaction, and the same appearances would ensue—I should say that trembling violently was one of the sufficient causes to produce the effect—I must have observed the stomach to be out of order if that had been the case—I saw nothing in the state of the stomach which should cause the child to complain of pain in it—if the head is bad for two or three weeks, the stomach generally becomes weak, and sickness would ensue from compression of the brain—that would account for violent pain in the stomach—all the symptoms I perceived could be accounted for by terror and anxiety—I saw no external marks of violence, but it must have got well in three weeks;—the parents did not point out any place where I could discover a mark—they pointed out a scar on the left part of the throat, which had evidently not been long there—it might have been five weeks, I cannot say—I have no doubt the symptoms about the brain and head arose from a recent cause—it could not have arisen from a blow six months previous—no doubt a previous accident would produce a predisposition for such a cause, if the brain was then affected, but if the functions were all right it would not.
COURT. Q. If the child fell out of window, and was playing about next morning? A. I should say that injury was of no consequence what-ever and would not produce predisposition—all the symptoms I perceived might be produced by fright, but it is most probable that the head fell against some solid substance, and caused concussion of the brain.
MARY ANN SMALL . I am going on for ten years old—I saw my mother struggling with the prisoner, and saw my sister Charlotte come up to her—the prisoner took her by the throat, and threw her right against the fire-place—the back of her head touched the stove, and her back was on the fender—I am sure of that.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you been talking a great deal to your mother about this? A. No—she did not tell me my sister's head fell against the fender—I saw it—I was standing against my sister—my mother had her face towards the door, and her back to the fire-place—my sister took hold of my mother's gown behind—the prisoner was shoving my mother—he did not get her through the door—she turned round and saw my sister shaking—she jumped up directly she was thrown there.
MR. BALLANTINE to MR. CURRY. Q. When did you first make any complaint about this? A. I could not get any money till the following Saturday, and then I took a warrant out—I made a, charge against the prisoner for assaulting me and also for assaulting the child—I did not get a warrant out for the assault of the child, but I did for the assault of myself.
MR. BALLANTINE called
HENRY COWARD . I am the prisoner's son, and am thirteen years old. On Monday, the 2nd of March, when Mrs. Curry came home my father asked her for her rent, and said she should not go into the room before she gave him some rent—she went to go in and broke open a middle door, and jumped in—my father jumped in at the window, and said she should not take any thing out before he had some rent—I did not see him do any thing to her or lay hold of her.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 44.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18400406-1304]
1304. JOSEPH LEWIS was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying John Brook Davies.
WILLIAM DWYER . I live in Taunton-place, Brunswick-street—the prisoner is a bricklayer and builder. On Saturday, the 22nd of February, I heard a cry out that a man had fallen into a hole—I went and found the deceased had fallen down a hole, and somebody with him—I asked the parties who were down with him if I could render any assistance—they said, "Not at present"—I went to doors to get my hat, and when I returned the policeman had come up and got him out of the hole—he wan sitting by the side of the hole—the policeman took him in a cab to St. Thomas's Hospital—I believe the prisoner was building in that spot.
JOSEPH BUTLER . I knew the deceased—his name was John Brook Davies—he was my clerk—he, was fifty-five years old—I went to see the hole he fell into on the following day—it was, left quite open—it was in the foot-path—there was nothing to protect people from falling in.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You say there was no protection on the 23rd? A. On the Sunday—I had not seen it before.
WILLIAM PAUL . I was building some houses adjoining when this happened. On the night in question there was no protection put to this hole—there had been a pole put up for two months at my three houses, a short distance from where this occurred, and my cellars had been turned over about nine days—I am certain the pole was there till then, but afterwards I am not sure pf it—this pole would prevent people from coming to this place, as they must pass my place to it—there was a pole at the end of this place at the time the accident occurred—while my pole was up nobody could have passed along there—I thought the pole was up at the time, and if it had been up a person could not have come on my ground and therefore could not get to this ground—it was frosty weather, and the
men had not been to work that Saturday—the prisoner was superintending, hit building himself—my men had not been to work on Friday and Saturday—I saw the prisoner at his premises on the Saturday—I did not observe then whether the pole was up or down—it having been there so long I considered it was up—when I heard of the accident I said, "How could the man have got there? he must have got over a quantity of bricks or over the pole," as I thought it was still up—I know a pole bad been put up by the prisoner.
JOSEPH PAUL . I was within twenty-five yards of the hole the same evening—I heard a cry of "Police," went up, and saw Mr. Davies sitting on the mould—I did not see whether the pole was up or down.
EDMUND M'DONALD . I am a policeman. I saw the hole which I took the deceased from the side of—there was no pole there that day—I live in the same street—I am certain there was no pole there when I was called—I had not taken notice before that day—I frequently saw the place—I never noticed whether there was a pole or not—one was put up on the Monday following to prevent accidents.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18400406-1305]
1305. THOMAS WALKER was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of March, 3 shirts, value 6 s.; 1 flannel-shirt, value 2 s.; 1 pair of drawers, value 2 s.; 2 handkerchiefs, value 4 s.; and 1 stocking, value 9 d.; the goods of Charles Civil.
CHARLES CIVILL . I belong to the ship Felicity, which was lying in Gould's Dock—the prisoner, who was mate, was paid off in February—before he left he went with me, to take the articles stated in this indictment to be washed—soon after, he left the ship, and when I went for my clothes they were gone—from what the washerwoman told me, I suspected him, and about six weeks after I saw him on board a vessel, as we were going down the river—he told me he had got some clothes of mine—I said I knew he had, and collared him—he went down below, and pulled my clothes out of his box, all but one stocking, which he could not find—I gave him in charge—these are my clothes—I never gave him authority to get them from the washerwoman—they were clean when he got them, and he has worn them.
JOSHUA JUDGE . I am a Thames police officer. I took the prisoner—I asked him how he got the clothes—he said he got them from the washer-woman, and he had done wrong, but was drunk at the time—he said the prosecutor did not allow him to get them.
Prisoner's Defence. I went with him, and left some things of my own to be washed, and when I went for them the washerwoman asked if I would take his things, as we both belonged to one vessel. I took them. I got tipsy, and the vessel he was on board of left the dock that night. I then went on board another vessel, and when I returned I saw the prosecutor going by in a boat; I hailed him to come and fetch his things. I said I had worn them, as I was short of linen; but I would make him any recompence; he would not take less than half-a-sovereign; that was more than they were worth, and be gave charge of me.
NOT GUILTY .
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18400406-1306]
1306. HENRY DRIVER was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of March, 1 jacket, value 10 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 5 s.; 1 pair of shoes, value 3 s.; 4 stockings, value 2 s.; 2 handkerchiefs, value 1 s.; 1 knife, value 6 d.; 2 shillings, 1 sixpence, and 5 d. in copper money, the property of Robert Samuel Pearson.
ROBERT SAMUEL PEARSON . I am a rafter in the Commercial Dock, and live with my mother, in Russell-street, Rotherhithe. The prisoner came to lodge there on the evening of the 2nd of March—he slept with me that night—I got up at half-past six o'clock next morning, and went to work, leaving him in bed—I did not miss my clothes till that evening, when I went to put them on—they had been hanging up in the room where we slept—these are my jacket, trowsers, and shoes—( looking at them.)
CHARLES HENRY FALCONER . I am an officer. The prisoner was given into custody to me—I found on him two duplicates, which led to where these articles were.
JAMES WALTER . I am a pawnbroker, and live in Thornton-street, Rotherhithe. This jacket and trowsers I took in from the prisoner on the 3rd of March.
THOMAS BAKER . I am a pawnbroker, and live in Free-school-street. I took in a pair of shoes of the prisoner, on the 5th of March.
Prisoner's Defence. I got up at seven o'clock and missed my money out of my pocket; I did not know what I should do, and took his things to the pawn shop (not knowing the English law) to get my money back again.
GUILTY . Aged 36.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18400406-1307]
1307. MARY SEARSTON was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, 1 coat, value 3 l.; 1 pair of breeches, value 1 l.; 1 waistcoat, value 9 s.; 2 frocks, value 6 s.; 4 yards of linen, value 6 s.; and 1 shawl, value 2 s.; the goods of Eli Webb, her master; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
ELI WEBB . I keep a beer-house at Clapham. The prisoner was in my service for six months—she left on the 10th of March without notice—I went up stain, and missed a coat, waistcoat, and the other articles which are here, and are my property.
HENRY DENMAN . I am in the service of Mr. Folkard, a pawnbroker at Vauxhall. These articles were pledged by the prisoner at different times previous to the 19th of March.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF ( police-constable L 31.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Lawson's office, the clerk of the peace for Surrey—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Transported for Seven Years.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

1308. WILLIAM CASEMORE was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of March, 4lbs. weight of flour, value 9 d., the goods of William Parsons, his master; to which be pleaded
GUILTY . Aged 39.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18400406-1309]
1309. WILLIAM HENRY LONG was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of March, 1 sack, value 2 s.; and 4 bushels of tares, value 30 s.; the goods of Francis Nash and another, in a barge in the navigable river Thames: and GEORGE JOHN VANDERVORDE , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MESSRS. CLARKSON AND PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM DURMME . I am fifteen years old. I am in the employ of William Betts—her son employed me on board her barges—she has one called the William Henry —Long went on board that barge on Wednesday, the 11th of March—he did not say any thing to me—it was lying in the road opposite Mr. Nash's, at Pickle Herring-wharf—some clover and straw which belonged to Mr. Vandervorde was in the barge—Long came on board shortly after me, and took her up to the Giraffe steamer—about three o'clock in the afternoon we got to the Giraffe, and took in thirty-nine quarters and four bushels of tares—we had finished the lading about half-past six—I did not see any thing of Vandervorde at that time, but soon after I saw him come on board to Long—he said to Long, "If I come and help you, don't you think we could get her up there, for the horses have not a bit of straw to lie on"—Long said, "Yes, if you come and help me I think we can"—the straw was to be delivered at St. Mary Overy's dock—I was behind the barge in the boat—Long brought the barge to Pickle Herring-wharf—(this was on the Wednesday night)—I went to another part, leaving them both on board, and they took her up to St. Mary Overy's dock, and left her there to unload the straw—that is on the same side of the river as Pickle Herring—that was about half-past seven o'clock—I came away in the boat—I saw Long again next day on the barge, about eleven o'clock in the morning, and the straw was then out of her—I said, "Why don't you bring her off?"—he said, "Because she is aground"—(there was plenty of water then to have brought her to Mr. Nash's wharf)—he then said he was not going to the wharf, he had to go over the water to fetch some seed—he took her over at flood in the afternoon—about ten o'clock that night I was on Pickle Herring-stairs, and I saw Long in the barge—I said, "Why don't you bring her in?"—he said, "You can see there is such a tide on her head, I can't get her head in; I must let her lie alongside of the roads"—when I found he would not bring her in, I got a man in a boat to give me a cast-off to the barge—Long was then bringing her round to Vandervorde's barge—he then went on board of Vandervorde's barge, and took the chain with him, made her fast to one of the timber-heads of the stern of Vandervorde'e vessel—Long then went on shore, and left me on board the barge all night—the next morning, about seven o'clock, Long came again on board, and he said to me, "Jump up, and let us haul the barge a-head, I ought to have been here before"—we hauled a-head, and laid alongside of Vandervorde's barge, and Long told me to hold on, which I did with my bands—there was a man in a smock-frock on board Vandervorde's barge—Long said to him, "Come and give us a heave up with, this," which was a sack of tares—the man came and lifted it on to Long's back—he carried it off the William Henry barge, and put it on to Vandervorde's vessel, close to the fore hatchway—Long then came back to the William Henry —when Long hauled her round he went across the other vessels, and went on board Vandervorde's vessel again—I then saw Vandervorde and him quite close together, and they appeared to me to be talking—this was after he put the sack on board—I did not hear what passed between them—Long came on board the William Henry, and took her to Mr. Nash's wharf—he got there about a quarter to eight o'clock—he then went away
—he said he was going in the lug to get some trefoil seed—he said, "If William comes, and asks where the barge was last night, tell him she was here; I don't want to hare any bother with him, and when I come back I will give you a penny; but if he don't say any thing to you, don't you tell him"—he went and left me in care of the barge, and went home to breakfast.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did you get your penny? A. No, I did not care about it—I do care about a penny, but when I was in the barge I did not think of it—I have never got into any trouble—I was never in prison, or threatened to be tent there—I do not recollect Sunday the 29th of March—I was never charged with having rope of my master's—I know Joe Williams—his name is not Taffy—I know nothing about Taffy—Williams is no friend of mine—sometimes I am in the barge with him—I sometimes walk with him—I was not with him last Sunday—I am with him some Sunday a on the barge—I do not remember any day when he was in a scrape, but I beard about it—I was never talked to about it by my mistress—I have been in her service about three months—before that I worked at Mr. Dennis's in the rope grounds for about three months—I left because his house was burned down—I went on Wednesday for these tares—I got them on board from the Giraffe steamer about six in the afternoon—I left after the tares were on board, when she got to St. Mary Overy's dock—I then went away in the boat and left Long on board with the there—I came about eleven o'clock the next day—the tares were all safe then, as far as I know—I was on board the barge all the Thursday night, and Long went away—Long came on Friday morning and this took place—he told me he ought to have been there before, because he was rather behind—he got forty one bags of trefoil seed in the lug boat—I did not fasten the boat with the hitcher—I held on with my hands—I could not have forgotten it if I had.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. You were before the Justice? A. Yes—this is my mark on, this paper—( looking at his deposition)—I am fifteen years old in about five months—I have never said I was going on for fourteen, not that I am aware of, I might have made a mistake saying I was fourteen—I may have said I was going on for fourteen years—I some-times make mistakes, in telling my age—if ever I said I held on to the barge with a hitcher that may have been a mistake, because I held on with my hands—I never said I held on with the hitcher—it was about ten o'clock on Thursday night, as far as I know, when I got to Vandervorde's barge—I could not see well across the river—it was not raining—it was a nice mild light night, but the moon was not shining—Vandervorde's barge was lying outside the tier—it was the very outside one—people on shore, who wanted to get to his barge, must have crossed the others—there are no covings to his barge at all—the deck and the barge sides are all even—there is no irregularity, no rise at all, only at the head and stern—she laid with her head up and her stern down—timber heads are what you make craft fast to—there are covings to the hatchway—they could make an unevenness on the deck—I saw Vandervorde on Friday morning—when he was speaking to Long he was dressed in a brown coat and black trowsers—he seemed to be up and dressed for the day—there was no smock-frock, or any thing of that kind—there was a man in a smock-frock that helped Long to lift up, but Vandervorde was not on deck then—the sack was deposited by the fore-hatchway—Vandervorde's cabin is aft—Vandervorde was not going for any seed that day that I know of.
MR. PAYNE. Q. When you were in the barge was any thing raised above the deck, or was the deck even? A. Yes, there were no sides—it was a flat deck—a person could go along the deck to the fore-hatchway.
WILLIAM HENRY HILDITCH . I am a corn-meter—I was delivering tares on board the Giraffe steamer. On Wednesday, the 11th of March, Long came alongside with a lighter—I emptied thirty-nine quarters and four bushels of tares into it—I gave him a certificate, which the officer has—this is it—( looking at it)—I am correct in saying it was thirty-nine quarters and four bushels.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did you mete it yourself? A. Yes—the fellowship porters put them into the lighter—I was down in the hold—I cannot swear that that quantity was delivered, but I measured them—I saw them emptied, but I did not see them leave the vessel—they were taken to the deck of the Giraffe —there were no more tares on board, nor any thing in the shape of grain—I have had great experience—I never had one mistake—it very often happens that a lighterman will try to defraud a meter—mistakes have occurred among lightermen in the tallying.
JURY. Q. Did you see that number of quarters taken out? A. Yes I swear that quantity went from the hold of the deck of the vessel in sacks.
JOHN SCOTT . I am foreman to Mr. Nash—I was at the wharf on the Friday morning—I assisted in unloading and receiving the tares in the warehouse—there were thirty-nine quarters and no more—I called it over to my master.
Cross-examined by MR. BALANTINE. Q. Who brought them to you? A. The porters we had on the wharf—they are not here.
JURY. Q. Could any one have taken a sack away? A. Not after it was in our possession on the wharf.
FRANCIS NASH . I have a partner of the name of Hicks—we are wharfingers at Pickle Herring lower wharf—MRS. Betts does our lightering work—Long was employed by her occasionally. On Wednesday, the 11th of March, I ordered some tares to be lightered by her—on the Friday morning I saw them landed at my wharf between ten and eleven o'clock—when the last sack was landed I spoke to Mr. Scott and examined the tares, and found one sack deficient—I said to Mr. Betts, in Long's presence, "There is a sack of tares short, how is this, William?"—he turned to Long, and said, "How is this, Bill?"—I saw he wanted to say something to him in an under tone, and I turned away—I did not hear what passed—I looked round almost immediately, and observed Long shoving off in a lug boat towards the road where Vandervorde's barge was lying—I got into a skiff and desired William Betts to row me under the stern of the road, and observed Long in the act of having a sack of something hove on his back—I saw him carry it from abaft the rigging of the barge, and throw it off his back—by this time I was on board Vandervorde's barge—I went to the fore part and asked Long what he had done with that sack of tares—he said, "I have it here, it is all right;" and I saw the sack, which I supposed was the tares, lying in the lug boat, which he had shoved off—he had hove the sack over the head of Vandervorde's barge into the lug boat, and he shoved the lug boat from the road to the William Henry, and with the assistance of a man on board, he got it to the head-sheets of the William Henry —I then went to the cabin hatchway of Vandervorde's
barge—I called to Vandervorde—he came up—I said "How is this, Vandervorde, that you had a sack of oar tares on board your barge?"—he said, "I know nothing at all about it, I have not seen the sack"—I said I should see about it—I believe the sack was mine—I saw it in the lug boat, and it was the only sack in the boat that was full, and I saw it was one of mine—I went on shore and spoke to my partner—when I got on the wharf Long said to me, "I thought I had got a sack of tares over, and I did not want to get the meter into any scrape or trouble"—I said it was a parcel of nonsense (in the course of business Long would have the meter's ticket describing the quantity)—I went in a skiff to the Thames police-office—as I was passing under the stern of Vandervorde's vessel, I saw him on board—he called to me, and said, "Mr. Nash, I hope you don't think I know any thing about the sack of tares—I said, "I don't know any thing about it, I am going to the Thames Police, and I shall leave them to sift the matter"—he said, "I hope you won't go any further in the matter, you can't think that I know any thing about them"—I went on, and met Mr. Evans, the police-inspector, in his boat—we went back to the wharf, and found that Long had left—he had instruction to go and work something else—MR. Evans then went, on board Vandervorde's, then came and told me something, I afterwards went to the corn-market, and saw Vandervorde there—I said, "Mr. Evans, the inspector, has been looking for you, and he will be here at two o'clock—Vandervorde said, "Very well, I will see him," or "I will be here at the time"—I sent my own sacks for the tares, and the sack produced is one of mine—the tares that were in it on board Vandervorde's vessel are the same kind as those I had—I have a sample from them and from the bulk—I cannot identify them, but I believe them to be the same.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. After you had been in communication with the Thames Police, Long was gone about business of yours? A. Yes—he did not run away—I am not aware that he had any bags of trefoil that morning—he has worked for us, and has occasionally had property to a large amount—for thirty-nine quarters of tares there would be seventy-eight sacks, and for thirty-nine quarters four bushels there would be seventy-nine sacks—Long was employed for us to work some other tares at that very time—we had not bought these tares—we are wharfingers, and take them for parties—some person had given us the order to lighter and land them—the ticket he would receive would be a cheek on the quantity be would produce.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. What did you see on the day of this transaction? A. I saw Long push off from my wharf in the lugboat with bundles of empty sacks that he was going to work the other tares with—I had come up to Vandervorde's barge in time to see him take up the sack which contained my property, and put it into the boat—I was astern—I went on shore, and desired the man not to touch the sack—the police officers brought it away from the head-sheets of the barge—I did not see it put on board of Vandervorde's barge—I saw it brought from it, and put on the head-sheets of the barge lying alongside my wharf, by Long——I was on board nearly at the time the man turned it off his back, and at that time I spoke to Vandervorde, who was in the cabin—this was between ten and eleven o'clock—I think Vandervorde had his coat on—I am not certain, I did not take particular notice—I understood he was shaving,
but he had no lather on his face—I celled him by name, and he came up from the cabin, and told me he bad never seen the sack, and knew nothing about it.
WILLIAM HENRY BETTS . I and my mother keep these barges. I was at Nash's-wharf on the Friday morning when these tares were delivering—there were about ten quarters in the barge when I came down at the end of the delivery—Nash said to me, in the presence of Long, "William, there is a sack short, there ought to be thirty-nine quarters and a half"—I asked Long how it was—he said, "I have got the sack, it is all right, I will go and fetch it"—he jumped into a lug-boat, went ahead of the road, and Mr. Nash told me to row him off in a skiff, which I did—we rowed round the stern of Vandefvorde's vessel, and Long went ahead—I observed him go and take the sack of tares from abaft the mast, and Mr. Nash walked forward into the sailing barge—I saw him go up to Vandervorde—the sack was put into the lug-boat—I heard Vandervorde say, at I was going to the police-office, be hoped Mr. Nash would not go any further with it.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Long has been in your service a great many years? A. Yes, and conducted himself Well.
JOHN ROBERT THORP . I am a corn-meter, and live in Red cross-square, Tower-street—I was on board Vandervorde's vessel on Friday morning, the 13th of March, I had to master some corn there—I got, there about seven o'clock in the morning—I saw Long come on board about half-past seven—Vandervorde and I were speaking together relative to the corn that I was about to deliver—Long came and said, "I have got the sack of tares," or "brought the sack," or "a sack of tares"—I saw nothing particular in his manner—Vandervorde replied, but I did not hear what—Long did not remain on board more than a few seconds—about nine o'clock in the morning I saw Vandervorde on boarde Long's barge, which was lying alongside Vandervorde's vessel—he was in the act of up-ending a sack of tares which was on Long's barge—Vandervorde said to me, "Brother Fellowship, tend me a hand," meaning me to assist him in getting the sack on board his own barge from Long's—I was on board Vandervorde's barge from the lime of Long's coming, and saying he had brought a sack—I did not see any sack removed from Vandervorde's barge to Long's—I was measuring corn below, and a sack of tares could have been removed without my seeing, it—a man assisted Vandervorde with a kind of pulley, and I assisted in getting it on board Vandervorde's barge—I hooked it on—there was a small rent in the bottom of the sack—I can swear to the sack—soon after the sack was hoisted on board Vandervorde's vessel, I heard him call for a sack, and one of his men replied to it—the man asked him what sack—he said, "Any one of ours"—then one of his own was brought to him—he held it up, and I said it was as bad as the one they were going to shoot it out of—they were shot out of one sack into the other—Vandervorde was standing by the side, assisting in holding up the sack—the sack they were in before had the name of Nash on it—about an hour after Long came on board, and inquired for the sack of tares—he said, "Where is that sack of tares? it is wrong; for God's sake let me have them, or there will be such a piece-of-work about it"—I do not know to whom he said it—Vandervorde was there—I did not hear him ask for any thing—that sack could not be found at first, it was afterwards found and brought—I did not see the re-shooting of them—I went down soon after—I heard Mr. Nash on board.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. At what time did Long come for this sack of tares again? A. I should think it was from half-past nine to ten o'clock—I saw Nash come on board—Long had been there a few moments before him, and had been gone from my sight some minutes—it was possible to go on one side of the vessel, and I not see him—Long had left with the tares—I do not know what boat brought him to the side—I spoke to Mr. Nash—I said, "I can assure you they have not been down in the hold"—I was told to hold my tongue, and I said no more—I knew that Mr. Nash complained of being robbed of his tares—he would not allow me to tell him what I have said to-day—he went away—the men who were with me told me to hold my tongue—I did not tell Nash what I knew, because he went down to the Thames Police—he said he would go there—I did not go there, my business detained me on board till four o'clock, and I could not leave.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You were not examined at the police-office? A. No—I first made this statement to Evans the officer, on the Monday—the examination was on Friday—I have known Vandervorde twenty years—I do not know how many hands he had on board his barge, I should think not more than three—one name is George—when Long came the second time one man was with me, and the others on board the barge.
MR. PAYNE. Q. You heard the other sack called for? A. Yes, and then went on working.
WILLIAM CALLOWAY . I am a Fellowship porter and fisherman, living in Pitt's-place, Bankside. On Friday morning, the 13th of March, I was on board Vandervorde's vessel with the meter—I saw a sack of tares hoisted on board the vessel from Long's barge—I lent a hand to get it on—after it had been hoisted on to his vessel I heard Vandervorde ask for his own sack to shoot them in, because the one they were in was a very old one—he said, "Throw up one of our sacks"—the tares were shot into his sack, and the empty sack I threw into Mr. Long's barge—while they were being shot he said, "These tares are very dear, they come to a great deal of money, and there are as many small peas among them as tares"—I said that sack was almost as bad as the other, I could see daylight through it—I went down below.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Is this the first time you have made your appearance? A. Yes—Vandervorde's business is carrying things for any body.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Do you know the difference between peas or white tares? A. I cannot say I do—they were two different colours.
JAMES CHRISTOPHER EVANS . I am inspector of the Thames Police. On Friday morning, the 13th of March, I was spoken to by Nash—I went to Horsleydown, and found Long there—I said, "Mr. Nash charges you with stealing a sack of tares out of his barge, and putting it on board Vandervorde's vessel"—he said, "What a fuss they are making about nothing; I will tell you how it was; I went off to Vandervorde's barge with him; I took in thirty-nine and a half quarters out of the Giraffe steamer; I thought Mr. Hilditch had given me one over, and I put it on board Mr. Vandervorde's barge"—I said, "Does Vandervorde allow you to take things to his vessel?"—he said "No, Vandervorde knew nothing about it"—I said, "This boy says one of his men assisted you"—he said, "There was a man, but I don't know whether he was one of Vandervorde's men"—I then
went and found the sack in the head-sheets of the barge—I took possession of it—this is it—( producing it)—I then went, and saw Vandervorde at the corn-market—I said to him, "Mr. Nash says he has had a sack of tares stolen out of his barge, and they were found on board your barge"—he said, "I know nothing about them, I did not know they were there"—I said, "Have you seen Long this morning?"—he said "No"—I said, "Then you have had no conversation with him"—he said, "No, none whatever"—I said, "Did you see the sack of tares on board your vessel?"—he said, "I did not, if I had I should have thought Mr. Nash sent them by me, to Southend, to my brother."
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. When did you go after Long? A. I think about twelve o'clock—I found him in a lug-boat at Horseley-down.
(The prisoner Long received a good character.)
LONG— GUILTY. Aged 34.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor. — Confined Three Months.
VANDERVORDE — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18400406-1311]
1311. JOHN FITZGERALD was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 1 hearth-rug, value 10 s.; the goods of William John Dowding and another, his masters.
WILLIAM JOHN DOWDING . I am a hearth-rug manufacturer, and live in Peter-street, Southwark—I have one partner—the prisoner was in our service. On Sunday, the 4th of April, about seven o'clock in the evening, I was going into my own house, and observed a hearth-rug on the grating before the window, where it had no business to be—I pulled the bell to go in, and saw the hearth-rug slide off the grating, and go down underneath into my premises again—I went in, and met the prisoner at the top of the cellar-steps—I went to where I had seen the hearth-rag fall to—I saw nothing there—I came up again—I went down with a light, and found the hearth-rug under the cellar-steps, where it had no business to be.
WILLIAM TAYLOR ( police-sergeant M 14.) I know Mr. Jeremys the magistrate's hand-writing—this is his signature to this examination—( looking at it)— read —"The prisoner says I admit taking the rug from the warehouse into the cellar, and putting it on the grating to take away—it is my first offence."
GUILTY .— Confined Three Months.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, MAY 11 TH, 1840.
